Kodaira-type classification of singular fibers of some minimal abelian fibrations
Yoon-Joo Kim

Abstract
. Let X ⟶ S X\longrightarrow S be a minimal abelian fibration of relative dimension n n over a curve. We classify all possible singular fibers X s X_{s} having ( n − 1 ) (n-1) -dimensional “abelian variety parts”. This generalizes Kodaira’s work on elliptic fibrations, and Matsushita and Hwang–Oguiso’s work on Lagrangian fibrations into a single framework. The classification is divided into three parts: semistable, unstable, and multiple. Multiple fibers are again divided into three types: semistable-like, mixed, and unstable-like.



1.
Introduction

Kodaira classified all possible fibers
X_{s}
arising in a minimal elliptic surface
f:X\longrightarrow S
. Besides smooth elliptic curves (
\mathrm{I}_{0}
), one encounters nodal rational curves (
\mathrm{I}_{1}
) and cycles of
r\geq 2
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s (
\mathrm{I}_{r}
), called
semistable fibers
. There are more fibers of types
\mathrm{II}
,
\mathrm{III}
,
\mathrm{IV}
,
\mathrm{I}_{r\geq 0}^{*}
,
\mathrm{II}^{*}
,
\mathrm{III}^{*}
, and
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, called
unstable fibers
. These are non-semistable but still manageable as they have a smooth point. Finally, nowhere-smooth fibers are called
multiple fibers
. They are necessarily of the form
X_{s}=m\cdot X_{s,\red}
and its reduction
X_{s,\red}
is a semistable fiber of type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
as above. Kodaira’s classification played an important role in his study of compact complex surfaces
[
Kod60
,
Kod63
]
and was later recovered in an algebraic setting by Néron
[
N“’64
]
. See
[
Bar+04
, §V.7]
for nice pictorial descriptions and simplified proof.

A higher-dimensional analog of a minimal elliptic fibration is a
minimal abelian fibration
, a flat projective degeneration
f:X\longrightarrow S
of
n
-dimensional abelian varieties over a smooth curve
S
that is relatively minimal in the sense of the minimal model program. A natural question is whether singular fibers of such fibrations admit a classification. In general this problem is intractable, and much of the existing literature instead focuses on their semistable reduction behavior via allowing ramified base change. This perspective is important in the compactification problems of the moduli stack
A_{n,d}
of polarized abelian varieties.

Note however that Kodaira’s original intent was to understand the total space
X
(surface in his case). In other words, if one’s goal is to understand the total space
X
, it is preferable to analyze the fiber
X_{s}
directly. Such attempts first arose in the study of Calabi–Yau varieties, but to our knowledge, the most successful results were obtained for algebraic symplectic varieties. In relative dimension
2
, Matsushita
[
Mat01
]
classified codimension
1
singular fibers of Lagrangian fibrations on smooth symplectic fourfolds. Hwang–Oguiso
[
HO09
,
HO11
,
HO16
]
subsequently extended this to higher-dimensional Lagrangian fibrations. Remarkably, their results closely resemble Kodaira’s classification, suggesting that a classification of singular fibers for higher-dimensional minimal abelian fibrations may be possible under suitable assumptions.

It is later observed in
[
Kim24
]
(see also forthcoming work
[
CKS26
]
) that every Lagrangian fibration satisfies a condition called
\delta
-regularity
. Roughly speaking, this condition implies that every codimension
1
fiber admits an
abelian variety part
of dimension
\geq n-1
. In this paper, we develop a general framework for
minimal and
\delta
-regular
abelian fibrations, and show that their singular fibers admit a complete classification, unifying the results of Kodaira, Matsushita, and Hwang–Oguiso. The following is the main theorem of this article.

1.0.1
.

Let
S
be a Dedekind scheme of characteristic
0
with algebraically closed residue fields and
f:X\longrightarrow S
a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration of relative dimension
n\geq 2
. Write
X_{s}=f^{-1}(s)
for a closed fiber. Then

(1)

(Semistable:
Theorem

5.1.1
)
Every semistable fiber
X_{s}
is isomorphic to one of the following.

(
\mathrm{I}_{0}
)

An abelian variety of dimension
n
.

(
\mathrm{I}_{1}
)

A
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
E\longrightarrow A
over an abelian variety of dimension
n-1
with two sections
0
and
\infty
identified.

(
\mathrm{I}_{r}
)

A cycle of
r\geq 2
isomorphic copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
E_{i}\longrightarrow A
for
i=1,\cdots,r
, whose sections
0_{i}
and
\infty_{i}
are successively identified by
\infty_{i}=0_{i+1}
.

(2)

(Unstable:
Theorem

5.2.1
)
Every unstable fiber
X_{s}
is isomorphic to
(C\times A)/G
, where
C
is an unstable curve in Kodaira’s classification (type
\mathrm{II}
,
\cdots
,
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
) and
G
is a finite abelian group classified in
Table

3
.

(3)

(Multiple:
Theorem

7.2.1
,
7.2.2
,
7.3.1
,
7.3.2
, and
7.4.1
)
Every multiple fiber
X_{s}
is of the form
X_{s}=m\cdot\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
, where
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
is isomorphic to one of the following.

(
\mathrm{I}_{0},\,\mathrm{I}^{1}_{1}
)

Type
\mathrm{I}_{0}
and
\mathrm{I}_{1}=\mathrm{I}_{1}^{1}
fibers described in (1).

(
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{k}
)

A cycle of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
E_{i}\longrightarrow A
for
i=1,\cdots,R\,(\geq 2)
similar to type
\mathrm{I}_{R}
in (1), but
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
are isomorphic when
i\equiv j\mod k
for
k\mid R
.

(
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{+}
)

A
\mathbb{Z}/d
-quotient of an abelian variety of dimension
n
by a free automorphism of order
d=2
,
3
,
4
, or
6
.

(
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{\pm}
)

A
\mathbb{Z}/2
-quotient of a type
\mathrm{I}_{2R}^{m/2}
or
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{m/2}
fiber with
2\mid m\mid 2R\ (R\geq 1)
by a free involution sending
E_{i}
to
E_{-i}
or
E_{1-i}
, or its additional
G
-quotient for
G=\mathbb{Z}/2
,
\mathbb{Z}/4
, or
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
.

(
\mathrm{II}
,
\cdots
)

Any unstable fiber in (2) except
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{*}
and its quotients for
R\geq 1
.

(
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{*}
)

Similar to type
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{*}
in (2) with
m\mid R
, but the
R+1
non-reduced
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
E_{i}
’s
(0\leq i\leq R)
are isomorphic only when
i\equiv\pm j\mod m
. Additionally, its
G
-quotient for
G=\mathbb{Z}/2
,
\mathbb{Z}/4
, or
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
.

(
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-a,
\cdots
)

Six additional cases stated in
Table

7
.

Moreover, possible multiplicities
m
in each case are classified in
Table

2
.

The readers can find the relevant definitions in §
2
(
Definition

2.2.1
for
\delta
-regularity and
2.3.5
for semistability). Some details and subtleties are omitted in
Theorem

1.0.1
for the brevity of exposition. The more precise versions are referred to the body of the article. The interested readers can draw the pictures of the above possible singular fibers as in
[
Mat01
, Figures 1–3]
.

, see

and

.

1.0.2
.

In
Theorem

1.0.1
, the
Kodaira type
of the central fiber
X_{s}
in each case is as follows.

(1)

The
Kodaira type
of a semistable fiber
X_{s}
is
\mathrm{I}_{r}
for
r\geq 0
.

(2)

The
Kodaira type
of an unstable fiber
X_{s}
is denoted by (Kodaira type of
C
)
/
(order of
G
). See
Table

1
for all possible unstable Kodaira types.

(3)

The
Kodaira type
of a multiple fiber
X_{s}
is
m
(the multiplicity) multiplied by the indicated type in
Theorem

1.0.1
(3). We say that type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}^{k}_{R}
has a
semistable-like
stability, type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}_{R}^{\pm}
has a
mixed
stability, and type
m\cdot\mathrm{II}
,
\cdots
, together with the additional cases have an
unstable-like
stability.

Our study of minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibrations is intimately related to the study of abstract Néron models of abelian varieties. Consequently,
Theorem

1.0.1
yields some new results about Néron models of abelian varieties. Their
1
-dimensional versions are well-known since Kodaira and Néron. We highlight the items (2a) and (3), which reveal new higher-dimensional properties of Néron models.

1.0.3
Theorem

4.1.5
and
5.3.2
.

Let
P\longrightarrow S
be the Néron model of an abelian variety over its function field, and write
P_{s}
for its closed fiber.

(1)

If the linear part of
P_{s}
is
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, then the Néron component group
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
is a cyclic group.

(2)

If the linear part of
P_{s}
is
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, then the following holds.

(a)

The Chevalley exact sequence of its neutral component splits:
P^{\circ}_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A
for an abelian variety
A
of dimension
n-1
.

(b)

The Néron component group
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
is either a cyclic group of order
\leq 4
or
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
(see
Table

4
).

(3)

Let
\check{P}\longrightarrow S
be the Néron model of the dual abelian variety over the function field. If the linear part of
P_{s}
has dimension
\leq 1
, then
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
and
\pi_{0}(\check{P}_{s})
are isomorphic.

Compared to the results of Matsushita and Hwang–Oguiso, our result has an advantage that it applies to wider classes of abelian fibrations and has more complete descriptions (even for Lagrangian fibrations). Our work, however, was highly inspired by theirs. We also note that
Theorem

1.0.1
does not fully recover the results in
[
HO11
, Theorem 1.1]
: in the paper, the symplectic form puts more constraints to
f
and eliminates many cases in
Theorem

1.0.1
(3). Symplectic forms play no role in this article.

1.1.
Strategy of the proof

Here is an overview of our strategy and the structure of this article. First, §
2
is devoted to some reviews of various different objects arising in the study of abelian fibrations: minimal abelian fibrations
f:X\longrightarrow S
, Néron models
P\longrightarrow S
of abelian schemes (t-automorphism scheme), etc.

Our proof proceeds in two big stages: non-multiple and multiple fibers. When
X_{s}
is non-multiple, the core of the proof boils down to an intersection number computation, similar to the work of Kodaira and Hwang–Oguiso. To realize this idea, however, we will first need to understand the geometry of each irreducible component
E_{i}
of
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}E_{i}
. Roughly speaking,
E_{i}
tends to be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over an
(n-1)
-dimensional abelian variety with some minor exceptions. This will be studied more precisely in §
3
. In the next §
4
, we address two new phenomena in higher-dimensional abelian fibrations that did not arise in elliptic fibrations: we call these a
shear
and
tangle
. Again roughly, they measure the failure of
X_{s}
being isomorphic to the product
C\times A
of a Kodaira singular curve
C
and an abelian variety
A
of dimension
n-1
(shear in the semistable case and tangle in the unstable case). Along the way, we prove the splitting of the t-automorphism group
P_{s}^{\circ}
in the unstable case (
Theorem

4.1.5
) and introduce a technique that
untangles
the unstable fibrations (
Proposition

4.2.4
). The main ingredient here is the result of Conrad and Laurent–Schröer
[
Con17
,
LS24
]
about the existence of Albanese morphisms. In §
5
, we collect the results in §§
3
–
4
to imitate Kodaira’s intersection number computation and classify non-multiple fibers
X_{s}
. As a consequence, the Néron component group
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
is classified as well.

It remains to classify multiple fibers. The intersection number computation is insufficient to capture the more intricate geometry of multiple fibers. To overcome this, we will start utilizing ramified coverings
T\longrightarrow S
and a new minimal abelian fibration
g:Y\longrightarrow T
obtained by the base change of
f
. This is done in §
6
. To study how
f
and
g
are related, we first recall the relation between their t-automorphism schemes
P
and
Q
following Edixhoven
[
Edi92
]
. A byproduct of these discussions is an alternative interpretation of the classification of unstable fibers through their semistable base change (
Proposition

6.2.1
).

In §
7
, we classify multiple fibers. Let us only highlight the two starting points. The first observation well-known: it is easier to study
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
than
X_{s}
. More precisely, there exists a preferred base change
g:Y\longrightarrow T
of degree
m
inducing an étale
\mu_{m}
-covering
Y_{t}\longrightarrow\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
(
7.1.4
). This reduces the study of
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
to that of a non-multiple fiber
Y_{t}
with a free
\mu_{m}
-action. The second is to divide the proof by the type of
P
and
Q
(
Lemma

7.1.5
). When
P
and
Q
are both semiabelian, we show that
Y_{t}
is semistable and its
\mu_{m}
-action cyclically permutes its components. When
Q
is semiabelian but
P
is not,
Y_{t}
is still semistable but the
\mu_{m}
-action reverses the orientation of its components. When
P
and
Q
are both non-semiabelian, we need to take a further semistable base change
h:Z\longrightarrow U
and study the interactions of three fibrations
X
,
Y
, and
Z
. All these cases behave slightly differently, so we need to analyze them one-by-one. In §
8
, we give examples of all types of fibers stated in
Theorem

1.0.1
. §
A
collects some facts in scheme and algebraic group theory that are used in this article.

Finally, let us briefly explain why unstable-like multiple fibers admit additional possibilities (in fact, type
\mathrm{I}^{-}_{R}
is a variation of
\mathrm{I}^{+}_{R}
in the same fashion). There are two technical reasons: the first is a non-splitting of a certain Galois group (
Lemma

7.3.20
and
7.4.9
). The second is a discrepancy between the degree of the semistable base change in
Proposition

6.2.1
and the order of the automorphism group of a certain elliptic curve
E
(
Lemma

7.4.11
).

2.
Models and delta-regularity

Throughout this article,
S
is always a connected Dedekind scheme whose residue field
k
is algebraically closed of characteristic
0
.

2.0.1
.

An
abelian fibration
over
S
is a flat projective morphism
f:X\longrightarrow S
whose generic fiber is a torsor under an abelian variety.

Fix a closed point
s\in S
and denote its complement by

S_{0}=S\setminus\{s\}.

We will use the subscript
-_{0}
to denote the base change
-\times_{S}S_{0}
. For simplicity, we assume that every abelian fibration in this article is smooth over
S_{0}
and has one possible singular fiber over
s
. The restriction
f_{0}:X_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is an
abelian torsor
, a smooth projective morphism whose fibers are torsors under abelian varieties. Equivalently, we can say that
X_{0}
is a torsor under a (unique) abelian scheme

(2.0.2)

P_{0}=\Aut^{\circ}_{f_{0}}\longrightarrow S_{0}.

Let us review some definitions and basic properties in this section.

2.1.
Minimal models and t-automorphism schemes

Following
[
Kol25a
, (16)]
, we drop the
\mathbb{Q}
-factoriality axiom in the definition of minimal models.

2.1.1
.

An abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
is
minimal
(resp.
klt-minimal
) if
X
has terminal singularities (resp. log terminal singularities) and
K_{X}
is numerically
f
-trivial.

By the theorem of Néron–Raynaud
[
N“’64
,
Ray66
]
, every abelian scheme
P_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
admits a unique
Néron model

P\longrightarrow S
, a quasi-projective, smooth, and commutative group scheme over
S
whose restriction over
S_{0}
is isomorphic to
P_{0}
. For the definition of Néron model, see
[
BLR90
]
. Its central fiber
P_{s}
is a possibly disconnected commutative algebraic group over
k
, sitting in a split exact sequence (
Lemma

A.1.2
)

0\longrightarrow P^{\circ}_{s}\longrightarrow P_{s}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(P_{s})\longrightarrow 0,

where
P^{\circ}_{s}
is its
neutral component
and
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
is its
(Néron) component group
. By
[
SGA $3˙I$
,
\mathrm{IV_{B}}
Theorem 3.10]
,
P
admits a unique open subgroup scheme
P^{\circ}\subset P
whose central fiber
P^{\circ}\times_{S}\{s\}
is the neutral component
P^{\circ}_{s}
of
P_{s}
as above. We call
P^{\circ}
the
neutral component
of
P
.

2.1.2
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a klt-minimal abelian fibration. The
translation-automorphism group scheme
(
t-automorphism scheme
for short) of
f
is the Néron model
P\longrightarrow S
of the abelian scheme
P_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
in (
2.0.2
).

2.1.3
.

We remark a slight notational discrepancy with
[
Kol25
, Definition 24]
: a translation-automorphism sheaf
Aut^{\circ}_{X/S}
in loc. cit. corresponds to our
P^{\circ}
, which is the neutral component of the translation-automorphism scheme in our terminology. In other words, our notion of the translation-automorphism
P
contains more data of
\pi_{0}(P)
. When
X
is only klt-minimal, this additional datum may not be useful because the
P^{\circ}
-action in
Theorem

2.1.5
below may not be extendable to
P
. However, when
X
is minimal and
\delta
-regular,
P
will act on
X
by
Proposition

2.2.2
. Our definition follows
[
Kim24
, Definition 4.1]
.

We start with the following special cases of two general theorems.

2.1.4
[
Kol25a
, Theorem 16.5]
.

Every abelian fibration has a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial minimal model.

2.1.5
[
Kol25a
, Theorem 47]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a klt-minimal abelian fibration and
P\longrightarrow S
its t-automorphism scheme. Then there exists a unique
P^{\circ}
-action on
X
over
S
extending the
P_{0}
-torsor structure on
X_{0}
.

2.1.6
.

The
P^{\circ}
-action in
Theorem

2.1.5
satisfies the following.

(1)

The
P^{\circ}
-action on
X
is faithful.

(2)

The
P^{\circ}
-action on
X^{\prime}=X\setminus\Sing(X_{s})
is free.

(3)

The
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action on
\Sing(X_{s})
has nontrivial linear stabilizer groups.

Proof.

(1) Consider the relative automorphism scheme
\Aut_{f}\longrightarrow S
of
f
. Let
M\subset\Aut_{f}
be its
main component
, which is a smooth, commutative, and closed subgroup scheme over
S
(see
[
Kim24
, Definition 2.36, Proposition 2.42]
).
Theorem

2.1.5
constructs a group scheme homomorphism
P^{\circ}\longrightarrow M\subset\Aut_{f}
. Since
M
is smooth over
S
, the Néron mapping property constructs a homomorphism
M\longrightarrow P
. The composition

P^{\circ}\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow P

must be the open immersion
P^{\circ}\subset P
again by the Néron mapping property, so this shows
P^{\circ}\longrightarrow M
is an open immersion.

(2) There is nothing to prove when
X^{\prime}_{s}=\varnothing
, so let us assume that
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
is surjective. Since the statement is local on
S
, we may shrink
S
étale locally and fix a section
S\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
. Consider the orbit map
P^{\circ}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}\subset X
of this section. Similar as above, the Néron mapping property constructs a morphism
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow P
, whose composition
P^{\circ}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}\longrightarrow P
must be the open immersion
P^{\circ}\subset P
. This forces
P^{\circ}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
to be an open immersion, meaning that the action is free along the section. Vary the section to conclude
P^{\circ}
acts on
X^{\prime}
freely.

(3) Since the
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action on
X_{s}
is faithful by (1), every stabilizer is linear by
Lemma

A.1.5
. To prove the remaining claim, assume that
x_{s}\in X_{s}
is a closed point with trivial stabilizer. Take a quasi-finite flat morphism
T\longrightarrow S
with a section
x:T\longrightarrow X
through
x_{s}
(e.g., take a general complete intersection of
f
-ample divisors or use
[
SGA $3˙I$
,
\mathrm{VI_{B}}
Lemma 5.6.1]
). The section defines an orbit map
P_{T}\longrightarrow X_{T}
by
a\longmapsto a\cdot x
, which is an open immersion because there are no stabilizers along the section
x
. In particular, the central fiber
X_{s}
is regular at
x_{s}
.
∎

2.1.7
.

The quasi-projectiveness of
P
,
Proposition

2.1.6
, and
[
AF24
, Theorem 1.2]
make the
P^{\circ}
-action on
X
a
weak abelian fibration
. Though important in the study of
Rf_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
, we will not discuss this topic in this article.

2.2.
Delta-regularity

The notion of
\delta
-regularity can be defined over arbitrary bases following
[
Ng“ˆo10
, (7.1.5)]
, but we restrict ourselves to Dedekind schemes. Given a smooth commutative group scheme
P\longrightarrow S
over a Dedekind scheme
S
, its fiber
P_{s}
admits a
Chevalley exact sequence

[
Bri15
, Theorem 4.2]

0\longrightarrow L_{s}\longrightarrow P_{s}\longrightarrow\tilde{A}_{s}\longrightarrow 0,\quad\mbox{or}\quad 0\longrightarrow L_{s}\longrightarrow P^{\circ}_{s}\longrightarrow A_{s}\longrightarrow 0,

where
L_{s}
is a connected linear algebraic group,
\tilde{A}_{s}
is a proper algebraic group, and
A_{s}=\tilde{A}_{s}^{\circ}
is an abelian variety. We call
L_{s}
the
linear part
and
A_{s}
the
abelian variety part
of
P_{s}
.

2.2.1
.

(1)

A smooth commutative group scheme
P\longrightarrow S
is
\delta
-regular
if its generic fiber is an abelian variety, and its closed fiber
P_{s}
has at most
1
-dimensional linear part
L_{s}
.

(2)

A klt-minimal abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
is
\delta
-regular
if its t-automorphism scheme
P\longrightarrow S
is
\delta
-regular.

In other words, the
\delta
-regularity condition says either
P_{s}
is an abelian variety (in which case
L_{s}=0
), or
L_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
or
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. The main interest of this article is an abelian fibration that is both minimal and
\delta
-regular. In this case,
Theorem

2.1.5
has the following improvement. The rest of this subsection proves this.

2.2.2
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration and
P\longrightarrow S
its t-automorphism scheme. Then

(1)

There exists a unique faithful
P
-action on
X
extending the
P_{0}
-torsor structure on
X_{0}
. Its restriction to
P^{\circ}
is
Theorem

2.1.5
.

(2)

If
X_{s}
has a regular point, then
X^{\prime}=X\setminus\Sing(X_{s})
is a
P
-torsor.

2.2.3
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a klt-minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration and
P
its t-automorphism scheme. Then

(1)

Every
P^{\circ}_{s}
-orbit in
X_{s}
has dimension
\geq n-1
.

(2)

Every
P^{\circ}_{s}
-orbit in
X_{s}
of dimension
n-1
is an abelian torsor.

Proof.

Since the linear part of
P^{\circ}_{s}
has dimension
\leq 1
, every point
x\in X
has a stabilizer with dimension
\leq 1
by
Proposition

2.1.6
. If an orbit has dimension
n-1
, then it is isomorphic to the quotient of
P^{\circ}_{s}
by the linear stabilizer, which is an abelian variety by the Chevalley sequence.
∎

2.2.4
.

(1)

Every minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
has a regular total space
X
.

(2)

Every birational map between two minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibrations uniquely extends to an isomorphism.

Proof.

(1) The singular locus
\Sing(X)
is over
s
and has dimension
\leq n-2
, since a terminal scheme is regular in codimension
2
. Since
\Sing(X)
is invariant under
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action and every orbit has dimension
\geq n-1
by
Lemma

2.2.3
, we have
\Sing(X)=\varnothing
.

(2) Let
X
and
Y
be two minimal
\delta
-regular fibrations and
\phi:X\dashrightarrow Y
a birational map over
S
. Then
\phi
is
P^{\circ}
-equivariant and hence its undefined locus is closed under the
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action. Since
X
and
Y
are minimal,
\phi
is an isomorphism in codimension
1
points
[
KM98
, Corollary 3.54]
. Hence the undefined locus
Z\subset X_{s}
of
\phi
(resp. undefined locus
W\subset Y_{s}
of
\phi^{-1}
) has dimension
\leq n-1
and is a disjoint union of abelian torsors by
Lemma

2.2.3
. Moreover,
\phi
induces an isomorphism
X\setminus Z\longrightarrow Y\setminus W
. Take the graph
\Gamma\subset X\times_{S}Y
of the birational map
\phi
. Then for every
x\in Z
, the fiber
\Gamma_{x}=\Gamma\cap\pr_{1}^{-1}(x)\subset Y_{s}
is contained in
W
, an abelian torsor of dimension
n-1
. But
\Gamma_{x}
is positive dimensional and rationally chain connected
[
Kol96
, Theorem VI.1.2]

[
HM07
, Corollary 1.5]
, producing rational curves in
W
. Abelian torsors contain no rational curves, so this shows
Z=\varnothing
. Thus
\phi
is a morphism, or an isomorphism since
\phi^{-1}
is a morphism, too.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.2.2
.

(1) The following strategy is taken from
[
Kim24
, §5]
. Take the relative automorphism scheme and its main component
M\subset\Aut_{f}
as in
Proposition

2.1.6
. The claim is equivalent to saying that
M
is a Néron model. Thanks to
[
BLR90
, Theorem 7.1.1]
, it is enough to show that it is a
weak Néron model
in the sense of
[
BLR90
, Definition 3.5.1 and Proposition 3.5.6]
(or
[
Kim24
, §3.6]
). Shrinking
S
étale locally, we need to show that every rational map
u:S\dashrightarrow M
defined on
S_{0}
is regular. Consider
u
as a birational automorphism
\phi_{u}:X\dashrightarrow X
over
S
.
Proposition

2.2.4
shows that
\phi_{u}
is necessarily an automorphism, so
u
uniquely extends to a section
S\longrightarrow M
and shows the weak Néron mapping property of
M
. Hence
M
is the Néron model of
P_{0}
, and it is isomorphic to
P
.

(2) Note that
X^{\prime}=X\setminus\Sing(X_{s})
is a Néron model of
X_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
: this is a consequence of
[
BLR90
, §1.3]
(see
[
Kim24
, Example 3.13]
for a summary) or its generalization in
[
Kol25a
, Corollary 2]
. When
X_{s}
has a regular point,
X^{\prime}
is a
P
-torsor by
[
BLR90
, Corollary 6.5.3]
or
[
Kim24
, Proposition 3.21]
.
∎

2.3.
Multiplicity and semistability

The central fiber
X_{s}
is an effective Cartier divisor

(2.3.1)

X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}E_{i}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad E_{i}:\mbox{prime divisor and }a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

The prime Weil divisors
E_{i}
will be considered as integral subschemes of
X
.

2.3.2
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be an abelian fibration from a normal scheme
X
and
X_{s}
its central fiber.

(1)

The
multiplicity
of the component
E_{i}\subset X_{s}
is the positive integer
a_{i}
in (
2.3.1
). We say
E_{i}
is a
reduced component
of
X_{s}
if
a_{i}=1
and
non-reduced component
of
X_{s}
if
a_{i}>1
.

(2)

The
multiplicity
of
X_{s}
is the positive integer

m=\gcd\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{r}\}\ \in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

2.3.3
[
Cam05
, Remark 1.3]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be an abelian fibration from a regular scheme
X
. If
X_{s}
has multiplicity
1
, then it has a reduced component.

Proof.

The following copies Campana’s argument but using techniques in
[
Kol25
, §§25–26]
. We may assume
S=\Spec R
and
S_{0}=\Spec K
are the Spec of a strictly henselian dvr and its function field. Write
z
for a uniformizing parameter of
R
and
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}E_{i}
. For each
i
, take a point
x_{i}\in E_{i}
such that it is a regular point of
(X_{s})_{\red}
. Then the point
x_{i}\in X_{s}
extends to a
multisection

x_{i}:S\longrightarrow X
of
f
such that the composition
f\circ x_{i}:S\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow S
is
z\longmapsto z^{a_{i}}
.

Recall that
X_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is a
P_{0}
-torsor. Set
X^{(0)}_{0}=P_{0}
and
X^{(m)}_{0}=X_{0}\times_{p_{m}}S_{0}
for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\neq 0}
where
p_{m}:S_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is a base change
z\longmapsto z^{m}
. (Equivalently, if
X_{0}
is a
P_{0}
-torsor defined by
\alpha\in H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(S_{0},P_{0})
, then
X^{(m)}_{0}
is a torsor defined by
m\alpha
.) Their disjoint union

\mathfrak{X}_{0}\coloneq\bigsqcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}X^{(m)}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}

form a commutative group scheme. Use the assumption
\gcd\{a_{i}\}=1
to take
b_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\sum_{i}a_{i}b_{i}=1
. The multisection
x_{i}
defines a section of
X_{0}^{(a_{i})}\subset\mathfrak{X}_{0}
, so we may take take their sum using the commutative group structure:

x=\sum_{i=1}^{r}b_{i}\cdot x_{i}:S_{0}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{0}.

This section lands into
X^{(1)}_{0}=X_{0}
, because the degree of the multisection
x
is
\sum_{i}a_{i}b_{i}=1
. The valuative criterion extends it to a section
x:S\longrightarrow X
. Such a section necessarily factors through
X^{\prime}=X\setminus\Sing(X_{s})
by the regularity of
X

[
BLR90
, Proposition 3.1.2]
and hence
X^{\prime}_{s}\neq\varnothing
. This means
X_{s}
has a reduced component.
∎

To talk about semistability, recall that
\delta
-regularity implies one of the three possibilities: (1)
P_{s}
has a linear part
0
, i.e.,
P_{s}
is an abelian variety, (2)
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, or (3) it has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. The first case is easy to analyze. In fact, we can immediately classify
X_{s}
in this case.

2.3.4
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a klt-minimal abelian fibration. If
P_{s}
is an abelian variety, then
X_{s}=m\cdot X_{s,\red}
for an abelian torsor
X_{s,\red}
of dimension
n
and
m\geq 1
.

Proof.

The
P_{s}
-action on
X_{s,\red}
can have only finite stabilizers by
Proposition

2.1.6
(2–3). Since
X_{s,\red}
is proper connected, the only possibility is
X_{s,\red}\cong P_{s}/K
for a finite subgroup
K\subset P_{s}
. Hence
X_{s,\red}
is an abelian torsor.
∎

2.3.5
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration and
m
the multiplicity of its central fiber
X_{s}
. Then
f
(or
X_{s}
) is called

•

semistable
(resp.
strictly semistable
) if
m=1
, and
P_{s}
has a linear part either
0
or
\mathbb{G}_{m}
(resp. only
\mathbb{G}_{m}
);

•

unstable
if
m=1
and
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
; and

•

multiple
if
m>1
.

2.3.6
.

We deviate from the usual convention and divide the non-semistable fibers into two categories: unstable and multiple. In other words,
unstable fibers are always assumed to be non-multiple
in this article. To justify, we will see in §
7
that a multiple fiber can behave similar to semistable fibers, unstable fibers, or even a mixture of them.

We also note that the multiplicity of the central fiber
X_{s}
is
not
a birational invariant of
f
. See the following simple example. For this reason, we call the central fiber
X_{s}
multiple (or non-multiple) only when the abelian fibration is
minimal

\delta
-regular, which is unique in its birational class by
Proposition

2.2.4
.

2.3.7
.

Take a minimal elliptic surface
f:X\longrightarrow S
with a Kodaira type
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
central fiber

X_{s}=(E_{1}+2F_{1})+(E_{2}+2F_{2})+(E_{3}+2F_{3})+3G.

Contracting three
E_{i}
’s yields an elliptic fibration
\bar{f}:\bar{X}\longrightarrow S
from a canonical surface
\bar{X}
with three
\mathrm{A}_{1}
singularities. Its central fiber
\bar{X}_{s}=2(\bar{F}_{1}+\bar{F}_{2}+\bar{F}_{3})+3\bar{G}
has four components with multiplicities
2
,
2
,
2
, and
3
, respectively. Hence
Lemma

2.3.3
fails without the regularity assumption on
X
.

This time, contract three chains
E_{i}\cup F_{i}
to yield another elliptic fibration
\bar{\bar{f}}:\bar{\bar{X}}\longrightarrow S
from a canonical surface
\bar{\bar{X}}
with three
\mathrm{A}_{2}
singularities. Its central fiber
\bar{\bar{X}}_{s}=3\bar{\bar{G}}
is irreducible with multiplicity
3
. We will
not
call it a multiple fiber because
\bar{\bar{f}}
is not minimal. Indeed, the original minimal model
f
had a non-multiple fiber
X_{s}
.

Semistable fibers are generally better understood than unstable/multiple fibers. The following is a consequence of Kollár’s characterization of semistable fibers.

2.3.8
[
Kol25a
, Theorem 42]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a klt-minimal abelian fibration and
P
its t-automorphism scheme. If
X_{s}
has a reduced component and
P_{s}
is semiabelian, then
X_{s}
has slc singularities. In particular, it is reduced and demi-normal.

2.4.
Dual of the t-automorphism scheme

Recall that the t-automorphism scheme
P
was nothing but the Néron model of the abelian scheme
P_{0}
in (
2.0.2
).

2.4.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a klt-minimal abelian fibration and
P
its t-automorphism scheme. The
dual
of
P
is the Néron model
\check{P}\longrightarrow S
of the dual abelian scheme
\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
.

2.4.2
.

Let
P_{0}
be an abelian scheme over
S_{0}
and
\check{P}_{0}
its dual. Letting
P
and
\check{P}
be their Néron models over
S
, their central fibers
P_{s}
and
\check{P}_{s}
satisfy the following.

(1)

Their linear parts
L_{s}
and
\check{L}_{s}
are isomorphic. In particular, the semiabelian property and
\delta
-regularity of
P
and
\check{P}
are the same.

(2)

If
P_{s}
(or equivalently
\check{P}_{s}
) is semiabelian, then their abelian variety parts
A_{s}
and
\check{A}_{s}
are dual to each other.

Proof.

(1) The abelian scheme
P_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is projective by
[
Ray70
, Theorem XI.1.4]
. Take any polarizations
\lambda_{0}:P_{0}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{0}
and
\check{\lambda}_{0}:\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow P_{0}
that satisfy

\lambda_{0}\circ\check{\lambda}_{0}=[m]_{\check{P}_{0}},\qquad\check{\lambda}_{0}\circ\lambda_{0}=[m]_{P_{0}}.

These homomorphisms and equalities extend over
S
by the Néron mapping property. Hence
\lambda
and
\check{\lambda}
have quasi-finite kernels over
S
. As a result,
\lambda_{s}:P^{\circ}_{s}\longrightarrow\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}
is an isogeny and hence the claim follows from
Lemma

A.1.1
.

(2) This is
[
SGA $7˙I$
, IX Theorem 5.4]
and
[
FC90
, §II.2]
. We note that this statement fails when
P
is not semiabelian. See
Remark

4.1.7
.
∎

By
[
KK25
, Theorem 1]
(or
Lemma

A.0.1
in our case),
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{S}
and
R^{1}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
are locally free and their formation commutes with arbitrary base change. This implies the existence of a locally separated and locally finite type group algebraic space
\Pic_{f}\longrightarrow S
that is smooth along the identity section (
[
BLR90
, Theorem 8.3.1 and 8.4.1]
and
[
Stacks
, Tag 0D2D]
). Its
strict neutral component

\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
is a unique open subgroup space of
\Pic_{f}
with connected fibers. Its
weak neutral component

\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
is the largest open subgroup space of
\Pic_{f}
with a connected total space
\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
. There exists a sequence of open immersions

\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\ \subset\ \Pic^{\bullet}_{f}\ \subsetneq\ \Pic_{f}\qquad\mbox{(the first inclusion is generally not an equality, too)},

and where
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
and
\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
are smooth group algebraic spaces. We note that
\Pic_{f}
is not smooth in general. See
[
Kim24
, §2.2]
for more detailed discussions, where the notation
\Pic^{\circ\circ}_{f}\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\subset\Pic_{f}
is used instead. The following is a higher-dimensional generalization of
[
BLR90
, Theorem 9.5.4]
.

2.4.3
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Then

(1)

\check{P}
is isomorphic to the separated quotient of
\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
.

(2)

\check{P}^{\circ}
is isomorphic to the separated quotient of
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
.

(3)

There exists an isogeny
\kappa:(\Pic^{\circ}_{f})_{s}=\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}\longrightarrow\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}
. If
f
is non-multiple, then
\kappa
is an isomorphism.

See
[
Hol19
, §1.1]
and
[
Kim24
, §2.3]
for the notion of a separated quotient.

Proof.

(1) Since
X
is regular by
Proposition

2.2.4
, the existence part of the Néron mapping property holds for
\Pic_{f}
(and hence
\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
). The uniqueness property fails due to the its non-separatedness, so once we take its separated quotient
\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}/E
, where
E
is the closure of its identity section, the uniqueness holds as well. See
[
Hol19
, Theorem 6.2]
and
[
Kim24
, Lemma 6.12]
. This proves
\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}/E
is the Néron model
\check{P}
. (2) The Néron mapping property constructs a homomorphism
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow\check{P}
, whose image is
\check{P}^{\circ}
and kernel is the closure of the identity section of
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
because
\check{P}
is separated.

(3) Recall that
\Pic_{f}
, as an fppf abelian sheaf, is the sheafification of the fppf presheaf
T\longmapsto\Pic X_{T}/\Pic T
(e.g.,
[
Kle05
, §9.2]
). The fiber
(\Pic_{f})_{s}
over
T=\{s\}
is the sheafification of the same fppf presheaf applied to
X_{s}\longrightarrow\{s\}
, so
(\Pic_{f})_{s}=\Pic_{X_{s}}
. Therefore, the fiber of (2) over
s
yields an isogeny
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}\longrightarrow\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}
in general.

Suppose now that
f
is non-multiple. It is enough to show that
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
is separated, or its identity section is closed. Shrinking
S
, we may assume
f
has a section by
Lemma

2.3.3
, and hence every
S
-section of
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\subset\Pic_{f}
is representable by a line bundle on
X
by
[
Kle05
, Theorem 9.2.5]
. To show the identity section is closed, let
L
be a line bundle on
X
that is
f
-trivial over
S_{0}
. Write
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}E_{i}
for the central fiber and
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
where
D=\sum_{i=1}^{r}b_{i}E_{i}
is a divisor supported in
X_{s}
. Since
L
is a section of
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\subset\Pic^{\tau}_{f}
, it is numerically
f
-trivial over
S
.

Let
Y\subset X
be a complete intersection of
n-1
very ample divisors of
X
, a regular surface
Y
with a fibration over
S
whose central fiber is
Y_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}F_{i}
for a prime divisor
F_{i}
in
Y
. The restriction of the line bundle is
\mathcal{O}_{Y}(D_{Y})
for a divisor
D_{Y}=\sum_{i}b_{i}F_{i}
. Since it is numerically trivial over
S
, Zariski’s lemma for fibrations of surfaces (
[
Bar+04
, Lemma III.8.2]
or
[
Liu02
, Theorem 9.1.23]
) applies and shows
D_{Y}=c\cdot Y_{s}
for
c\in\mathbb{Q}
. Hence
D=c\cdot X_{s}
. But note that
f
was non-multiple and hence we may assume
a_{1}=1
by
Lemma

2.3.3
. This means
c=b_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
and hence the divisor
D
is an integral multiple of
X_{s}
. Hence
L
is
f
-trivial over
S
.
∎

Combining
Proposition

2.4.2
–
2.4.3
, the semistability of a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
f
can be checked from the Picard scheme
\Pic_{X_{s}}
of the central fiber.

2.4.4
.

By Néron–Raynaud,
\check{P}^{\circ}\longrightarrow S
is always a quasi-projective group scheme. By Artin,
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow S
is always a locally separated group algebraic space of finite type. Let us point out a subtle fact that
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
may fail to be a scheme. More precisely, consider the following four conditions:

(i)

f:X\longrightarrow S
is non-multiple.

(ii)

\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow S
is separated.

(iii)

\Pic^{\circ}_{f}=\check{P}^{\circ}
.

(iv)

\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
is a scheme.

We claim (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii)
\Longleftrightarrow
(iii)
\Longleftrightarrow
(iv). The implications (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii)
\Longleftrightarrow
(iii) are noting but
Proposition

2.4.3
(2–3). (iii)
\Longrightarrow
(iv) is clear, and (iv)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) is
[
SGA $3˙I$
,
\mathrm{VI_{B}}
Corollary 5.5]
. We thank Johan de Jong for clearing up our original confusions. We will later prove in
Lemma

7.1.9
that if
f
has multiplicity
m
, then the isogeny
\kappa:\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}\longrightarrow\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}
has
\ker\kappa\subset\mathbb{Z}/m
.

2.4.5
.

In
Example

8.0.8
, a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
with a multiple central fiber
X_{s}=m\cdot(A/a)
will be constructed. Its dual t-automorphism scheme is the constant abelian scheme
\check{P}=\check{A}\times S\longrightarrow S
and
(\Pic^{\circ}_{f})_{s}=\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}=(A/a)^{\vee}
. The homomorphism
(A/a)^{\vee}\longrightarrow\check{A}
in
Proposition

2.4.3
(3) is only an isogeny but not an isomorphism. Indeed, its kernel is generated by the restriction of a line bundle
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}(\frac{1}{m}X_{s})
, which is trivial over
S_{0}
but only numerically trivial over
S
. As a result,
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow S
is a non-separated group space and hence not a scheme.

2.5.
Weierstrass models

A minimal elliptic surface
f:X\longrightarrow S
with non-multiple central fiber admits a unique
Weierstrass model

\bar{f}:\bar{X}\longrightarrow S
.
1
1

1

A more standard terminology is a minimal Weierstrass model. We omitted the word minimal to prevent potential confusions with minimal models.
It is a birational model of
f
where the total space
\bar{X}
has canonical singularities and the central fiber
\bar{X}_{s}
is integral (e.g.,
[
ASD73
, §1]
). One cheap way of constructing Weierstrass model is to use the Kodaira classification:
X_{s}
is always a union of a reduced component and a chain of smooth rational curves with negative ADE intersection matrix. Contracting the latter, we obtain an elliptic fibration
\bar{X}\longrightarrow S
from a canonical surface
\bar{X}
with an integral central fiber, the Weierstrass model of
f
.

One can still contract a different set of rational curves and yield a different model, resulting a total space with possibly klt singularities. This motivates the following definitions.

2.5.1
.

A
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
is
Weierstrass
(resp.
klt-Weierstrass
) if
X
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial and has canonical singularities (resp. log terminal singularities), and the central fiber
X_{s}
is integral (resp. irreducible).

A Weierstrass (resp. klt-Weierstrass) model automatically satisfies
K_{X}\sim_{f}0
(resp.
K_{X}\sim_{f,\mathbb{Q}}0
) because the central fiber is integral (resp. irreducible). In particular, both are klt-minimal. The following are the main results of this subsection.

2.5.2
.

Every
\delta
-regular abelian fibration has a klt-Weierstrass model.

2.5.3
.

Every minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
with a non-multiple central fiber has a Weierstrass model
\bar{f}:\bar{X}\longrightarrow S
. Moreover,
\bar{X}
is a contraction of
X
and is unique up to isomorphism (possibly after shrinking
S
).

Proof of
Proposition

2.5.2
.

Start with a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial klt-minimal model
f:X\longrightarrow S
(e.g., take the minimal model in
Theorem

2.1.4
).

If the central fiber
X_{s}
is irreducible, then
f
is a klt-Weierstrass model. Suppose that
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}E_{i}
has
r\geq 2
components. Take any irreducible component
E=E_{1}
. Then there exists another component
E^{\prime}
intersecting with
E
. Take a curve
C
that is contained in
E
, passing through a point in
E\cap E^{\prime}
, but not contained in any other components
E_{i}
for
i\geq 2
. This makes the intersection
(E\cdot C)
negative, because we have
(X_{s}\cdot C)=0
and
(E^{\prime}\cdot C)>0
, so

(2.5.4)

(E\cdot C)=-\frac{1}{a_{1}}\sum_{i=2}^{r}a_{i}(E_{i}\cdot C)<0.

Fix a rational number
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that the pair
(X,\varepsilon E)
is klt
[
KM98
, Corollary 2.35]
. Since the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
K_{X}+\varepsilon E
is not
f
-nef by (
2.5.4
), we can apply the relative cone/contraction theorem (Theorem 3.25 in op. cit.). First, the cone theorem guarantees the existence of a
(K_{X}+\varepsilon E)
-negative rational curve
R
defining an extremal ray in the relative Mori cone. Such a rational curve
R
must be contained in
E
. We will soon prove in
Corollary

3.1.3
that in this case, the normalization
\tilde{E}
admits a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
over an abelian torsor
A
of dimension
n-1
and moreover, every rational curve in
E
is the image of a fiber of
\tilde{p}
. Therefore, there is only one
(K_{X}+\varepsilon E)
-extremal ray (defined by
R
) and its contraction morphism

\phi:X\longrightarrow\bar{X}

contracts each rational curve in
E
to a point. This means
\phi
is a divisorial contraction of
E
and
\phi(E)\cong A
. This yields a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial klt pair
(\bar{X},\phi_{*}E=0)
by Proposition 3.36 and 3.43 in op. cit. Finally, take the identity

K_{X}\sim_{f,\mathbb{Q}}\phi^{*}K_{\bar{X}}+aE\qquad\mbox{for}\quad a\in\mathbb{Q}.

Since
(K_{X}\cdot R)=0
, we have
a=0
and hence
K_{\bar{X}}
is numerically
\bar{f}
-trivial as well. This means
\bar{f}:\bar{X}\longrightarrow S
is again klt-minimal, so we may run the same process until we reach an irreducible central fiber
X_{s}
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.5.3
.

For the existence, use
Lemma

2.3.3
to fix a reduced component of
X_{s}
. Contract all other components using argument of
Proposition

2.5.2
: this yields a contraction to a klt-Weierstrass model
X\longrightarrow\bar{X}
with an integral central fiber
\bar{X}_{s}=E
. Take a canonical divisor
K_{\bar{X}}
of the form
aE
for
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
E=X_{s}
is Cartier,
K_{\bar{X}}
is Cartier and hence
\bar{X}
is canonical.

Let
\bar{X}
be a Weierstrass
\delta
-regular abelian fibration over
S
and
X
its minimal model. We claim the birational map
\phi:X\dashrightarrow\bar{X}
is a morphism. First, both
X
and
\bar{X}
admit
P^{\circ}
-action by
Theorem

2.1.5
and
\phi
is equivariant. Take a section
S\longrightarrow\bar{X}
through a regular point of
\bar{X}_{s}
and extend it to a section
S\longrightarrow X
via valuative criterion. This picks up a reduced component
E_{i}\subset X_{s}
corresponding to
\bar{X}_{s}
. Contracting all other components yields another Weierstrass model
X\longrightarrow\bar{X}^{\prime}
and a birational map
\bar{\phi}:\bar{X}^{\prime}\dashrightarrow\bar{X}
that generically identifies the central fibers. The undefined loci of
\bar{\phi}
and
\bar{\phi}^{-1}
are disjoint unions of abelian torsors by
Lemma

2.2.3
. Since they do not contain rational curves, both
\bar{\phi}
and
\bar{\phi}^{-1}
are morphisms.

Finally, let
\bar{\phi}:\bar{X}\dashrightarrow\bar{Y}
be a birational map between two Weierstrass
\delta
-regular abelian fibrations. Their minimal models
X\longrightarrow\bar{X}
and
Y\longrightarrow\bar{Y}
are necessarily isomorphic via
\phi:X\longrightarrow Y
by
Proposition

2.2.4
. Say
E\subset X_{s}
and
F\subset Y_{s}
are the reduced components corresponding to
\bar{X}_{s}
and
\bar{Y}_{s}
. Then there exists a t-automorphism of
Y
sending
\phi(E)
to
F
by
Proposition

2.2.2
(possibly after shrinking
S
). Replacing
\phi
by its composition with this t-automorphism, we may assume
\phi(E)=F
, or
\bar{\phi}:\bar{X}\dashrightarrow\bar{Y}
is isomorphic in codimension
1
. Again this extends to an isomorphism.
∎

3.
Irreducible components of the central fiber

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration and
P
its t-automorphism scheme. In this section, we focus on studying each irreducible component
E
of the central fiber
X_{s}
. The main theme is to construct a morphism
p:E\longrightarrow A
to an abelian torsor
A
of dimension
n-1
, such that the
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action on
E
descends to a transitive
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action on
A
(so
p
will be a locally trivial morphism). This is almost always possible, as we will see in this section.

We highlight four main results. In
Proposition

3.1.2
, we show that every irreducible component
E\subset X_{s}
after normalization is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
, with only few exceptions. In
Proposition

3.2.1
, we show that
E
is either a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
- or
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
E\longrightarrow A

without
a normalization, but assuming
X_{s}
is reducible. When
X_{s}
is integral, this may be false, but we will treat this case separately in
Proposition

3.3.1
and
3.4.1
.

3.1.
Irreducible component after normalization

Let us first study the normalization
\tilde{E}
of an irreducible component
E\subset X_{s}
. The results in this subsection were first proved in
[
HO09
, Theorem 1.3]
. Our argument generalizes
[
Kim24
, Proposition 5.14]
.

3.1.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a klt-minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Then every irreducible component
E\subset X_{s}
has a smooth normalization
\tilde{E}
.

Proof.

Take the
P^{\circ}
-action on
X
in
Theorem

2.1.5
. The
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action on
X_{s}
lifts to its normalization
\tilde{X}_{s}
and restricts to its connected component
\tilde{E}\subset\tilde{X}_{s}
. Note that
\Sing(\tilde{E})
is a
P^{\circ}_{s}
-orbit of dimension
\leq n-2
by normality. On the other hand, every orbit of
\tilde{E}
has dimension
\geq n-1
by
Lemma

2.2.3
. This forces
\Sing(\tilde{E})=\varnothing
and hence
\tilde{E}
is smooth.
∎

3.1.2
.

In
Proposition

3.1.1
, assume either of the following.

(i)

P_{s}
is not an abelian variety and the
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action on
E
has a finite kernel (e.g., when
E\subset X_{s}
is a reduced component by
Proposition

2.1.6
).

(ii)

E
has a rational curve.

Then there exists an
P^{\circ}_{s}
-equivariant
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle morphism
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
to an abelian torsor
A
of dimension
n-1
. Moreover,
P^{\circ}_{s}
acts on
A
transitively.

Proof.

(i) Let
K
be the kernel of the action and denote
\bar{P}_{s}=P^{\circ}_{s}/K
. Then the
\bar{P}_{s}
-action on
\tilde{E}
is generically free with a unique open orbit
\bar{P}_{s}\subset\tilde{E}
. Let
0\longrightarrow\bar{L}_{s}\longrightarrow\bar{P}_{s}\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow 0
be the Chevalley exact sequence, where
\bar{L}_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
or
\mathbb{G}_{a}
and
A
is an abelian variety of dimension
n-1
. The equivariant rational map
\tilde{E}\supset\bar{P}_{s}\longrightarrow A
is defined everywhere because
\tilde{E}
is smooth and
A
is an abelian torsor. Since the
\bar{P}_{s}
-action on
A
is transitive, all its fibers are isomorphic to each other and contains the linear part
\bar{L}_{s}
as an open subset. Generic smoothness shows it is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle.

(ii) Note that
P_{s}
cannot be an abelian variety by
Lemma

2.3.4
. Let us further assume that (i) does not hold, i.e.,
E
has a
1
-dimensional kernel containing the linear part
L_{s}\subset P^{\circ}_{s}
(
Proposition

2.1.6
). We may thus consider an
P_{s}^{\circ}/L_{s}=A_{s}
-action instead. Take a sufficiently large finite subgroup
K\subset A_{s}
that contains all stabilizers of the
A_{s}
-action on
\tilde{E}
. Defining the quotients
F=\tilde{E}/K
and
\bar{A}=A_{s}/K
, the variety
F
admits an
\bar{A}
-action by
Lemma

A.1.4
, which is free because we killed every stabilizer. The variety
F
is smooth by arguing
\Sing(F)=\varnothing
as in
Proposition

3.1.1
. The free quotient (e,g,
[
Stacks
, Tag 06PH]
)

F\longrightarrow C=F/\bar{A}

defines an
\bar{A}
-torsor over a smooth projective curve
C
. At this point, use the rational curve assumption to see that
C
is dominated by a rational curve. This proves
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and hence forces
F=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\bar{A}
because
H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\bar{A})=0
.

We have an equivariant morphism
\tilde{E}\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow\bar{A}
to an abelian torsor
\bar{A}
of dimension
n-1
with a transitive
A_{s}
-action. Take its Stein factorization
\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow\bar{A}
. Then
A
admits a transitive
A_{s}
-action as well, so it is an abelian torsor of dimension
n-1
(and
A\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is an isogeny). Moreover,
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
is smooth projective and is locally trivial. Since
\tilde{E}
has a rational curve by assumption, it is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle.
∎

3.1.3
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a klt-minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Then every rational curve in
E\subset X_{s}
is the image of a fiber of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
in
Proposition

3.1.2
. ∎

3.1.4
.

(1)

Propositions
3.1.1
–
3.1.3
are proved in
[
HO09
, §2–3]
in the context of Lagrangian fibrations. A rational curve in
X_{s}
is called a characteristic leaf there, since it is obtained as a leaf of a certain rank
1
foliation induced form the symplectic form.

(2)

A posteriori, (i) implies (ii) in
Proposition

3.1.2
. According to
Lemma

2.2.3
, the kernel of the
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action on
X_{s}
is either finite or
1
-dimensional. It is these two cases that behaved differently in the proof of
Proposition

3.1.2
.

(3)

We will see in
Proposition

3.2.1
that once
X_{s}
is reducible, every irreducible component
E\subset X_{s}
is covered by smooth rational curves.

We conclude with some properties of the morphism
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
, which will be used in future arguments.

3.1.5
.

In
Proposition

3.1.2
, assume the condition (i) holds.

(1)

If
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, then
\tilde{E}
has precisely two
P^{\circ}_{s}
-orbits with nontrivial stabilizers. Both are sections of
\tilde{p}
, which we call
0
and
\infty
.

(2)

If
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, then
\tilde{E}
has precisely one
P^{\circ}_{s}
-orbit with nontrivial stabilizers. It is a section of
\tilde{p}
.

Proof.

The
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\tilde{p}
was constructed as a compactification of the Chevalley morphism
\bar{P}_{s}\longrightarrow A
where
\bar{P}_{s}\subset\tilde{E}
is the open orbit. Its complement
Z=\tilde{E}\setminus\bar{P}_{s}\longrightarrow A
is a
\bar{P}_{s}
-equivariant morphism.

(1)
Z\longrightarrow A
is finite étale of degree
2
because
\bar{L}_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a two-point compactification. The stabilizers of
Z
contain
\bar{L}_{s}
because
\dim Z=n-1
. But the stabilizer of
A
is precisely
\bar{L}_{s}
, so this concludes
Z
has the stabilizers
\bar{L}_{s}
as well. This means
Z
a disjoint union of two sections. (2)
Z\longrightarrow A
is an isomorphism because
\bar{L}_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a one-point compactification.
∎

3.1.6
.

In
Proposition

3.1.2
, assume (ii) holds but (i) fails. Then
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
admits a finite étale Galois base change as follows with the following properties:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(1)

The Galois group
G
acts diagonally on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s}
.

(2)

The
A_{s}
-action on
\tilde{E}
has the stabilizer group
\ker(G\longrightarrow\PGL_{2})\subset A_{s}
at every point
x\in\tilde{E}
.

Proof.

Recall from the proof of
Proposition

3.1.2
that
L_{s}\subset P^{\circ}_{s}
acts trivially on
E
and hence
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
is an
A_{s}
-equivariant morphism. Since the
A_{s}
-action on
A
is transitive (say
G\subset A_{s}
is its stabilizer group), there exists an isogeny
A_{s}\longrightarrow A
with Galois group
G
. Let us temporarily write
\tilde{p}^{\prime}:\tilde{E}^{\prime}\longrightarrow A_{s}
for the base change of
\tilde{p}
. Then the
A_{s}
-action on
\tilde{E}^{\prime}
is free because
\tilde{p}^{\prime}
is
A_{s}
-equivariant and the action is free on
A_{s}
. Its free quotient
\tilde{E}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\tilde{E}^{\prime}/A_{s}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an
A_{s}
-torsor. It is trivial because any fiber of
\tilde{p}
gives its section, showing
\tilde{E}^{\prime}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s}
.

(1) Because
\pr_{2}
is equivariant,
g\in G\subset A_{s}
acts on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s}
as

g\cdot(x,y)=(g_{y}(x),y+g)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s},\ g_{y}:\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}.

This defines a morphism
A_{s}\longrightarrow\PGL_{2}
sending
y\longmapsto g_{y}
, which is necessarily constant since
\PGL_{2}
is affine and
A_{s}
is proper connected. This shows the
G
-action on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s}
is diagonal. (2) directly follows from (1). The same idea will be used again in
Proposition

4.2.4
.
∎

3.2.
Irreducible component without normalization

To analyze the structure of an irreducible component
E\subset X_{s}
without normalization, we need two additional ingredients. The first is the existence of a log terminal contraction in §
2.5
, and the second is the structural results of non-normal varieties (§
A.3
).

The following is the main result of this subsection.

3.2.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Assume the central fiber
X_{s}
is reducible. Then every irreducible component
E\subset X_{s}
, reduced or not, admits an étale locally trivial morphism
p:E\longrightarrow\bar{A}
to an abelian torsor
\bar{A}
with a transitive
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action. Moreover, the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
in
Proposition

3.1.2
exists and is related to
p
by one of the following:

(i)

E=\tilde{E}
is smooth projective and
p=\tilde{p}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle.

(ii)

E
is singular along a section of
p
, and there exists a commutative diagram

Here
\nu
is the normalization, the fiber of
p
is isomorphic to
(xy=0)\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}_{[x,y,z]}
, and
A\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is an isogeny of degree
2
.

That is, when
X_{s}
is reducible, every irreducible component
E
admits an étale locally trivial fiber bundle

p:E\longrightarrow\bar{A}\qquad\mbox{with a fiber }\mathbb{P}^{1}\mbox{ or }\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}\ (=(xy=0)\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}).

The second case did not arise when the relative dimension
n
is
1
, as
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
is not irreducible. For
n\geq 2
, the second case does arise and has already been observed in
[
Mat01
, Figure 1]
. We thank Mirko Mauri for pointing this out. The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of
Proposition

3.2.1
.

A tree of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s is a proper, connected, and reduced curve with nodal singularities, whose irreducible components are
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and dual graph is a tree.

3.2.2
.

In the setting of
Proposition

3.2.1
, every irreducible component
E\subset X_{s}
admits an equivariant isotrivial fibration
p:E\longrightarrow\bar{A}
, where
\bar{A}
is an abelian torsor isogenous to
A_{s}
and its fiber
C
is a tree of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s. In particular,
E
is covered by smooth rational curves, and every rational curve in
E
is smooth.

Proof.

Since
X_{s}
is reducible, we may apply the argument in
Proposition

2.5.2
to a single irreducible component
E\subset X_{s}
and yield a contraction

\phi:X\longrightarrow\bar{X}.

The contracted locus
\bar{A}
is a connected
P^{\circ}_{s}
-invariant subvariety of
X_{s}
of dimension
\leq n-1
. Hence it is a single
A_{s}
-orbit and is an
(n-1)
-dimensional abelian torsor (
Lemma

2.2.3
). Hence the equivariant morphism
p=\phi_{|E}:E\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is locally trivial, say, with fiber
C
.

Let
\bar{Z}\subset\bar{X}
be a complete intersection of
n-1
general very ample divisors and
Z=\phi^{-1}(\bar{Z})
its preimage. Then
Z
is regular by Bertini’s theorem (the divisors are still base point free),
\bar{Z}
is log terminal by
[
KM98
, Lemma 5.17]
, and
\bar{Z}\cap\bar{A}
is
0
-dimensional. Hence
\phi:Z\longrightarrow\bar{Z}
is a resolution of log terminal surface singularities, so its fiber
C
is necessarily a tree of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s as claimed (see case 4–5 in
[
KM98
, Theorem 4.7]
). Every rational curve in
E
is contained in a fiber
C
, so it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
∎

Take the normalization
\nu:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow E
and conductor subscheme diagram (§
A.3
)

(3.2.3)

The diagram is
P^{\circ}_{s}
-equivariant because
E
came equipped with a
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action.
Proposition

3.2.1
will eventually compute the full structures of
\tilde{Z}
and
Z
, but the following weaker claims are sufficient at this point.

3.2.4
.

The following holds in the diagram (
3.2.3
).

(1)

\tilde{Z}_{\red}
(resp.
Z_{\red}
) is a disjoint union of
(n-1)
-dimensional abelian torsors, each of which is an
A_{s}
-orbit in
\tilde{E}
(resp.
E
).

(2)

Every connected component of
\tilde{Z}_{\red}
is isomorphic to each other.

(3)

The surjective morphism
\tilde{Z}_{\red}\twoheadrightarrow Z_{\red}
is an isogeny of abelian torsors on each connected component.

Proof.

(1, 3) Both
\tilde{Z}\subset\tilde{E}
and
Z\subset E
are
P^{\circ}_{s}
-subschemes of dimension
\leq n-1
, so by
Lemma

2.2.3
,
\tilde{Z}_{\red}
and
Z_{\red}
are disjoint unions of abelian torsors of dimension
n-1
, each of which is a single
P^{\circ}_{s}
-orbit. Moreover,
Z
and
\tilde{Z}
are homogeneous and thus have no embedded points. The equivariant morphism
\tilde{Z}_{\red}\longrightarrow Z_{\red}
is a finite étale morphism between disjoint union of abelian torsors.

(2) Suppose that
E
satisfies the condition (i) in
Proposition

3.1.2
. Then
Lemma

3.1.5
shows that
\tilde{Z}_{\red}\subset\tilde{E}
is either one section or a disjoint union of two sections of
\tilde{p}
. Either case,
\tilde{Z}_{\red}
has isomorphic connected components. Suppose now that
E
satisfies the condition (ii) in
Proposition

3.1.2
(by
Lemma

3.2.2
) but not (i). Then
Lemma

3.1.6
shows that
A_{s}
acts on
E
with constant stabilizers. That is, every
A_{s}
-orbit is isomorphic and hence
\tilde{Z}_{\red}
has isomorphic components.
∎

3.2.5
.

Let
p:E\longrightarrow\bar{A}
be a morphism constructed in
Lemma

3.2.2
and
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle in
Proposition

3.1.2
. Then the two are related by an equivariant commutative diagram

\hbox to65.93pt{\vbox to37.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 32.96233pt\lower-18.94716pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{21.78601pt}{-1.59862pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
A\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is an isogeny whose degree is the number of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s in
C
.

Proof.

Corollary

3.1.3
showed every rational curve
R\subset E
must be of the form
\nu(\tilde{R})
where
\tilde{R}
is a fiber of the equivariant
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
. We have already proved that the rational curve
R
is smooth in
Lemma

3.2.2
(and
\nu
collapses only the abelian variety directions by
Lemma

3.2.4
(3)), so the morphism
\nu_{|\tilde{R}}:\tilde{R}\longrightarrow R
is an isomorphism for every rational curve
R\subset E
. In summary, there is a bijective correspondence between the fibers
\tilde{R}
of
\tilde{p}
and (smooth) rational curves
R
in
E
, and
\nu
restricts to an isomorphism between them.

Every fiber
\tilde{R}
is sent to a rational curve
R=\nu(\tilde{R})
in
E
, and hence to a point in
\bar{A}
. Rigidity lemma as in
[
Kaw81
, Lemma 14]
constructs the desired commutative diagram. The diagram is
P^{\circ}_{s}
-equivariant and both
A
and
\bar{A}
are a single
A_{s}
-orbit. This shows
A\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is an isogeny. To compute its degree
d
, take a point
y\in\bar{A}
with fiber
C=p^{-1}(y)
. Its preimage in
\tilde{E}
is
\nu^{-1}(C)
on the one hand, and on the other hand is a disjoint union of
d
number of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibers of
\tilde{p}
. Since
\nu
sends the fibers
\tilde{R}
isomorphically to a rational curve in
C
, this shows the degree
d
coincides with the number of rational curves in
C
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.2.1
.

If
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, then
p:E\longrightarrow A
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle and hence we fall into the first case. Assume from now on that
C
is a tree of at least two
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s.

Take two smooth rational curves
R_{1},R_{2}\subset C
intersecting at a single node
x_{12}\in C
. Say
R_{i}=\nu(\tilde{R}_{i})
for
i=1,2
, where
\tilde{R}_{i}=\tilde{p}^{-1}(t_{i})
for
t_{i}\in A
are fibers (
Corollary

3.1.3
). We have shown in
Lemma

3.2.5
that
\nu
induces isomorphisms
\tilde{R}_{i}\longrightarrow R_{i}
. Therefore, the intersection
x_{12}=R_{1}\cap R_{2}
lifts to two distinct points
\tilde{x}_{1}\in\tilde{R}_{1}
and
\tilde{y}_{2}\in\tilde{R}_{2}
.

Let us first prove that
\tilde{x}_{1}
and
\tilde{y}_{2}
are in the same
P^{\circ}_{s}
-orbit of
\tilde{E}
, or
A_{s}
-orbit because they are in
\tilde{Z}_{\red}
in (
3.2.3
). Assume on the contrary that they are not. Then
Lemma

3.2.4
(1–3) says the two different orbits
A_{s}\cdot\tilde{x}_{1}
and
A_{s}\cdot\tilde{y}_{2}
are connected components of
\tilde{Z}
and are mapped into a single connected component of
Z
, i.e., the two orbits have the same image in
E
. Take any point
\tilde{x}_{2}\in(A_{s}\cdot\tilde{x}_{1})\cap\tilde{R}_{2}
and write it as

\tilde{x}_{2}=a\cdot\tilde{x}_{1}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad a\in A_{s}.

Since
\nu
is equivariant, it sends both
\tilde{x}_{2}=a\cdot\tilde{x}_{1}
and
\tilde{y}_{3}\coloneq a\cdot\tilde{y}_{2}
to a single point
x_{23}\coloneq a\cdot x_{12}\in E
. Take a fiber
\tilde{R}_{3}
of
\tilde{p}
containing
\tilde{y}_{3}
(potentially identical to
\tilde{R}_{1}
) and its image
R_{3}=\nu(\tilde{R}_{3})
, which is a rational curve in
C
intersecting with
R_{2}
at
x_{23}
. This inductive process stops within a finite step, i.e., we have
\tilde{R}_{i}=\tilde{R}_{j}
for some
i<j
. But this constructs a cycle of rational curves in
C
, contradicting the fact that
C
is a tree of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s. This shows
\tilde{x}_{1}
and
\tilde{y}_{2}
are in the same
A_{s}
-orbit.

At this point, we have two smooth rational curves
R_{1},R_{2}\subset C
intersecting at
x_{12}\in Z
, whose lifts
\tilde{x}_{1}
and
\tilde{x}_{2}\coloneq\tilde{y}_{2}
lie in a single
A_{s}
-orbit, or the same connected component of
\tilde{Z}
. We now claim that the Galois group of
A\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is
2
-torsion. Say
\tilde{x}_{2}=a\cdot\tilde{x}_{1}
for
a\in A_{s}
. Take a point
\tilde{x}_{3}\coloneq a\cdot\tilde{x}_{2}
in
\tilde{E}
and a fiber
\tilde{R}_{3}
passing through it. By
Lemma

3.2.5
, the claim is equivalent to
\tilde{R}_{3}=\tilde{R}_{1}
. Assume on the contrary that
\tilde{R}_{3}\neq\tilde{R}_{1}
. Then there are three rational curves
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
, and
R_{3}=\nu(\tilde{R}_{3})
that pass through a single point
x_{12}=\nu(\tilde{x}_{1})=\nu(\tilde{x}_{2})=\nu(\tilde{x}_{3})
. This violates that
C
has only nodes. Therefore, the Galois group of
A\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is
2
-torsion.

It only remains to show that the Galois group of
A\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is
\mathbb{Z}/2
. Assume on the contrary that the Galois group contains
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
. Then there exist four fibers
\tilde{R}_{ij}
for
i,j=0,1
corresponding to the four points in
A
with the same image in
\bar{A}
. Using
Lemma

3.2.4
–
3.2.5
and similar arguments as above, the only possible way to make this sense is that there are eight points
\tilde{x}_{ij},\tilde{y}_{ij}\in\tilde{R}_{ij}
for
i,j=0,1
such that

\nu(\tilde{x}_{ij})=\nu(\tilde{x}_{i+1,j})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\nu(\tilde{y}_{ij})=\nu(\tilde{y}_{i,j+1})\qquad\mbox{for}\quad i,j=0,1

where the additions are taken mod
2
. But this produces four rational curves
R_{ij}
consisting of a cycle in
C
, which is again impossible. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

3.2.6
.

Applying the argument of
Lemma

3.2.2
to a full klt-Weierstrass contraction
\phi:X\longrightarrow\bar{X}
in
Proposition

2.5.2
, its reduced fiber
C=F_{\red}
is again a tree of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s. This already shows that if
X_{s}
is reducible, then for every irreducible component
E_{j}\subset X_{s}
, its complement
\bigcup_{i\neq j}E_{i}
has no loops and has only nodal singularities.

3.3.
Integral fibers: unstable case

Proposition

3.2.1
considered the case when
X_{s}
is reducible. Its starting point was the existence of a log terminal contraction in
Lemma

3.2.2
, which was from the negativity of the intersection number in (
2.5.4
). Such an intersection number is
0
when
X_{s}
is irreducible and the argument fails.

The goal of the remaining two subsections is to treat the case when
X_{s}
is integral separately. In this first subsection, we consider the unstable case.

3.3.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a Weierstrass
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with a central fiber
X_{s}=E
. Assume that
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. Then there exists an equivariant morphism

p:E\longrightarrow A,

where
A
is an abelian torsor of dimension
n-1
with a transitive
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action.

This is a consequence of the following statement about a single variety instead of degenerations of abelian varieties.

3.3.2
.

Let
P
be a connected commutative algebraic group acting on a proper integral variety
E
, both with dimension
n
over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
. Assume

•

P
admits a Chevalley decomposition
0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow 0
for an abelian variety
A
of dimension
n-1
.

•

The action is generically free, and every nontrivial stabilizer is
\mathbb{G}_{a}
.

Then there exists a Zariski locally trivial and
P
-equivariant morphism

p:E\longrightarrow A,

whose fiber
R
is a projective rational curve with one potentially singular point
x\in R
with
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{R,x}=k[[t^{\delta+1},t^{\delta+2},\cdots]]
for
\delta\geq 0
.

Proof of
Proposition

3.3.1
from
Proposition

3.3.2
.

Since
X_{s}=E
is reduced,
P^{\circ}_{s}
acts on it generically freely by
Proposition

2.1.6
. Moreover, every nontrivial stabilizer at
x\in X_{s}
is of the form
\mathbb{G}_{a}\times K_{x}
for a finite group
K_{x}\subset P^{\circ}_{s}
by
Lemma

A.1.2
. Take a sufficiently large finite group
K\subset P^{\circ}_{s}
containing all
K_{x}
, and take the quotient
\bar{P}_{s}=P^{\circ}_{s}/K
and
\bar{X}_{s}=X_{s}/K
. Then
\bar{P}_{s}
acts on
\bar{X}_{s}
(
Lemma

A.1.4
) generically freely with only nontrivial stabilizers
\mathbb{G}_{a}
.
Proposition

3.3.2
shows that
\bar{X}_{s}
is an
R
-fiber bundle over
\bar{A}=A_{s}/K
. In fact,
R
is a cuspidal cubic because
\delta
coincides with the dimension of the linear part of
\Pic^{\circ}_{E}=\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}
, which is
1
in our case. The composition
X_{s}\longrightarrow\bar{X}_{s}\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is the desired morphism.

We note a subtle point that the fiber of
X_{s}\longrightarrow\bar{A}
may
not
be a cuspidal cubic curve. For example, type
\mathrm{III}/2
and
\mathrm{IV}/3
unstable fibrations will produce counterexamples (they will both have integral central fibers, see
Table

1
).
∎

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of
Proposition

3.3.2
.

3.3.1.
Descent along normalization

Keep the notation and assumptions in
Proposition

3.3.2
. Take the normalization
\nu:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow E
. The
P
-action lifts to
\tilde{E}
and
\nu
is equivariant. The
P
-action on
\tilde{E}
is generically free, so the argument in
Proposition

3.1.2
(i) shows that the Chevalley morphism
P\longrightarrow A
compactifies to an equivariant
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle morphism

\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A.

Our goal is to prove
\tilde{p}
descends along
\nu
to a morphism
p:E\longrightarrow A
.

3.3.3
.

The normalization
\nu
is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

Both
E
and
\tilde{E}
have open orbits
j:P\hookrightarrow E
and
\tilde{j}:P\hookrightarrow\tilde{E}
that are identified by
\nu
. Since
P
is a
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-torsor, the complement of
\tilde{j}
is a section
\tilde{e}:A\longrightarrow\tilde{E}
of
\tilde{p}
.

(3.3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

This section is a single
P
-orbit with stabilizer
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. Hence its image
e=\nu\circ\tilde{e}:A\longrightarrow E
is again a single
P
-orbit whose stabilizer contains
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. Every nontrivial stabilizer is
\mathbb{G}_{a}
by assumption, so this shows that
\tilde{e}(A)
and
e(A)
are isomorphic by
\nu
. In particular, the normalization
\nu
is bijective and hence (universally) homeomorphic by
[
Stacks
, Tag 0BRD]
.
∎

Let us keep using the notations in (
3.3.4
) but also consider the conductor subscheme diagram (§
A.3
)

(3.3.5)

\hbox to102.83pt{\vbox to37.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 51.41658pt\lower-18.8472pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-51.41658pt}{-15.18747pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

Lemma

3.3.3
showed that
\mu
induces an isomorphism of
\tilde{Z}_{\red}=\tilde{e}(A)
and
Z_{\red}=e(A)
. The schemes
Z
and
\tilde{Z}
have no embedded points by
P
-equivariance, so
\tilde{Z}\subset\tilde{E}
is a Cartier divisor.

3.3.6
.

Z
is reduced, i.e.,
Z=e(A)
scheme-theoretically.

Proof.

The question is local on
E
. Since
P\longrightarrow A
is a Zariski
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-torsor (e.g.,
[
Mil17
, Proposition 16.55]
), there exists an open subset
0\in U\subset A
with a splitting
P_{U}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times U
. Though
P_{U}
is no longer a group, we may still think of its orbits in
\tilde{E}
and
E
. Fix a reference point
t\in A
, a fiber
\tilde{E}_{t}=\tilde{p}^{-1}(t)=\mathbb{P}^{1}
and its
P_{U}
-orbit
\tilde{E}_{U\cdot t}=\tilde{p}^{-1}(U\cdot t)
. By
Lemma

3.3.3
, its image
E_{U\cdot t}\coloneq\nu(\tilde{E}_{U\cdot t})
is an open subscheme of
Y
. Let us from now on restrict everything over
E_{U\cdot t}
and drop the subscripts for brevity.

Fix a section
U\hookrightarrow P
using the splitting
P=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times U
. The
U
-translations on
\tilde{E}
and
E
have no stabilizers because every nontrivial stabilizer was
\mathbb{G}_{a}\subset P
, whose intersection with
U\subset P
is trivial. Hence the
U
-translations define smooth equivalence relations on both
E
and
\tilde{E}
. Taking quotients, we have a cartesian diagram by
[
Stacks
, Tag 06PH and 04S6]
:

The horizontal arrows are smooth, so
\nu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{E}}/\mathcal{O}_{E}
is the pullback of
\nu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}/\mathcal{O}_{R}
and we may show
\nu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}/\mathcal{O}_{R}
is supported on a reduced point of
R
. This reduces the statement to the case when
E=R
is a
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-curve. For curves, we will prove this with explicit computations in
Proposition

3.3.7
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.3.2
.

Consider the diagram (
3.3.5
).
Lemma

3.3.6
shows that the composition
\tilde{Z}\hookrightarrow\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
descends to an isomorphism
Z\longrightarrow A
along
\mu
. By the universal property of pushout,
\tilde{p}
descends to
p:E\longrightarrow A
along
\nu
. The remaining claims about the rational curve
R
will follow again from
Proposition

3.3.7
.
∎

3.3.2.
Explicit calculations for singular curves

It remains to consider the case when
E=R
is a curve:

3.3.7
.

Let
R
be a projective integral curve with a generically free
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action and let
x\in R
be the only potentially singular point. Then

(1)

\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{R,x}=k[[t^{\delta+1},t^{\delta+2},\cdots]]=k\oplus(t^{\delta+1})\ \subset k[[t]]
for
\delta\geq 0
.

(2)

The conductor subscheme
Z\subset R
is a reduced point
x
.

The generically free
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action has an open orbit
\mathbb{G}_{a}\subset R
, so
R
is a rational curve. Writing
\nu:\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow R
for the normalization, the
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action lifts to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\nu
is equivariant. Fixing an appropriate coordinate of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we can assume

a\cdot[x,y]=[x,\,ax+y]\qquad\mbox{for}\quad[x,y]\in\mathbb{P}^{1},\ a\in\mathbb{G}_{a}.

With this coordinate, the lift of the point
x\in R
is
[0,1]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Locally around
[0,1]
with coordinate
t=x/y
, the action is

(3.3.8)

a\cdot t=\frac{x}{ax+y}=\frac{t}{1+at}=t(1-at+a^{2}t^{2}-\cdots)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad a\in\mathbb{G}_{a},

describing the
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action on every thickening of
[0,1]
.

3.3.9
.

Let
A_{i}=k[t]/t^{i+1}
be a
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-equivariant algebra defined by (
3.3.8
). Then every nonzero
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-equivariant subalgebra of
A_{i}
is of the form

B=k\oplus(t^{\delta+1})\ \ \subset A_{i}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad\delta=0,1,\cdots,i.

Proof.

Assume
B\neq k
and set (we will get
d=\delta+1
)

d\coloneq\max\{d\leq i\,:\,t^{d}\mid f\mbox{ for every }f\in B\mbox{ without constant terms}\}.

Start with
f_{d}=c_{d}t^{d}+\cdots+c_{i}t^{i}\in B
for
c_{d}\neq 0
. Use
\operatorname{char}k=0
to find
a\in\mathbb{G}_{a}
such that

f_{d+1}\coloneq a\cdot f_{d}-f_{d}=c_{d+1}^{\prime}t^{d+1}+\cdots+c_{i}^{\prime}t^{i}\in B\qquad\mbox{with}\quad c_{d+1}^{\prime}\neq 0.

Find polynomials
f_{j}
with leading degrees
j
inductively for all
d\leq j\leq i
. Their linear combinations span
(t^{d})
and thus completes the proof.
∎

.
