computing the ring
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{R,x}
as claimed.

(2) By definition, the conductor ideal is

\mathfrak{c}=\big\{f\in k[[t^{\delta+1},\cdots]]:f\cdot k[[t]]\subset k[[t^{\delta+1},\cdots]]\big\}=(t^{\delta+1}),

which is maximal in
k[[t^{\delta+1},\cdots]]
. Hence
Z=\Spec k
as claimed. We can also compute
\tilde{Z}=\Spec k[[t]]/(t^{\delta+1})=\Spec A_{\delta}
, so in fact we have
i=\delta
.
∎

3.4.
Integral fibers: semistable case

The goal of this subsection is to formulate a semistable version of
Proposition

3.3.1
.

3.4.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a Weierstrass
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with a central fiber
X_{s}=E
. Assume that
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
and take the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
in
Proposition

3.1.2
. Then
E
is obtained by identifying two sections
0
and
\infty
of
\tilde{p}
in
Lemma

3.1.5
(1).

The strategy is similar but simpler than
Proposition

3.3.2
. First, the normalization of
E
admits an equivariant
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\tilde{p}:\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A
as usual. Take the conductor scheme diagram (
3.3.5
): understanding the conductor subschemes
\tilde{Z}\subset\tilde{E}
and
Z\subset E
will determine the variety
E
. The complement of the open orbit
P\subset\tilde{E}
is a union of two sections in this case (
Lemma

3.1.5
), so the conductor subscheme
\tilde{Z}\subset\tilde{E}
is either

\tilde{Z}=\tilde{Z}_{1}\quad\mbox{or}\quad\tilde{Z}_{1}\sqcup\tilde{Z}_{2},\qquad\tilde{Z}_{i,\red}\cong A\mbox{ are sections of }\tilde{p}.

Similar to
Lemma

3.2.4
,
Z
is a union of either one or two abelian torsors, and
\tilde{Z}\longrightarrow Z
is a surjective equivariant morphism. The following is a counterpart of
Lemma

3.3.6
.

3.4.2
.

\tilde{Z}=\tilde{Z}_{1}\sqcup\tilde{Z}_{2}
is a union of two abelian torsors,
Z
is an abelian torsor, and the morphism
\mu:\tilde{Z}\longrightarrow Z
is a disjoint union of two isomorphisms
\tilde{Z}_{i}\longrightarrow Z
.

Proof.

We will use two additional inputs about
X_{s}=E
: it is demi-normal (
Theorem

2.3.8
) and has a trivial canonical bundle. By demi-normality,
E
has nodal singularities in codimension
1
and hence nodal singularities everywhere by equivariance. Therefore, the conductor subschemes
Z
and
\tilde{Z}
are reduced and the morphism
\mu:\tilde{Z}\longrightarrow Z
in diagram (
3.3.5
) is finite étale of degree
2
. This leaves three possibilities:

•

\tilde{Z}\longrightarrow Z
is a degree
2
isogeny between connected abelian torsors.

•

\tilde{Z}=\tilde{Z}_{1}\sqcup\tilde{Z}_{2}
and
Z=Z_{1}\sqcup Z_{2}
are both disconnected, and
\tilde{Z}_{i}\longrightarrow Z_{i}
are degree
2
isogenies of abelian torsors.

•

\tilde{Z}=\tilde{Z}_{1}\sqcup\tilde{Z}_{2}
is disconnected,
Z
is connected, and
\tilde{Z}_{1}\cong\tilde{Z}_{2}\cong Z
are abelian torsors.

To rule out the first two cases, we use the triviality of the canonical divisor. Take a single smooth rational curve
\tilde{R}\subset\tilde{E}
, a fiber of
\tilde{p}
, and its image
R=\nu(\tilde{R})
in
E
. In all cases, take a sufficiently small étale neighborhood
U
of
A
and work on a
\mathbb{G}_{m}\times U
-orbit of
R
. The smooth quotient argument in
Lemma

3.3.6
reduces the claim to the case when
E=R
is a curve with a generically free
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action and trivial canonical divisor. From the computation of
[
Ser88
, §IV.3]
, the arithmetic genus of
E
in the first, second, and third cases are
0
,
0
, and
1
, respectively. Hence only the third case is possible.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.4.1
.

Lemma

3.4.2
implies that
X_{s}=E
is obtained by identifying the two sections
\tilde{Z}_{1}
and
\tilde{Z}_{2}
.
∎

4.
Shears and untangles

From now on, we will always assume that
f:X\longrightarrow S
is a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration of relative dimension
\geq 2
. The goal of this section is to bring forward two different behaviors of
f
. Both of them are only present when the relative dimension
n
is at lest
2
.

The first phenomenon is well-known in the study of semistable degenerations. In the previous section, we have constructed a morphism
p_{i}:E_{i}\longrightarrow\bar{A}_{i}
for each irreducible component
E_{i}\subset X_{s}
in various situations. One hopes to patch them all to a single morphism
X_{s}\longrightarrow A
to an abelian torsor
A
of dimension
n-1
. This is in general impossible: there is an obstruction class
a\in A_{s}
preventing this, which we call a
shear
(
Definition

4.1.3
). See the introduction of
[
Saw22
]
for a nice picture of this.

When
f
is unstable, we will prove that the central fiber
X_{s}
always admits a surjective morphism
X_{s}\longrightarrow A
to an abelian torsor of dimension
n-1
, or equivalently, its Albanese torsor
\Alb_{s}
has dimension
n-1
. The Albanese morphism
\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
will be a locally trivial morphism, but it may fail to be trivial. The obstruction for its triviality can be measured by the stabilizer
G
of the
A_{s}
-action on
\Alb_{s}
, which we call the
Albanese stabilizer
(
Definition

4.2.3
). We will also explain how to clear up this obstruction with a process called the
untangle
of
f
(
Proposition

4.2.4
), which is the key technique in our study of unstable fibrations. This is similar to (but not the same as) the trivialization of the Albanese morphism of a K-trivial variety in
[
Kaw81
, Theorem 15]
.

4.1.
Albanese morphism and shears

The central fiber
X_{s}
is a projective scheme with
h^{0}(X_{s},\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}})=1
by
Lemma

A.0.1
. The existence of the Albanese morphism

\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}\coloneq\Alb_{X_{s}}

in this situation is proved in
[
Con17
,
LS24
]
. See
Theorem

A.2.2
for a summary of their results. The pullback
\alb^{*}
induces an isomorphism between
\Alb_{s}^{\vee}
and the maximal abelian subvariety of
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
. Since
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
has dimension
n
in this case, the dimension of
\Alb_{s}
is bounded above by
n
.

The universal property of
\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
descends the
P_{s}
-action to
\Alb_{s}
, making the Albanese morphism equivariant
[
LS24
, Corollary 10.3]
. The neutral component
P^{\circ}_{s}
acts on
\Alb_{s}
by translations.

4.1.1
.

The
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action on
\Alb_{s}
is transitive. In particular,
\alb
is an étale locally trivial morphism.

Proof.

By
Corollary

A.2.3
,
\Alb_{X_{s,\red}}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
is surjective. Hence it is enough to show the
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action on
\Alb_{X_{s,\red}}
is transitive. Suppose first that
X_{s}
is reducible. Each irreducible component
E_{i}\subset X_{s}
admits a locally trivial bundle
p_{i}:E_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}
by
Proposition

3.1.2
. Therefore, we have an
P^{\circ}_{s}
-equivariant commutative diagram

\hbox to153.46pt{\vbox to49.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 76.72841pt\lower-24.64162pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Since each
A_{i}
admits a transitive
P^{\circ}_{s}
-action, the image of
A_{i}\longrightarrow A\coloneq\Alb_{X_{s,\red}}
is a single
P^{\circ}_{s}
-orbit, an abelian subtorsor of
A
. Hence
\alb(X_{s,\red})=\bigcup_{i}\alb(E_{i})\subset A
is a union of orbits but also connected, so it has to be a single abelian subtorsor of
A
. By the universal property of the Albanese morphism, this proves both
\alb:X_{s,\red}\longrightarrow A
and
A_{i}\longrightarrow A
are surjective. Hence the
P_{s}^{\circ}
-action on
A
is transitive.

Suppose now
X_{s}=m\cdot E
is irreducible. If
P_{s}
is an abelian variety, then
E
is an abelian torsor by
Lemma

2.3.4
. Hence
P_{s}
acts on
X_{s,\red}=\Alb_{X_{s,\red}}
transitively and we are done. Assume that
P_{s}
is not an abelian variety. In (
7.1.2
), we will construct a new
\delta
-regular minimal abelian fibration
Y\longrightarrow T
such that its central fiber
Y_{t}
is integral and
E=Y_{t}/\varphi
is its free quotient. By
Proposition

3.1.2
(i),
\tilde{Y}_{t}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle. By
Proposition

3.1.2
(ii),
\tilde{E}=\tilde{Y}_{t}/\varphi
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle, too. We may thus use a similar diagram above but with a normalization
\tilde{E}\longrightarrow X_{s,\red}
to conclude.
∎

Suppose that
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
and abelian variety part
A_{s}
. Then
\check{P}_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
and abelian variety part
\check{A}_{s}
by
Proposition

2.4.2
. The Chevalley sequence
0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\longrightarrow\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}\longrightarrow\check{A}_{s}\longrightarrow 0
yields an extension class

(4.1.2)

[\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}]\in\Ext^{1}(\check{A}_{s},\mathbb{G}_{m})=A_{s}(k),

where the computation of the Ext-group is from
[
Ser88
, §VII.3.16]
.

4.1.3
.

Assume that
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

(1)

The
shear
of
f
(or
X_{s}
) is the extension class
a\in A_{s}
of the dual t-automorphism group
\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}
in (
4.1.2
).

(2)

We say
f
(or
X_{s}
) has a
torsion shear
if the shear
a\in A_{s}
is torsion.

4.1.4
.

The Chevalley sequence
0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\longrightarrow P^{\circ}_{s}\longrightarrow A_{s}\longrightarrow 0
of the original t-automorphism group measures the non-triviality of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\tilde{E}\longrightarrow A_{s}
in
Proposition

3.1.2
. We thus call its extension class
\check{a}\in\Ext^{1}(A_{s},\mathbb{G}_{m})=\check{A}_{s}
the
slope
of
f
(or
X_{s}
). Notice that shears and slopes play the dual role in the following sense: assume that
f:X\longrightarrow S
and
\check{f}:\check{X}\longrightarrow S
are semistable minimal compactifications of
P
and
\check{P}
, respectively (use
Theorem

2.1.4
and
Proposition

2.2.2
). Then the shear of
\check{f}
is the slope of
f
, and the slope of
\check{f}
is the shear of
f
by
Proposition

2.4.2
. The shear
a\in A_{s}
is torsion if and only if the slope
\check{a}\in\check{A}_{s}
is torsion because
P_{s}^{\circ}
and
\check{P}_{s}^{\circ}
are isogenous.

The terminology will be justified in
Theorem

5.1.1
, or more precisely in
Proposition

5.1.10
. One can consider a similar definition when
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, but it turns out this is redundant. Let us formulate this phenomenon in the following more general context.

4.1.5
.

Let
P\longrightarrow S
be the Néron model of an abelian scheme
P_{0}
over
S_{0}
. If its central fiber
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, then the Chevalley exact sequence of its neutral component uniquely splits. In other words,

P^{\circ}_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}.

Proof.

Start with the dual abelian scheme
\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
and its Néron model
\check{P}\longrightarrow S
. Use
Theorem

2.1.4
and
Proposition

2.2.2
to compactify
\check{P}
into a minimal abelian fibration
\check{f}:\check{X}\longrightarrow S
. Since
P
was
\delta
-regular, so is
\check{P}
by
Proposition

2.4.2
and hence is
\check{f}
. The central fiber
\check{X}_{s}
is non-multiple because it contains
\check{P}_{s}
, so
\check{f}
is an unstable minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Thanks to
Proposition

2.4.3
, the original group
P^{\circ}_{s}
is isomorphic to the neutral Picard group
\Pic^{\circ}_{\check{X}_{s}}
.

Take the Weierstrass model
\check{X}\longrightarrow\bar{X}\longrightarrow S
using
Proposition

2.5.3
.
Proposition

3.3.1
constructs a surjective morphism
\bar{X}_{s}\longrightarrow A
to an abelian torsor of dimension
n-1
, so we have a surjective morphism

\check{X}_{s}\longrightarrow\bar{X}_{s}\longrightarrow A.

This shows the Albanese torsor of
\check{X}_{s}
has dimension
\geq n-1
. Recall that
\Alb_{\check{X}_{s}}^{\vee}
is the maximal abelian subvariety in
\Pic^{\circ}_{\check{X}_{s}}=P^{\circ}_{s}
, a
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-extension of
A_{s}
. Hence
\Alb_{\check{X}_{s}}
has dimension
n-1
and
P^{\circ}_{s}
has an abelian subvariety of dimension
n-1
. This forces the extension class
[P^{\circ}_{s}]\in\Ext^{1}(A_{s},\mathbb{G}_{a})
to be torsion by
[
MM66
, Lemma 1(b)]
. But
\Ext^{1}(A_{s},\mathbb{G}_{a})=H^{1}(A_{s},\mathcal{O}_{A_{s}})=k^{n}
is torsion-free by
[
Ser88
, §VII.3.17]
, so
[P^{\circ}_{s}]
vanishes and the Chevalley sequence splits. The splitting is unique because
\Hom(A_{s},\mathbb{G}_{a})=0
.
∎

4.1.6
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration and
P
its t-automorphism scheme. Assume
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. Then

(1)

There exist unique splittings of algebraic groups

P^{\circ}_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s},\qquad\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}^{*},\qquad\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times\Alb_{s}^{\vee},

where
A_{s}
,
A_{s}^{*}
, and
\Alb_{s}
are isogenous abelian varieties of dimension
n-1
.

(2)

The
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action on
X_{s}
is along the fibers of
\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
.

Proof.

(1) Apply
Theorem

4.1.5
to the Néron models
P
and
\check{P}
. For the splitting of
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
, notice that it is isogenous to
\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}
(
Proposition

2.4.3
) and
\Alb_{s}
is the dual of its maximal abelian subvariety (
Theorem

A.2.2
). (2) The
P_{s}
-action descends to
\Alb_{s}
and
\alb
is equivariant by the universal property. The
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action on
\Alb_{s}
is necessarily trivial.
∎

4.1.7
.

If
f
is non-multiple, then we have
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}=\check{P}_{s}^{\circ}
by
Proposition

2.4.3
and hence
A_{s}^{*}=\Alb_{s}^{\vee}
. If
f
is multiple, then this may not be the case.

Moreover, we caution the reader that the abelian variety parts
A_{s}
of
P_{s}
and
A_{s}^{*}
of
\check{P}_{s}
may
not
be dual to each other, in contrast to
Proposition

2.4.2
(2).
Theorem

5.2.1
will construct counterexamples: when
f
is unstable with nontrivial Albanese stabilizer
G
(see
Definition

4.2.3
), the abelian varieties
A_{s}^{*}=\Alb_{s}^{\vee}=(A_{s}/G)^{\vee}
and
A_{s}
are not dual in general.

4.1.8
.

(1)

If
P_{s}
is an abelian variety, then
\Alb_{s}
has dimension
n
.

(2)

If
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, then
\Alb_{s}
has dimension
\leq n-1
. Moreover, the equality holds if and only if
f
has a torsion shear.

(3)

If
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, then
\Alb_{s}
has dimension
n-1
.

Proof.

(1) If
P_{s}
is abelian, then so is
\check{P}_{s}
and hence is
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
by
Proposition

2.4.3
. (2) Similarly, if
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
then so is
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
. Hence
\Alb_{s}^{\vee}\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
has dimension at most
n-1
. Note further that
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
has a maximal abelian subvariety of dimension
n-1

\Longleftrightarrow

\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}
has a maximal abelian subvariety of dimension
n-1
by
Proposition

2.4.3

\Longleftrightarrow

[\check{P}^{\circ}_{s}]\in\Ext^{1}(\check{A}_{s},\mathbb{G}_{m})
is torsion by
[
MM66
, Lemma 1(b)]
. (3) This follows from
Proposition

4.1.6
.
∎

4.2.
Untangles

The untangling process will be the key to our study of unstable fibrations. We present three versions of it. First,
Lemma

4.2.1
is a primitive version that applies to slightly broader situations. Next, its specialization
Proposition

4.2.4
is the most useful version that applies to unstable fibrations. Finally,
Lemma

7.1.8
is a generalization of
Lemma

4.2.1
to multiple fibrations. The last version is a bit more technical to formulate, so we postpone this to §
7
.

4.2.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Assume that
f
is non-multiple and
\dim\Alb_{s}=n-1
. Then the following holds for each isogeny of abelian torsors
u:A\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
.

(1)

Shrinking
S
étale locally, there exists a unique minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration

(2)

Let
G
be the Galois group of
u
and
\tilde{P}
the t-automorphism scheme of
\tilde{f}
. Then there exists a left exact sequence
0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\tilde{P}\longrightarrow P
.

Proof.

(1) Let
G
be the Galois group of
u
and
\hat{G}=\Hom(G,\mathbb{G}_{m})
its character group. We can realize the isogeny
u
as

u:A=\Spec_{\Alb_{s}}\big(\bigoplus_{\chi\in\hat{G}}L_{\chi}\big)\longrightarrow\Alb_{s},\qquad\mbox{where}\quad\hat{G}\subset\Alb_{s}^{\vee}\ \ \mbox{by}\ \ \chi\longmapsto[L_{\chi}].

Note that
\hat{G}
is sent to
\Alb_{s}^{\vee}=\Pic^{\circ}_{\Alb_{s}}
because the Néron–Severi group of
\Alb_{s}
is torsion-free, and the map is injective because
A
is connected. The base change
\tilde{X}_{s}\coloneq X_{s}\times_{\Alb_{s}}A\longrightarrow X_{s}
is therefore defined by the image
\alb^{*}(\hat{G})\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
of
\hat{G}
sent by an
injective
homomorphism (
Theorem

A.2.2
)

\Alb_{s}^{\vee}\xhookrightarrow{\alb^{*}}\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}.

Since
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
is smooth, shrinking
S
if necessary, we can uniquely extend this to torsion sections
\hat{G}\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{f}(S)
.

Take the four-term exact sequence from the Leray spectral sequence

0\longrightarrow\Pic(S)\longrightarrow\Pic(X)\longrightarrow\Pic_{f}(S)\longrightarrow\Br(S)=H^{2}(S,\mathbb{G}_{m}).

The Picard and Brauer groups of strictly henselian dvrs are trivial
[
Stacks
, Tag 03QO]
. Therefore, we may uniquely lift
\hat{G}\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{f}(S)
to
\hat{G}\subset\Pic(X)
after shrinking
S
étale locally
[
CTS21
, §2.2.2]
. In other words, we obtain torsion line bundles
M_{\chi}
on
X
for every
\chi\in\hat{G}
. The Galois covering
u:\tilde{X}=\Spec_{X}\big(\bigoplus_{\chi\in\hat{G}}M_{\chi}\big)\longrightarrow X
sits in the desired diagram.

At this moment, we need to use the non-multiple fiber assumption to show
\tilde{f}
is an abelian fibration. The non-multiple assumption on
f
implies that
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
is separated (
Proposition

2.4.3
). Hence
\hat{G}\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
is nontrivial over
S_{0}
, showing that the fibers of
\tilde{X}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
are (connected) abelian torsors. This means
\tilde{f}:\tilde{X}\longrightarrow S
is a minimal abelian fibration. The
\delta
-regularity will follow from (2).

(2) The isogeny
\tilde{P}_{0}\longrightarrow P_{0}
over
S_{0}
extends to an isogeny
\tilde{P}\longrightarrow P
over
S
by
[
BLR90
, Proposition 7.3.6]
. More precisely, the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\tilde{P}_{0}\longrightarrow P_{0}\longrightarrow 0
over
S_{0}
extends to a left exact sequence
0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\tilde{P}\longrightarrow P
(e.g.,
[
Kim24
, Proposition 3.29]
).
∎

4.2.2
.

The non-multiple assumption in
Lemma

4.2.1
was used only at the end for the separatedness of
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
. If
f
is multiple, then
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
can be non-separated (
Proposition

2.4.3
–
2.4.4
) and hence some of the torsion sections
\hat{G}\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
may be trivial over
S_{0}
. If this is the case, then
\tilde{f}:\tilde{X}\longrightarrow S
has disconnected fibers over
S_{0}
and hence not an abelian fibration. Avoiding this carefully will give a generalization of the lemma that works for multiple fibrations (
Lemma

7.1.8
).

Specializing to unstable fibrations, we have a preferred choice of an isogeny
u:A\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
in the lemma.

4.2.3
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Assume
f
is unstable. Then its
Albanese stabilizer
is the stabilizer group
G\subset A_{s}
of the
A_{s}
-action on
\Alb_{s}
in
Proposition

4.1.6
. The
untangling isogeny
of
f
is the isogeny of abelian torsors with Galois group
G
:

u:A_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}.

4.2.4
.

In
Lemma

4.2.1
, assume further that
f
is unstable and take
u:A_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
to be the untangling isogeny in
Definition

4.2.3
. Then the following holds.

(1)

\tilde{X}_{s}=C\times A_{s}
for a proper curve
C
and the base change of the Albanese morphism is a projection map
\tilde{X}_{s}\longrightarrow A_{s}
.

(2)

Let
G
be the Albanese stabilizer. Then there exists a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\tilde{P}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0\quad\mbox{such that}\quad G\cap\tilde{P}^{\circ}=0.

In particular, over
s\in S
, there exists a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(P_{s})\longrightarrow 0\quad\mbox{and an isomorphism}\ \ \tilde{P}^{\circ}_{s}=P^{\circ}_{s}.

4.2.5
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be an unstable minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. The unstable minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
\tilde{f}:\tilde{X}\longrightarrow S
in
Proposition

4.2.4
is called the
untangle
of
f
.

Proof of
Proposition

4.2.4
.

(1) Recall from
Proposition

4.1.6
that
X_{s}
admits an
A_{s}
-action and
\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
is an
A_{s}
-equivariant locally trivial morphism. Say its fiber is a proper curve
C
. The base change
\tilde{X}_{s}\longrightarrow A_{s}
is still
A_{s}
-equivariant, so the
A_{s}
-action on
\tilde{X}_{s}
is free. The free
A_{s}
-quotient of
\tilde{X}_{s}
is an
A_{s}
-torsor
[
Stacks
, Tag 06PH]

\tilde{X}_{s}\longrightarrow C\cong\tilde{X}_{s}/A_{s}.

Since
C\subset\tilde{X}_{s}
was a fiber, this
A_{s}
-torsor has a section and hence is trivial.

(2) The left exactness of the sequence is proved in
Lemma

4.2.1
. To show its right exactness, take

\tilde{X}^{\prime}=\tilde{X}\setminus\Sing(\tilde{X}_{s})\quad\mbox{and}\quad X^{\prime}=X\setminus\Sing(X_{s})

as usual and identify
\tilde{P}=\tilde{X}^{\prime}
and
P=X^{\prime}
using the non-multiple fiber assumption and
Proposition

2.2.2
. Since
\tilde{X}^{\prime}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
is surjective,
\tilde{P}\longrightarrow P
is surjective as claimed.

It remains to show
G\cap\tilde{P}^{\circ}_{s}=0
. Both
\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
and
u:A_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
are
P_{s}
-equivariant, so their fiber product
\tilde{X}_{s}=C\times A_{s}
admits a
P_{s}
-action and
\tilde{X}_{s}\longrightarrow X_{s}
is equivariant. This yields a
P_{s}
-equivariant, Galois, finite and étale morphism

\tilde{X}_{s}^{\prime}\longrightarrow X_{s}^{\prime}

with Galois group
G
. But the
P_{s}
-action on
X_{s}^{\prime}
is already free, so this means not only
P_{s}
acts on
\tilde{X}_{s}^{\prime}
freely but the covering
\tilde{X}_{s}^{\prime}\longrightarrow X_{s}^{\prime}
is a disjoint union of isomorphic copies of
X_{s}^{\prime}
. Identifying
P_{s}=X_{s}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{P}_{s}=\tilde{X}_{s}^{\prime}
shows
|\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})|=|\pi_{0}(P_{s})|\cdot|G|
. Therefore, the composition
G\hookrightarrow\tilde{P}_{s}\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})
is injective, or equivalently
G\cap\tilde{P}^{\circ}_{s}=0
.
∎

5.
Classification of non-multiple central fibers

We classify minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with non-multiple fibers in this section.
Theorem

5.1.1
,
5.2.1
, and
5.3.2
are the three main results. The first two results classify semistable and unstable singular fibers, and the last classifies the component group
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
.

5.1.
Semistable fibrations

We classify the semistable central fibers in this subsection. For the notion of identifying two subschemes, see §
A.3
.

5.1.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Assume
f
is semistable. Then
X_{s}
is isomorphic to one of the following.

(
\mathrm{I}_{0}
)

An abelian torsor of dimension
n
.

(
\mathrm{I}_{1}
)

A
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
A_{s}
with two sections
0
and
\infty
identified by a translation by the shear
a\in A_{s}
in
Definition

4.1.3
.

(
\mathrm{I}_{r}
)

A cycle of
r\ (\geq 2)
isomorphic copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
E_{i}\longrightarrow A_{s}
whose sections
0_{i}
and
\infty_{i}
in
Lemma

3.1.5
are successively identified by
\infty_{i}=0_{i+1}
, with an overall translation by the shear
a\in A_{s}
.

5.1.2
.

The
Kodaira type
of a semistable fiber
X_{s}
in
Theorem

5.1.1
is as indicated.

5.1.3
.

Our type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
corresponds to Matsushita’s type
\tilde{A}_{r}
in Theorem 1.2(2) and Figure 2 (left picture) of
[
Mat01
]
. Our type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
corresponds to Hwang–Oguiso’s
[
HO09
]
type
\mathrm{I}_{er}
or
\mathrm{I}_{\infty}
, and coincides with
[
Eng+25
, §6.2]
. The former case arises when the shear
a\in A_{s}
is torsion of order
e
, and the latter case arises when it is not torsion.

5.1.4
.

By
Theorem

5.1.1
, the
slope
and
shear

\check{a}=[P_{s}^{\circ}]\in\check{A}_{s}=\Ext^{1}(A_{s},\mathbb{G}_{m}),\qquad a=[\check{P}_{s}^{\circ}]\in A_{s}=\Ext^{1}(\check{A}_{s},\mathbb{G}_{m})

determine the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
E_{i}\longrightarrow A_{s}
and the gluing datum of
E_{i}
’s, respectively. We note that these two are
dependent
to each other, since they are invariants of the Néron models of the dual abelian schemes
P_{0}
and
\check{P}_{0}
. For example, if the abelian scheme
P_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is principally polarized, then we have an isomorphism
P_{s}\cong\check{P}_{s}
whence the equality of the two extension classes. That is, for each choice of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
E_{i}
, there is a
unique way
of gluing them into a cycle (when
P
is principally polarized).

The rest of this subsection proves
Theorem

5.1.1
.

5.1.5
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be an arbitrary minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Assume
X_{s}
is reducible.

(1)

Let
E\subset X_{s}
be an irreducible component and
R

(=\mathbb{P}^{1}
or
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1})
the fiber of
p:E\longrightarrow\bar{A}
in
Proposition

3.2.1
. Then
(E\cdot R)=-2
.

(2)

Let
E,E^{\prime}\subset X_{s}
be two distinct irreducible components, and
R\subset E
and
R^{\prime}\subset E^{\prime}
be the fibers of
p
and
p^{\prime}
.

(a)

If
E\cap E^{\prime}=\varnothing
, then
(E\cdot R^{\prime})=(E^{\prime}\cdot R)=0
.

(b)

If
E\cap E^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
, then
(E\cdot R^{\prime})
and
(E^{\prime}\cdot R)
are positive.

Proof.

(1) This follows the idea of
[
HO09
, Proposition 4.9]
. The intersection number
(E\cdot R)
is the degree of the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{R}(E)
, which by the adjunction formula is numerically equivalent to

\mathcal{O}_{R}(E)\equiv\mathcal{O}_{R}(K_{X}+E)=\mathcal{O}_{R}(K_{E}).

Note that
R\subset E
has a trivial normal bundle because
p
is locally trivial, so again by adjunction we have

\mathcal{O}_{R}(K_{E})=\mathcal{O}_{R}(K_{R})\otimes\det N_{R/E}^{\vee}=\mathcal{O}_{R}(K_{R}).

Both cases
R=\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
have
\deg K_{R}=-2
.

(2a) is clear. (2b) The intersection
E\cap E^{\prime}
is a disjoint union of abelian torsors of dimension
n-1
by
Lemma

2.2.3
, so it intersects with
R
and
R^{\prime}
at points. Hence
(E\cdot R^{\prime})
and
(E^{\prime}\cdot R)
are positive.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.1.1
(without the claim about shears).

By
Theorem

2.3.8
,
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}E_{i}
is reduced. If
P_{s}
is an abelian variety, then
X_{s}
is a
P_{s}
-torsor by
Proposition

2.2.2
(type
\mathrm{I}_{0}
). Assume from now on
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. If
X_{s}=E
is integral, then
f
is a Weierstrass model and hence it is of type
\mathrm{I}_{1}
by
Proposition

3.4.1
.

Assume
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}E_{i}
with
r\geq 2
. Every irreducible component
E_{i}
admits an
R_{i}
-fiber bundle morphism
p_{i}:E_{i}\longrightarrow\bar{A}_{i}
with fiber
R_{i}=\mathbb{P}^{1}
or
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
by
Proposition

3.2.1
. For each
j=1,\cdots,n
,
Lemma

5.1.5
(1) yields an identity

(5.1.6)

\sum_{i\neq j}(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})=2,

which was the main ingredient of Kodaira’s original proof in
[
Kod63
, Theorem 6.2, (6.5)]
and its generalization in
[
HO09
, Proposition 4.10–4.12]
. Hwang–Oguiso called it a
key formula
. Set
j=1
in (
5.1.6
). There are only two numerical possibilities:

(i)

(E_{r}\cdot R_{1})=(E_{2}\cdot R_{1})=1
, and
E_{i}\cap E_{1}=\varnothing
for
i\neq 1,2,r(\geq 3)
.

(ii)

(E_{2}\cdot R_{1})=2
, and
E_{i}\cap E_{1}=\varnothing
for
i>2
.

Before analyzing the cases, notice that every irreducible component
E_{i}
is isomorphic to each other. This is because
X_{s}
is reduced: by
Proposition

2.2.2
, the
P_{s}
-action on
X_{s}
acts transitively on the set of irreducible components of
X_{s}
. Hence either every
E_{i}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
A_{s}
or every
E_{i}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
\bar{A}
, a degree
2
quotient of
A_{s}
.

(i) Suppose that every
E_{i}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
A_{s}
. Note that
E_{r}\cap E_{1}
and
E_{1}\cap E_{2}
are both
(n-1)
-dimensional orbits by
Lemma

2.2.3
. By
Lemma

3.1.5
,
E_{1}=\tilde{E}_{1}
has precisely two orbits of dimension
n-1
, the two sections
0
and
\infty
of
p_{1}:E_{1}\longrightarrow A_{s}
. If
E_{r}\cap E_{1}=E_{1}\cap E_{2}=0
(or
\infty
), then
E_{r}
,
E_{1}
, and
E_{2}
create three branches around
0
, violating the demi-normality in
Theorem

2.3.8
. This shows
E_{r}\cap E_{1}=0
and
E_{1}\cap E_{2}=\infty
. Same discussion applies to every component, meaning
X_{s}=\sum E_{i}
is a cycle of isomorphic copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles over
A_{s}
. This is type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
for
r\geq 3
.

Suppose that every
E_{i}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
\bar{A}
. The two intersections
E_{r}\cap E_{1}
and
E_{1}\cap E_{2}
are still
(n-1)
-dimensional orbits in
E_{1}
, which are different by the demi-normality of
X_{s}
. According to
Lemma

3.1.5
and the description of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle,
E_{1}
has two orbits of dimension
n-1
: one isomorphic to
A_{s}
and other one isomorphic to
\bar{A}
, the singular locus of
E_{1}
and the only section of
p_{1}:E_{1}\longrightarrow\bar{A}
. None of the possible configurations of
E_{r}
,
E_{1}
, and
E_{2}
can make
X_{s}
demi-normal. Hence this case is impossible.

(ii) Suppose first that every
E_{i}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
A_{s}
. Then a similar argument shows that
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
meet twice along two
(n-1)
-dimensional orbits in
Lemma

3.1.5
. This is type
\mathrm{I}_{2}
. Suppose that every
E_{i}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
\bar{A}
. Again a similar argument leaves only one possibility:
X_{s}=E_{1}+E_{2}
where
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
intersect along the
(n-1)
-dimensional orbits
A_{s}\subset E_{1}
and
A_{s}\subset E_{2}
. Note that the equality
(E_{2}\cdot R_{1})=2
holds because
R_{1}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
meets with
A_{s}
twice. It remains to rule out this possibility.

Since the intersection
E_{1}\cap E_{2}
is isomorphic to
A_{s}
, the two
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
patch together and yields a morphism
\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\bar{A}
, the Albanese morphism of
X_{s}
(use the diagram in
Proposition

3.2.1
and §
A.3
). Applying
Lemma

4.2.1
to the degree
2
isogeny
A_{s}\longrightarrow\bar{A}
, we obtain a new semistable, minimal, and
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
\tilde{f}:\tilde{X}\longrightarrow S
whose central fiber
\tilde{X}_{s}
has four irreducible components. Hence it must be of Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{4}
by the discussion above. The covering
\tilde{X}_{s}\longrightarrow X_{s}
has Galois group
G=\mathbb{Z}/2\subset\tilde{P}_{s}
. Since
\Sing(\tilde{X}_{s})=\bigsqcup^{4}A_{s}
and
\Sing(X_{s})=A_{s}\sqcup\bar{A}\sqcup\bar{A}
for
\bar{A}=A_{s}/a
, the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-action on
\Sing(\tilde{X}_{s})
swaps two
A_{s}
’s and preserves two
A_{s}
’s. This contradicts the following lemma (recall
G\cap P_{s}^{\circ}=0
by
Proposition

4.2.4
), ruling out the last case.
∎

5.1.7
.

Assume
f:X\longrightarrow S
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
for
r\geq 1
. Then every nontrivial stabilizer of the
P_{s}
-action on
X_{s}
is
\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Proof.

Write
Z=\Sing(X_{s})=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{r}(E_{i}\cap E_{i+1})\cong A_{s}^{\sqcup r}
(index taken mod
r
). The group
P_{s}
acts on
X_{s}^{\prime}=X_{s}\setminus Z
freely and on
Z
with nontrivial stabilizers (
Proposition

2.1.6
and
2.2.2
). Let us first show that the stabilizers of
Z
are subgroups of
P_{s}^{\circ}
. Assume on the contrary that
f\in P_{s}\setminus P_{s}^{\circ}
preserves a component
E_{r}\cap E_{1}
. Then the cyclic configuration of
X_{s}
forces
r
to be even and
f(E_{i})=E_{r+1-i}
for all
i
. Hence
f^{2}\in P_{s}
preserves all
E_{i}
, or
f^{2}\in P_{s}^{\circ}
. Taking any
g\in P_{s}^{\circ}
with
g^{2}=f^{2}
(because
P_{s}^{\circ}
is divisible) and adjusting
f
by
f-g
, we may assume
f^{2}=\id
. In this case,
f
has a fixed locus
E_{r}\cap E_{1}
and
E_{r/2}\cap E_{r/2+1}
.

Now take a degree
2
quotient
\bar{f}:\bar{X}=X/f\longrightarrow S
. Around a point
x\in E_{r}\cap E_{1}
(resp.
E_{r/2}\cap E_{r/2+1}
),
f
fixes
E_{r}\cap E_{1}
and swaps the nearby components
E_{1}
and
E_{r}
. Hence the
f
-fixed locus of
T_{x}X\cong k^{n+1}
is
n
-dimensional. The Chevalley–Shepherd–Todd theorem shows
\bar{X}
is regular, and hence
\bar{f}
is a semistable minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. But
\bar{X}_{s}=X_{s}/f
is a
chain
of
r/2

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles over
A_{s}
. This falls into neither of the case (i) or (ii) derived from (
5.1.6
), a contradiction.

Therefore, every stabilizer is contained in
P_{s}^{\circ}
and is linear, so it has a neutral component
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. But the connected components of
Z=\Sing(X_{s})
are isomorphic to
A_{s}
, so the stabilizers must be precisely
\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
∎

5.1.8
.

The intersection numbers
(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})
and
(E_{j}\cdot R_{i})
in (
5.1.6
) may a priori be different. Hwang–Oguiso’s strategy was to make this equality hold by defining a symmetric bilinear form
q(R_{i},R_{j})
on the rational curves
R_{i}
, but allowing possibly infinite chains of them due to a potential non-torsion shear. See the proof of
[
HO09
, Proposition 4.10]
. Our proof dealt with the possible discrepancy
(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})\neq(E_{j}\cdot R_{i})
.
A posteriori
,
(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})=(E_{j}\cdot R_{i})
turns out to hold in the semistable fibrations. This is no longer the case for unstable fibrations due to a potential tangle.

5.1.1.
Gluing and shear

To complete the proof of
Theorem

5.1.1
, it remains to show that the shear
a\in A_{s}
(the extension class of
[\check{P}_{s}^{\circ}]\in\Ext^{1}(\check{A}_{s},\mathbb{G}_{m})=A_{s}(k)
) is equal to the gluing datum described in
Theorem

5.1.1
. This claim will be about a single fiber
X_{s}
but not a degeneration
f
, so to ease the notation, let us prove this in the following setup.

Let
X
be a
type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
variety
for
r\geq 1
in
Theorem

5.1.1
, a reduced projective scheme over
k
obtained by identifying the sections
\infty_{i}
and
0_{i+1}
in
r
isomorphic copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
p:E_{i}\longrightarrow A
over an abelian torsor
A
of dimension
n-1
. Take a normalization of
X
along the node
E_{r}\cap E_{1}
but nothing more. Such a partial resolution
\nu:\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
admits a locally trivial and equivariant Albanese morphism

\alb:\tilde{X}\longrightarrow A,

whose fiber
C
is a
chain
of
r
number of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s. The original
X
is obtained back by gluing two sections (see §
A.3
)

0=0_{1},\ \infty=\infty_{r}:A\longrightarrow\tilde{X},\qquad 0_{A}=0(A),\ \ \infty_{A}=\infty(A)\subset\tilde{X}.

More precisely, the gluing is determined by a morphism

(5.1.9)

0_{A}\sqcup\infty_{A}\longrightarrow A\quad\mbox{combining}\quad\alb:0_{A}\longrightarrow A,\quad t_{a}\circ\alb:\infty_{A}\longrightarrow A,

where
t_{a}:A\longrightarrow A
is a translation by
some
element
a\in A
. Together with
Proposition

2.4.3
, the following proposition concludes
Theorem

5.1.1
.

5.1.10
.

Let
X
be a type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
variety as above, using the contraction morphism (
5.1.9
) determined by
a\in A
. Then the pullback under the morphism
\nu:\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
defines a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{X}\xlongrightarrow{\nu^{*}}\Pic^{\circ}_{\tilde{X}}=\check{A}\longrightarrow 0,

whose extension class is precisely
a\in\Ext^{1}(\check{A},\mathbb{G}_{m})=A
.

Proof.

We write
X_{a}=X
to emphasize that its construction depends on the choice of
a\in A
. Consider the Poincaré line bundle on
\check{A}\times A
rigidified along
\{0\}\times A
and
\check{A}\times\{0\}
. Such a line bundle defines a universal
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-torsor
B\longrightarrow\check{A}\times A
(
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-biextension) whose restriction to
\check{A}_{a}\coloneq\check{A}\times\{a\}
is a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-torsor
0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\longrightarrow B_{a}\longrightarrow\check{A}_{a}\longrightarrow 0
with an extension class
a\in A
. We claim that the two
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-torsors
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{a}}
and
B_{a}
over
\check{A}_{a}
are isomorphic.

Suppose first that
a\in A
is a torsion point of order
e
. Use the universal property of pushouts (
Proposition

A.3.3
) to construct a commutative diagram

Here
\alpha:X_{a}\longrightarrow A/a
is a locally trivial morphism with a fiber
\tilde{C}
, a Kodaira singular curve of type
\mathrm{I}_{er}
. Hence
\alpha^{*}:(A/a)^{\vee}\longrightarrow\Pic_{X_{a}}^{\circ}
is an injective homomorphism. Since
\alb^{*}:\check{A}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\tilde{X}_{a}}
is an isomorphism, the composition
\alpha^{*}\circ\nu^{*}
is an isogeny
(A/a)^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\Pic_{X_{a}}^{\circ}\twoheadrightarrow\check{A}
. This means the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-torsor
\Pic_{X_{a}}^{\circ}\longrightarrow\check{A}
has an
(A/a)^{\vee}
-section, or equivalently the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\longrightarrow\Pic_{X_{a}}^{\circ}\longrightarrow\check{A}\longrightarrow 0
is defined by the extension class
a\in A
.

To prove the statement for arbitrary
a\in A
, notice first that our construction of
X=X_{a}
for each
a\in A
forms a universal family as follows. Start with a trivial family of
\tilde{X}
with two sections
0
and
\infty
over
A
:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The relative neutral Picard scheme of
\mathfrak{X}\longrightarrow A
is a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-torsor
\nu^{*}:\Pic_{\mathfrak{X}/A}^{\circ}\longrightarrow\check{A}\times A
. Comparing this with the Poincaré
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-torsor
B\longrightarrow\check{A}\times A
, the two are isomorphic over
\check{A}_{s}=\check{A}\times\{a\}
for every torsion point
a\in A
by the previous paragraph. The seesaw principle (e.g., Proposition in
[
Mum70
, §10]
) concludes that they are isomorphic for every
a\in A
.
∎

5.2.
Unstable fibrations

Here is a classification of unstable fibers.

5.2.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration. Assume
f
is unstable. Then
\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
is equivariantly isomorphic to

(C\times A_{s})/G\longrightarrow A_{s}/G,

where
C
,
G
, and the action are classified as follows.

•

C
is an unstable Kodaira singular curve: it is of type
\mathrm{II}
,
\mathrm{III}
,
\mathrm{IV}
,
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
for
r\geq 0
,
\mathrm{II}^{*}
,
\mathrm{III}^{*}
, or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
.

•

The Albanese stabilizer
G
is classified as in
Table

3
.

•

G
acts on
C\times A_{s}
diagonally: it acts on
A_{s}
freely by translations, and acts on
C
in a unique way.

Kod. type of
C

\mathrm{II}

\mathrm{III}

\mathrm{IV}

\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
(even
r
)

Alb. stab.
G

0

0,

\mathbb{Z}/2

0,

\mathbb{Z}/3

0,

\mathbb{Z}/2,

(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}

Kod. type of
C

\mathrm{II}^{*}

\mathrm{III}^{*}

\mathrm{IV}^{*}

\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
(odd
r
)

Alb. stab.
G

0

0,

\mathbb{Z}/2

0,

\mathbb{Z}/3

0,

\mathbb{Z}/2,

\mathbb{Z}/4

Table 3.
Classification of the Albanese stabilizer
G
for each unstable Kodaira type curve
C
. The
Kodaira type
of the unstable fiber
X_{s}=(C\times A_{s})/G
is (Kodaira type of
C
)/(order of
G
).

5.2.2
.

The
Kodaira type
of an unstable fiber
X_{s}
in
Theorem

5.2.1
is denoted by

(\mbox{Kodaira type of the curve }C)/(\mbox{order of }G).

For example, when
C
has type
\mathrm{IV}
and
G=0
, we call it a type
\mathrm{IV}
fiber. When
C
has type
\mathrm{IV}
and
G=\mathbb{Z}/3
, we call it a type
\mathrm{IV}/3
fiber. When
C
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
and
G=(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
, we call it a type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}/4
fiber.

5.2.3
.

The Albanese morphism
\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
is a locally trivial fiber bundle with fiber
C
. Its fibers are called
characteristic cycles
in
[
HO09
]
.
Theorem

5.2.1
was proved in
[
KLM26
, Corollary 4.18–19]
under the assumption that
f
is isotrivial. Comparing it with Matsushita’s result, we note that some types in
[
Mat01
, Table 4–5]
(e.g.,
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-1,
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-3,
\mathrm{IV}
-1, and
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
-1) only arise as multiple fibers, therefore not appearing in
Theorem

5.2.1
but only in
Theorem

7.4.1
. Finally, when
C
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
for
r\geq 2
even, the Néron component group
\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})=(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
in
Table

4
has three different subgroups
\mathbb{Z}/2
, but our notation does not distinguish them.

Proof of
Theorem

5.2.1
(without the classification of Albanese stabilizers).

Take an untangle
\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
in
Proposition

4.2.4
, so that
\tilde{f}:\tilde{X}\longrightarrow S
has a central fiber
\tilde{X}_{s}=C\times A_{s}
. We have
X_{s}\cong\tilde{X}_{s}/G
for the Albanese stabilizer
G
in
Definition

4.2.3
. Denote by
\tilde{P}
the t-automorphism scheme of
\tilde{f}
.

Let us first classify the curve
C
. If
C
is integral, then
\tilde{P}^{\circ}_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}
acts on the integral variety
\tilde{X}_{s}=C\times A_{s}
generically freely and with nontrivial stabilizers
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. Hence
Proposition

3.3.2
determines
C
: its unique singular point
x
is locally defined by
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{C,x}=k[[t^{\delta+1},\cdots]]
. The constant
\delta
is captured by the dimension of the linear part of
\Pic^{\circ}_{C}
, which is
1
by the
\delta
-regularity assumption. This concludes that
C
is isomorphic to the cuspidal cubic curve.

Assume now
C
is reducible and write
\tilde{X}_{s}=C\times A_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}E_{i}
. Notice that every irreducible component
E_{i}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s}
. Fix a curve
C\subset\tilde{X}_{s}
and set
R_{i}=C\cap E_{i}
, a rational curve in
E_{i}
. We claim
(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})=(E_{j}\cdot R_{i})
for every
1\leq i,j\leq r
. The variety
\tilde{X}_{s}=C\times A_{s}
has an embedding dimension
n+1
everywhere, so
C
has an embedding dimension
2
. For each point
p\in R_{i}\cap R_{j}\subset C
, we can assume
(C,p)\subset(\mathbb{A}^{2},0)
and hence the local intersection multiplicity at
p
is

(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})_{(\tilde{X},p)}=(R_{i}\cdot R_{j})_{(\mathbb{A}^{2},0)}=(E_{j}\cdot R_{i})_{(\tilde{X},p)},

proving the desired equality
(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})=(E_{j}\cdot R_{i})
. (Note that this completely fails in
X
. That is, an irreducible component
\bar{E}\subset X_{s}
may be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle in
Proposition

3.2.1
(ii) and the above numerical equality may fail for
\bar{E}_{i},\bar{E}_{j}\subset X_{s}
.)

Since we have an equality
(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})=(E_{j}\cdot R_{i})
, we can pretend
q(E_{i},E_{j})=(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})
is a symmetric bilinear form and use the numerical equality

\sum_{i\neq j}a_{i}\cdot q(E_{i},E_{j})=2\qquad\mbox{for}\quad j=1,2,\cdots,n

as in
Lemma

5.1.5
and (
5.1.6
). Now Kodaira’s original proof
[
Kod63
, Theorem 6.2, (6.5)]
applies and computes the desired multiplicities
a_{i}
and configurations of
E_{i}
’s as claimed. Note that Kodaira’s argument is purely numerical with the following exceptions, as already pointed out in
[
HO09
, p. 1003]
:

•

Kodaira’s Lemma 6.1 gives a geometric criterion for
\tilde{X}_{s}
to be non-multiple. We are simply assuming
\tilde{X}_{s}
is non-multiple within this theorem.

•

Kodaira’s equation (6.4) is used to compute the arithmetic genera of irreducible components
E\subset\tilde{X}_{s}
. We have already proved
E=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s}
.

This classifies the curve
C
as claimed.

It remains to classify
G
and its action on
\tilde{X}_{s}=C\times A_{s}
. The action is by t-automorphisms from the inclusion
G\subset P_{s}
in
Proposition

4.2.4
. By construction in
Lemma

4.2.1
, the action on the second factor
A_{s}
is a translation by
G\subset A_{s}
, so the
G
-action on
C\times A_{s}
is of the form

g\cdot(x,y)=(g_{y}(x),y+g)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in C\times A_{s},\quad g_{y}:C\longrightarrow C.

This yields a homomorphism
A_{s}\longrightarrow\Aut_{C}
by
y\longmapsto g_{y}
, which is necessarily constant as in
Lemma

3.1.6
because all irreducible components of
C
are rational curves (
Lemma

A.1.6
). Hence the
G
-action is diagonal.

Finally, recall from
Proposition

4.2.4
(2) the inclusion
G\subset\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})
. Hence it only remains to compute
\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})
. This will be done in
Theorem

5.3.2
(2) and §
6.2.3
. Note that
|\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})|
is the number of reduced components of
C
because of the isomorphism
\tilde{X}_{s}^{\prime}=\tilde{X}_{s}\setminus\Sing(\tilde{X}_{s})\cong\tilde{P}_{s}
(
Proposition

2.2.2
). Hence we already know
|\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})|=1
,
2
,
3
, or
4
, and the only ambiguity arises in the last case where
\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})
can be either
\mathbb{Z}/4
or
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
.
∎

5.3.
Néron component groups

If a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
has a section, then
X^{\prime}=X\setminus\Sing(X_{s})
is isomorphic to the t-automorphism scheme
P
by
Proposition

2.2.2
. Conversely, let
P\longrightarrow S
be a
\delta
-regular Néron model of an abelian scheme over
S_{0}
. Then it admits a unique minimal model
f:X\longrightarrow S
by
Theorem

2.1.4
and
Proposition

2.2.4
with an isomorphism
X^{\prime}\cong P
.

5.3.1
.

Let
P\longrightarrow S
be a
\delta
-regular Néron model of an abelian scheme over
S_{0}
.

(1)

The
minimal compactification
of
P
is a unique minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
with
X^{\prime}=X\setminus\Sing(X_{s})\cong P
.

(2)

The
Kodaira type
of
P
is the Kodaira type of its minimal compactification.

The main result of this subsection is the computation of the Néron component group of
P
, generalizing Kodaira
[
Kod63
, Table 1 in p.604]
and Néron
[
N“’64
, Table in pp.124–125]
into higher dimensions.

5.3.2
.

Let
P\longrightarrow S
be a
\delta
-regular Néron model of an abelian scheme over
S_{0}
.

(1)

If
P
is semistable of type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
, then
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/r
.

(2)

If
P
is unstable, then
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
is classified in
Table

4
. In particular, it is either a cyclic group of order
\leq 4
or
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}

(3)

If
\check{P}\longrightarrow S
is the Néron model of the dual abelian scheme
\check{P}_{0}
, then
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
and
\pi_{0}(\check{P}_{s})
are isomorphic.

Type of
X_{s}

\mathrm{I}_{r}

\mathrm{II}

\mathrm{III}

\mathrm{III}/2

\mathrm{IV}

\mathrm{IV}/3

\pi_{0}(P_{s})

\mathbb{Z}/r

0

\mathbb{Z}/2

0

\mathbb{Z}/3

0

Type of
X_{s}

-

\mathrm{II}^{*}

\mathrm{III}^{*}

\mathrm{III}^{*}/2

\mathrm{IV}^{*}

\mathrm{IV}^{*}/3

\pi_{0}(P_{s})

0

\mathbb{Z}/2

0

\mathbb{Z}/3

0

Type of
X_{s}

\mathrm{I}_{\operatorname{ev}}^{*}

\mathrm{I}_{\operatorname{ev}}^{*}/2

\mathrm{I}_{\operatorname{ev}}^{*}/4

\mathrm{I}_{\operatorname{odd}}^{*}

\mathrm{I}_{\operatorname{odd}}^{*}/2

\mathrm{I}_{\operatorname{odd}}^{*}/4

\pi_{0}(P_{s})

(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}

\mathbb{Z}/2

0

\mathbb{Z}/4

\mathbb{Z}/2

0

Table 4.
Classification of the Néron component group
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
for each Kodaira type of
X_{s}
. Here
X
is a compactification of
P
as a minimal abelian fibration.

5.3.3
.

The classification of the Albanese stabilizers in
Theorem

5.2.1
is not completed at the moment. If we classify
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
when the minimal compactification
X
has no Albanese stabilizers, then this will conclude both
Theorem

5.3.2
(2) and the remaining claims of
Theorem

5.2.1
.

If
P
is semistable or unstable with a trivial Albanese stabilizer, then
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
is isomorphic to the discriminant group of the Cartan matrix of ADE affine Dynkin diagrams. This Cartan matrix is the
intersection matrix
of the minimal compactification
\check{f}:\check{X}\longrightarrow S
of the dual Néron model
\check{P}
.

Though
P
and
\check{P}
have isomorphic Néron component groups, they
may not
have the same Kodaira type. For example, we will see that type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/2
and
\mathrm{I}_{2r}^{*}/2
are dual to each other when
r
is odd.

The rest of this subsection proves
Theorem

5.3.2
when
P
and
\check{P}
are semistable. We will follow the idea of
[
BLR90
, Theorem 9.6.1]
and
[
Liu02
, §10.4.2]
, but note that
P
and
\check{P}
are not isomorphic in our case (they are no longer Jacobians of a family of curves). The proof of the unstable case of
Theorem

5.3.2
will be postponed to the next section, after we discuss
Proposition

6.2.1
and its consequences.

5.3.1.
Semistable case

Theorem

5.3.2
is clear when
P
is an abelian scheme, so let us assume that
P
is strictly semistable. Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be its minimal compactification, whose central fiber
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}E_{i}
is strictly semistable of type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
for
r\geq 1
.

5.3.4
.

For a type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
fiber
X_{s}
, we have an isomorphism

\NS(X_{s})=\mathbb{Z}^{r}\times\NS(A_{s}).

In particular,
\NS(X_{s})
is torsion-free.

Proof.

The singular locus
Z=\Sing(X_{s})
is a disjoint union of
r
isomorphic copies of
A_{s}
, and the normalization
\nu:\tilde{X}_{s}\longrightarrow X_{s}
is a disjoint union of
r
isomorphic copies of a
Zariski-locally trivial

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
A_{s}
. The exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m,X_{s}}\longrightarrow\nu_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m,\tilde{X}_{s}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m,Z}\longrightarrow 0
of fppf abelian sheaves on
X_{s}
induces

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\longrightarrow\Pic_{X_{s}}\xlongrightarrow{\nu^{*}}\Pic_{\tilde{X}_{s}}=(\mathbb{Z}\times\Pic_{A_{s}})^{\times r}\longrightarrow\Pic_{Z}=\Pic_{A_{s}}^{\times r},

where the last homomorphism sends
(L_{i})\in\Pic_{A_{s}}^{\times r}
to
(L_{i}-L_{i+1})\in\Pic_{A_{s}}^{\times r}
. Its kernel is isomorphic to
\Pic_{A_{s}}
and this concludes the lemma.
∎

Therefore, for each line bundle
L
on
X_{s}
, we can define

c_{1}(L)=(\deg L,\ [L])\quad\in\ \ \NS(X_{s})=\mathbb{Z}^{r}\times\NS(A_{s}).

The part
\deg L\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
is often called the
multidegree
of
L
. It is a collection of its
E_{i}
-degree
for
i=1,\cdots,r
, an integer
(L\cdot R_{i})
for a rational curve
R_{i}
in the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
E_{i}
. The
total degree
of
L
is the sum of all
E_{i}
-degrees. Two line bundles
L
and
L^{\prime}
are
numerically equivalent
if
c_{1}(L)=c_{1}(L^{\prime})
.

5.3.5
.

The
intersection matrix
of
X_{s}
is an
r\times r
symmetric matrix over
\mathbb{Z}
whose rows are the multidegrees of
\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}}(E_{i})
. In other words, its
(i,j)
-th entry is the intersection number
q(E_{i},E_{j})\coloneq(E_{i}\cdot R_{j})
, and it is the Cartan matrix of the type
\tilde{A}_{r-1}
affine Dynkin diagram by
Theorem

5.1.1
.

It is convenient to fix a special line bundle
M
on
X_{s}
. When
r\geq 2
, use
Lemma

5.3.4
to fix a line bundle
M
with

\deg M=(0,\cdots,0,-1,1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}\quad\mbox{and}\quad[M]=0\in\NS(A_{s}).

When
r=1
, simply take
M=\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}}
.

5.3.6
.

M^{\otimes r}
is numerically equivalent to
\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}}(E_{1}+2E_{2}+\cdots+(r-1)E_{r-1})
.

Proof.

Every
\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}}(E_{i})
has a multidegree
(0,\cdots,0,1,-2,1,0,\cdots 0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
(where
-2
is at the
i
-th entry) and has trivial
\NS(A_{s})
.
∎

5.3.7
.

Let
L
be a line bundle on
X_{s}
with a total degree
0
and
[L]=0\in\NS(A_{s})
. Then there exists a unique integer
0\leq i<r
such that
L\otimes M^{\otimes-i}
is numerically equivalent to a linear combination of
\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}}(E_{i})
’s.

Proof.

The intersection matrix of
X_{s}
defines a semi-negative symmetric bilinear form on
\mathbb{Z}^{r}=\mathbb{Z}\{E_{i}:i=1,\cdots,r\}
, or equivalently a linear map of rank
r-1
:

\deg:\mathbb{Z}^{r}=\mathbb{Z}\{E_{i}\}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{r}=\mathbb{Z}\{E_{i}^{\vee}\},\qquad E_{i}\longmapsto q(E_{i},-).

Since
\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}}(E_{i})
’s have a total degree
0
, the image of this homomorphism is contained in the kernel of the summation map
\mathbb{Z}^{r}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. The cohomology group of the complex
\mathbb{Z}^{r}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{r}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is the
discriminant group
of the
\tilde{A}_{r}
Cartan matrix

D(\tilde{A}_{r})\cong\mathbb{Z}/r=\langle-E_{r-1}^{\vee}+E_{r}^{\vee}\rangle.

This proves the claim.
∎

5.3.8
.

Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
. Then
L_{s}=L_{|X_{s}}
has a total degree
0
and
[L_{s}]=0\in\NS(A_{s})
if and only if
L
defines a section of
\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
.

Proof.

(
\Longleftarrow
) Recall from
Proposition

2.4.3
the isomorphism
\check{P}=\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}/E
, where
E\subset\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
is the closure of the identity section. Since the Néron model
\check{P}
is quasi-projective, a sufficient power of
L
is numerically equivalent to a line bundle contained in
E
. A line bundle contained in
E
is trivial over
S_{0}
, so it is necessarily a linear combination of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(E_{i})
’s. Each
\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}}(E_{i})
has a trivial class in
\NS(A_{s})
and total degree
0
. Hence, so is the power of
L
. Now use the fact that
\NS(X_{s})=\mathbb{Z}^{r}\times\NS(A_{s})
is torsion-free.

(
\Longrightarrow
) By
Lemma

5.3.6
–
5.3.7
,
L_{s}^{\otimes r}
is numerically equivalent to a linear combination of
\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}}(E_{i})
. The latter is a section of
E\subset\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
, so
L^{\otimes r}
defines a section of
\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
. Since
\pi_{0}(\Pic_{X_{\eta}})=\NS(X_{\eta})
is torsion-free for the generic point
\eta
(because
X_{\eta}
is an abelian torsor),
L
defines a section of
\Pic^{\bullet}_{f}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.3.2
: semistable case.

Assume that
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}E_{i}
is strictly semistable of type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
for
r\geq 1
.
Lemma

5.3.7
–
5.3.8
and
Proposition

2.4.3
show the isomorphism
\pi_{0}(\check{P}_{s})\cong\mathbb{Z}/r
(generated by
M
). To compute
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
, let us consider the dual Néron model
\check{P}\longrightarrow S
and its compactification
\check{f}:\check{X}\longrightarrow S
. This is strictly semistable and hence necessarily of type
\mathrm{I}_{r^{\prime}}
for some
r^{\prime}\geq 1
. Applying
Lemma

5.3.7
–
5.3.8
to
\check{P}
and
\check{f}
instead, we conclude
\pi_{0}(P_{s})\cong\mathbb{Z}/r^{\prime}
. But recall from
Proposition

2.2.2
that
X^{\prime}_{s}=X_{s}\setminus\Sing(X_{s})
is a
P_{s}
-torsor. This shows
P_{s}
has precisely
r
connected components and shows
r=r^{\prime}
.
∎

5.3.9
.

Assume
f
is strictly semistable of type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
. Then
\pi_{0}(P_{s})\cong\mathbb{Z}/r
acts on
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}E_{i}
by cycling the components
E_{i}
.

Proof.

Fix any
f\in P_{s}
that generates the component group
\pi_{0}(P_{s})\cong\mathbb{Z}/r
. Then
f
permutes
E_{i}
without fixing any of them because
X_{s}^{\prime}
is a
P_{s}
-torsor. Moreover, by
Lemma

5.1.7
,
f
permutes the connected components of
\Sing(X_{s})=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{r}(E_{i}\cap E_{i+1})
without fixing any of them. The only possibility is
f(E_{i})=E_{i+1}
for all
i
.
∎

5.3.2.
Unstable case

Similar idea applies to the unstable case, but (1) it cannot compare the Albanese stabilizers of
P
and
\check{P}
, and (2) it does not distinguish types
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
for odd and even
r
. We will complete the proof of
Theorem

5.3.2
(unstable case) in §
6.2.3
, after discussing the semistable reduction theorem in
Proposition

6.2.1
.

6.
Base change behaviors

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be an arbitrary minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration, possibly multiple. Consider a finite Galois morphism
p:T\longrightarrow S
of degree
d
that is totally ramified at
t\in T
over
s\in S
. The morphism
T_{0}=T\setminus\{t\}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is finite étale, and the base change
Y_{0}=X_{0}\times_{S_{0}}T_{0}\longrightarrow T_{0}
is an abelian torsor. Taking its relative minimal model
g:Y\longrightarrow T
, we obtain a commutative diagram

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.19049pt}{-8.77916pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We call such
Y
a
minimal base change
of
X
: it is the minimal model of the base change
f_{T}:X_{T}\longrightarrow T
. The goal of this section is to study the relation between
X
and
Y
. The t-automorphism schemes will play a crucial role as usual. We denote by
P\longrightarrow S
and
Q\longrightarrow T
the t-automorphism schemes of
f
and
g
, respectively.

As a result, we prove the remaining claims in
Theorem

5.3.2
and conclude the classification of Albanese stabilizers in
Theorem

5.2.1
.

6.1.
The inertia actions

Since
Y_{0}\longrightarrow X_{0}
and
Q_{0}\longrightarrow P_{0}
are Galois coverings, both
Y_{0}
and
Q_{0}
admit free
\mu_{d}
-actions. Such actions extend to
Y
and
Q
by the minimal model and Néron mapping property, respectively. We will first study these
\mu_{d}
-actions.

6.1.1.
Inertia action on the t-automorphism group

This subsection follows the results of Edixhoven
[
Edi92
]
.

6.1.1
.

(1)

There exists a natural automorphism
\psi
of order
d
on
Q
making
Q\longrightarrow T
equivariant.

(2)

The induced
\langle\psi\rangle
-action on
Q_{t}
is by group automorphisms:

\psi(a\cdot b)=\psi(a)\cdot\psi(b)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad a,b\in Q_{t}.

Proof.

(1) Write
\zeta:T\longrightarrow T
for the Galois
\mu_{d}
-action on
T
. Then the equivariant
\mu_{d}=\langle\psi_{0}\rangle
-action on
Q_{0}
is modeled by an isomorphism
\Psi_{0}:Q_{0}\longrightarrow\zeta^{*}Q_{0}\coloneq Q_{0}\times_{\zeta}T_{0}
over
T_{0}
with a cocycle condition

(\zeta^{*})^{d-1}\Psi_{0}\,\circ\,\cdots\,\circ\,\zeta^{*}\Psi_{0}\,\circ\,\Psi_{0}=\id_{Q_{0}}.

Both
Q
and
\zeta^{*}Q
are Néron models over
T
, so
\Psi_{0}
uniquely extends to an isomorphism
\Psi:Q\longrightarrow\zeta^{*}Q
over
T
with the same cocycle condition. That is, the
\langle\psi\rangle
-action uniquely extends to
Q
equivariantly over
T
.

(2) The group law
Q_{0}\times_{T_{0}}Q_{0}\longrightarrow Q_{0}
is
\langle\psi_{0}\rangle
-equivariant over
T_{0}
, or the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\times_{T}Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\times_{T}Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\times\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

commutes over
T_{0}
. The diagram automatically commutes over
T
because
Q
is separated (or
Q
is Néron). Restricting it over
t\in T
yields the claim.
∎

To minimize confusions, we write the
\mu_{d}
-action on
Q
by the
\langle\psi\rangle
-action. We note that the induced
\langle\psi\rangle
-action on
Q_{t}
may
not
be faithful, i.e., the order of the automorphism
\psi:Q_{t}\longrightarrow Q_{t}
can be strictly smaller than
d
.

6.1.2
.

The
\langle\psi\rangle
-action on the central fiber
Q_{t}
is called the
inertia action
.

The inertia action on
Q_{t}
is related to the original
P_{s}
as follows. Use the Néron mapping property to extend the isomorphism
P_{T_{0}}=Q_{0}
over
T_{0}
to a homomorphism over
T
:

p^{*}:P_{T}=P\times_{S}T\longrightarrow Q

Its restriction over
t\in T
yields a
pullback
homomorphism of central fibers

(6.1.3)

p^{*}:P_{s}\longrightarrow Q_{t}.

6.1.4
[
Edi92
, Theorem 5.3]
.

Let
p^{*}:P_{s}\longrightarrow Q_{t}
be the pullback homomorphism (
6.1.3
). Then

(1)

\im p^{*}
is the inertia-invariant subgroup
Q_{t}^{\psi}\subset Q_{t}
.

(2)

\ker p^{*}
is unipotent.

In particular, the abelian variety part of
Q_{t}
is always
larger
than that of
P_{s}
. This significantly restricts the behavior of
p^{*}
for
\delta
-regular Néron models.

6.1.5
.

Suppose that
P\longrightarrow S
is
\delta
-regular.

(1)

If
P_{s}
is an abelian variety, then
p^{*}
is an isomorphism.

(2)

If
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, then so does
Q_{t}
and
p^{*}
is an open immersion. Moreover, there exists a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(P_{s})\xlongrightarrow{p^{*}}\pi_{0}(Q_{t})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/d\longrightarrow 0.

(3)

If
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, then
Q_{t}
has a torsion shear.

Proof.

Everything immediately follows from
Theorem

6.1.4
except for the short exact sequence claim in (2).

Assume
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{m}
and
\pi_{0}(P_{s})=\mathbb{Z}/r
(semistable part of
Theorem

5.3.2
). The minimal compactification
f:X\longrightarrow S
of
P
has a strictly semistable central fiber
X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}E_{i}
of type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
. Write
Z=\Sing(X_{s})=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{r}A_{s}
. The usual base change

\bar{g}:\bar{Y}=X_{T}\longrightarrow T

is singular along the preimage of
Z
, which is locally described by an equation
X_{T}=(t^{d}=xy)\subset\Spec k[[x,y,t,z_{1},\cdots,z_{n-1}]]
. It has canonical singularities (type
\mathrm{A}_{d-1}
Du Val) and admits a
crepant
resolution by successive blowups along
A_{s}
. The resulting exceptional divisor is a chain of
d-1
number of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles over
A_{s}
. Therefore, the minimal model
g:Y\longrightarrow T
has a central fiber consisting of a cycle of
r+(d-1)r=dr
number of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles, proving
\pi_{0}(Q_{t})=\mathbb{Z}/dr
again by
Theorem

5.3.2
.
∎

6.1.2.
Inertia action on the minimal model

The same argument produces the
inertia action
on
Y_{t}
. Again to minimize potential confusions, we write such a
\mu_{d}
-action on
Y
the
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action.

6.1.6
.

(1)

There exists a natural automorphism
\varphi
of order
d
on
Y
making
g:Y\longrightarrow T
equivariant.

(2)

The
Q_{t}
-and
\langle\varphi\rangle
-actions on
Y_{t}
are related by

\varphi(a\cdot y)=\psi(a)\cdot\varphi(y)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad a\in Q_{t},\ y\in Y_{t}.

Proof.

(1) Same argument as
Proposition

6.1.1
, but use
Proposition

2.2.4
to extend an automorphism
\varphi_{0}:Y_{0}\longrightarrow Y_{0}
to
\varphi:Y\longrightarrow Y
. (2) Same as the above lemma.
∎

6.1.7
.

Note that the second item means that the
Q_{t}
- and
\langle\varphi\rangle
-actions on
Y_{t}

do not
commute in general. However, the
Q_{t}^{\psi}
- and
\langle\varphi\rangle
-actions commute. Note also that the inertia
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
Y_{t}
may not be faithful in general.

We may therefore consider the quotient
\bar{X}=Y/\varphi
and a commutative diagram

(6.1.8)

\hbox to103.64pt{\vbox to38.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 51.81934pt\lower-18.65274pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-51.81934pt}{-14.99301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

Since
\bar{f}:\bar{X}\longrightarrow S
is klt-minimal,
Theorem

2.1.5
applies and endows it with a
P^{\circ}
-action. The following extends this to a
P
-action.

6.1.9
.

There exists a unique
P
-action on
\bar{X}
extending the
P_{0}
-torsor structure on
\bar{X}_{0}=X_{0}
. Consequently, the birational map
\bar{X}\dashrightarrow X
is
P
-equivariant.

Proof.

The base change
P_{T}\longrightarrow T
is a smooth group scheme with an equivariant
\mu_{d}
-action generated by
\zeta:P_{T}\longrightarrow P_{T}
(pulled back from
\zeta:T\longrightarrow T
). The pullback homomorphism
p^{*}:P_{T}\longrightarrow Q
of t-automorphism schemes endows
Y
with a
P_{T}
-action with the following commutative diagram:

The rightmost diagram is nothing but
Proposition

6.1.6
, and the first two diagrams are commutative because they commute over
T_{0}
. Hence
P\times_{S}Y\longrightarrow Y
is
\langle\varphi\rangle
-equivariant, and its quotient is the desired group action morphism
P\times_{S}\bar{X}\longrightarrow\bar{X}
.
∎

For simplicity, let us assume that the
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
\bar{X}_{s}
is faithful in the following two lemmas (if not, factorize
T\longrightarrow S
into two steps). Define a closed subscheme
\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}\subset\bar{X}
by

\tfrac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}\coloneq Y_{t}/\varphi\ \ \subset\ \ \bar{X}=Y/\varphi.

It is of pure codimension
1
, has no embedded points, and is a closed subscheme of
\bar{X}_{s}
.

6.1.10
.

Assume that the
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
\bar{X}_{s}
is faithful. Then there exists an equality of Weil divisors
\bar{X}_{s}=d\cdot\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}
on
\bar{X}
. In particular, the multiplicity of
\bar{X}_{s}
is divisible by
d
.

Proof.

Notice an isomorphism of schemes

\bar{X}_{s}=\big(q^{-1}(\bar{X}_{s})\big)/\varphi=Y_{d}/\varphi\qquad\mbox{for}\quad Y_{d}=g^{-1}(\Spec k[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{d}).

Since the closed immersion
Y_{t}\subset Y_{d}
is set-theoretically equal and their multiplicities differ by a factor of
d
, the same holds for
\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}\subset\bar{X}_{s}
.
∎

6.1.11
.

The
P_{s}
-action on
\bar{X}_{s}
restricts to that on
\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}
.

Proof.

Since the
Q_{t}^{\psi}
- and
\langle\varphi\rangle
-actions on
Y_{t}
commute by
Proposition

6.1.6
, the
Q_{t}^{\psi}
-action on
Y_{t}
descends to
\frac{1}{d}X_{s}=Y_{t}/\varphi
. This means we can endow
\frac{1}{d}X_{s}
with a
P_{s}
-action via
p^{*}:P_{s}\longrightarrow Q_{t}^{\psi}
(so it may not be a faithful action). To show this action is compatible with the
P_{s}
-action on
X_{s}
, take a
\mu_{d}
-equivariant diagram

6.2.
Semistable base change of unstable fibrations

In this subsection, we discuss an alternative interpretation of
Theorem

5.2.1
using a base change to a semistable fibration. This will complete the promised proof of
Theorem

5.3.2
and will also be extensively used in the future
Theorem

7.3.2
and
7.4.1
.

According to the semistable reduction theorem (e.g.,
[
BLR90
, Theorem 7.4.1]
), every unstable abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
admits a semistable base change
g:Y\longrightarrow T
, i.e., a finite (totally ramified) base change
T\longrightarrow S
such that
f_{T}
has a semistable minimal model
g
. We make this explicit for minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibrations as follows. Let us first consider the case with no Albanese stabilizers.

6.2.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be an unstable minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with trivial Albanese stabilizer, and let
g:Y\longrightarrow T
be its semistable base change of degree
d
.

(1)

If
f
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
\mathrm{II}
,
\cdots
, or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, then
g
is smooth with a central fiber

Y_{t}\cong Q_{t}\cong E\times A_{s}\quad\mbox{for an elliptic curve }E,

and its inertia action is
\varphi(x,y)=\psi(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)
for
(x,y)\in E\times A_{s}
. Here
\omega
is the primitive
d^{\prime}
-th root of unity for
d^{\prime}\mid d
, which is classified as follows:

(a)

If
f
has type
\mathrm{II}
or
\mathrm{II}^{*}
, then
j(E)=0
and
d^{\prime}=6
.

(b)

If
f
has type
\mathrm{III}
or
\mathrm{III}^{*}
, then
j(E)=1728
and
d^{\prime}=4
.

(c)

If
f
has type
\mathrm{IV}
or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, then
j(E)=0
and
d^{\prime}=3
.

(d)

If
f
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
, then
d^{\prime}=2
.

(2)

If
f
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
for
r\geq 1
, then
d=2k
is even and
g
is strictly semistable of type
\mathrm{I}_{2kr}
with a central fiber

Y_{t}\cong C\times A_{s}\ \ \supset\ \ Q_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2kr\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}.

Considering
C
as
C^{\nu}=\mathbb{Z}/2kr\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
with identifications
(i,\infty)=(i+1,0)
, the inertia involutions are
\varphi(i,z,y)=\psi(i,z,y)=(-i,\frac{\zeta^{i}}{z},y)
for
(i,z,y)\in C\times A_{s}
or
\mathbb{Z}/2kr\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
. Here
\zeta
is a primitive
2k
-th root of unity.

The following deals with the case when
f
has a nontrivial Albanese stabilizer.

6.2.2
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be an unstable minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with a nontrivial Albanese stabilizer
G
, and
\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
its untangle in
Proposition

4.2.4
. Then every semistable base change
g:Y\longrightarrow T
of
f
admits a cartesian diagram

\hbox to67.56pt{\vbox to37.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 33.78177pt\lower-18.94716pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-33.78177pt}{-15.28743pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{

{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

such that
\tilde{g}:\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow T
has a trivialized central fiber as in
Proposition

6.2.1
. Letting
Q
and
\tilde{Q}
be the t-automorphism schemes
g
and
\tilde{g}
, the following holds.

(1)

There exists a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\tilde{Q}\longrightarrow Q\longrightarrow 0
. In particular,
Q_{t}=\tilde{Q}_{t}/G
and
Q^{\circ}_{t}=\tilde{Q}^{\circ}_{t}/(G\cap\tilde{Q}^{\circ}_{t})
.

(2)

Assume that
\tilde{f}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
\cdots
, or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
. Then
G\hookrightarrow\tilde{Q}_{t}=E\times A_{s}
decomposes into two injections
\tau:G\hookrightarrow E^{\omega}\subset E
and
\sigma:G\hookrightarrow A_{s}
.

(3)

Assume that
\tilde{f}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
for
r\geq 1
. Then recalling the unique splitting
\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
, we have

G\cap\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\circ}=\begin{cases}\langle(-1,a)\rangle\qquad&\mbox{for odd }r\\
\langle(-1,a)\rangle\quad\mbox{or}\quad 0\qquad&\mbox{for even }r\\
\end{cases}.

Here
a\in A_{s}
is a nonzero
2
-torsion point. Moreover, the Albanese morphism
\alb:Y_{t}\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}
is always a free
G
-quotient of
\pr_{2}:\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}\longrightarrow A_{s}
.

6.2.3
.

The
conjugate
of a Kodaira type
\mathrm{T}=\mathrm{II}
,
\cdots
,
\mathrm{IV}^{*}/3
is
\mathrm{T}^{*}
. Two Kodaira types are
isogenous
if they are the same up to Albanese stabilizers.

For example, the conjugate of
\mathrm{III}^{*}/2
is
\mathrm{III}/2
, and
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/2
and
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/4
are isogenous Kodaira types.

.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be an unstable minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration and
g:Y\longrightarrow T
its arbitrary semistable base change of degree
d
. Then

(1)

is determined by the type of
g
and the order of the inertia automorphism
\psi
on
Q_{t}
as in
Table

5
.

(2)

Assume
g
is smooth and
d=d^{\prime}
is minimal in

6.2.1
(1). Assume that the generator of
\mu_{d}
acts on
T
by
\omega
. Then
f
has a
*
-type if and only if
\psi
acts on the elliptic curve

by
\omega^{-1}
.

(3)

The Albanese stabilizer
G
of
f
is captured by the non-triviality of the central fiber
Y_{t}
of
g
.

d^{\prime}\mid d
,

is smooth, and the inertia
\mu_{d^{\prime}}
-action on

is faithful.

(2)

\varphi^{i}:Y_{t}\longrightarrow Y_{t}
has a fixed point for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

(1) Assume
\varphi^{k}
acts as the identity on
Y_{t}
for
d=kd^{\prime}
. Then the fixed locus of
\varphi^{k}:Y\longrightarrow Y
is set-theoretically
Y_{t}
and forces
Y_{t,\red}
to be regular (the fixed locus of a finite order automorphism of a regular scheme is again regular). Since
Y
is not multiple, this means
Y_{t}
is regular whence
Y\longrightarrow T
is smooth. The intermediate quotient
g^{-}:Y^{-}=Y/\varphi^{k}\longrightarrow T^{-}=T/\mu_{k}
is again an abelian torsor.

(2) Suppose we have
d=ij
for integers
1<i,j<d
and consider a
\mu_{j}=\langle\varphi^{i}\rangle
-action on
Z
. Since
f:X\longrightarrow S
has a section (after shrinking
S
), so does
Y/\varphi^{i}\longrightarrow T/\mu_{j}
. But this has a central fiber of multiplicity
j
by
Lemma

6.1.10
, so the total space
Y/\varphi^{i}
cannot be regular. Hence the
\langle\varphi^{i}\rangle
-action on
Y_{t}
cannot be free.
∎

Thanks to
Lemma

6.2.5
(1), up to replacing
Y
with
Y^{-}
we may assume
d=d^{\prime}
and the inertia
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action is faithful. The pullback homomorphism in (
6.1.3
) is

(6.2.6)

p^{*}:P_{s}\cong\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}\longrightarrow Q_{t},

and it necessarily has
\ker p^{*}=\mathbb{G}_{a}
and
\im p^{*}=Q_{t}^{\psi}=\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times A_{s}\subset Q_{t}
by
Theorem

6.1.4
.

6.2.7
.

(1)

\bar{X}_{s}\cong d\cdot\bar{C}\times A_{s}
and
\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}\cong\bar{C}\times A_{s}
for a proper curve
\bar{C}
.

(2)

Taking
A_{s}\subset P_{s}
, the
A_{s}
-action on
\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}=\bar{C}\times A_{s}
is only on the second factor by translations.

Proof.

By
Proposition

6.1.9
, the birational map
\bar{X}\dashrightarrow X
is
P
-equivariant. Hence its undefined loci are
P_{s}
-invariant closed subsets of
\bar{X}
and
X
of dimension
\leq n-1
. By
P^{\circ}_{s}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}
and
Lemma

2.2.3
they are
A_{s}
-orbits with finite stabilizers. This means
\bar{X}
and
X
can be connected by a sequence of blowups and blowdowns along
A_{s}
-orbits. Since
X
is untangled, the
A_{s}
-action on
X_{s}=C\times A_{s}
is free and acting only on the second factor by translations. Therefore, all intermediate birational models connecting
\bar{X}
and
X
have central fibers of the form
C_{i}\times A_{s}
with translation
A_{s}
-action on the second factor. In particular, same holds for
\bar{X}_{s}
and hence
\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}
.
∎

6.2.1.
When
g
is smooth

The abelian fibration
g
is either smooth or strictly semistable. Let us first consider the case when
g
is smooth. Then
Q_{t}
is an abelian variety and
Y_{t}
is a
Q_{t}
-torsor. Identify them using a
\varphi
-fixed point
0\in Y_{t}
in
Lemma

6.2.5
(2) as a reference point. Then the two inertia actions
\psi
on
Q_{t}
and
\varphi
on
Y_{t}
can be identified as well, thanks to
Proposition

6.1.6

\varphi(a\cdot 0)=\psi(a)\cdot\varphi(0)=\psi(a)\cdot 0\qquad\mbox{for}\quad a\in Q_{t}.

As a result, we do not need to distinguish
Q_{t}
and
Y_{t}
(and their inertia actions).

6.2.8
.

Q_{t}=Y_{t}\cong E\times A_{s}
for an elliptic curve
E
.

Proof.

We had
\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}\cong\bar{C}\times A_{s}
from
Lemma

6.2.7
. The composition
Y_{t}\longrightarrow Y_{t}/\varphi=\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}\longrightarrow A_{s}
is
A_{s}
-equivariant whence locally trivial. Any
A_{s}
-orbit in
Y_{t}
constructs its section, so
Y_{t}\cong E\times A_{s}
for an elliptic curve
E
.
∎

6.2.9
.

Choosing an appropriate isomorphism
Q_{t}=Y_{t}\cong E\times A_{s}
, the inertia action is of the form

\psi(x,y)=\varphi(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in E\times A_{s}.

Proof.

Start with an isomorphism
Q_{t}\cong E\times A_{s}
in
Lemma

6.2.8
. Recall from (
6.2.6
) that
\{0\}\times A_{s}\subset Q_{t}
is fixed by
\psi
. Hence the group automorphism
\psi
of an abelian variety
E\times A_{s}
is necessarily of the form

\psi(x,y)=(\alpha x,y+\xi(x))\qquad\mbox{for}\quad\alpha\in\Aut_{0}(E),\quad\xi:E\longrightarrow A_{s}\mbox{ homomorphism}.

If
x\in E^{\alpha}
, then
\varphi=\psi
acts on
\{x\}\times A_{s}
as a translation by
\xi(x)\in A_{s}
. If it is a nontrivial translation, then the quotient
\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}=(E\times A_{s})/\varphi
cannot be of the form
\bar{C}\times A_{s}
in
Lemma

6.2.7
. This proves
\xi(x)=0
for every
x\in E^{\alpha}
. The isogeny
1-\alpha:E\longrightarrow E
has a kernel
E^{\alpha}
, so this factorizes
\xi
into

\hbox to95.53pt{\vbox to41.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 47.76213pt\lower-19.40274pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-33.63545pt}{-6.86388pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
