}}{
}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{17.91682pt}{-6.11665pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\qquad\mbox{where }\ \eta:E\longrightarrow A_{s}\mbox{ is a homomorphism}.

Define an automorphism
u
on
E\times A_{s}
by
u(x,y)=(x,y+\eta(x))
. Direct computation shows
u\circ\psi\circ u^{-1}
is an automorphism on
E\times A_{s}
by
(x,y)\longmapsto(\alpha x,y)
.

To show
\alpha=\omega^{\pm 1}
, consider
\pi_{0}(P_{s})=\pi_{0}(Q_{t}^{\psi})=\pi_{0}(E^{\alpha})
. The order of
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
is already decided at the end of the proof of
Theorem

5.2.1
: it is either
1
,
2
,
3
, or
4
. For each of the possibility, there exists a unique
\alpha\in\Aut_{0}(E)
with
|E^{\alpha}|=|\pi_{0}(P_{s})|
. This shows
\alpha=\omega^{\pm 1}
and
j(E)
as desired in
Proposition

6.2.1
.
∎

This completes the proof of
Proposition

6.2.1
(1). In fact, we can describe the singularities of
\bar{X}=Y/\varphi
in each type and hence the explicit decomposition of the birational map
\bar{X}\dashrightarrow X
into a sequence of blowups and blowdowns, copying the process in
[
Bar+04
, §V.10, p.209]
. We also note the following equivalences, generalizing
[
KLM26
, Proposition 4.24–25]
:
\psi
acts on
E
by
\zeta^{-1}

\Longleftrightarrow

\bar{Y}
has canonical singularities
\Longleftrightarrow

Y\longrightarrow\bar{Y}
is a birational morphism
\Longleftrightarrow

f
has a
*
-type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
\mathrm{II}^{*}
,
\mathrm{III}^{*}
, or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
. If
d\neq 2
and
\psi
acts on
E
by
\zeta
, then
f
has a non-
*
-type
\mathrm{II}
,
\mathrm{III}
, or
\mathrm{IV}
.

6.2.2.
When
g
is strictly semistable

Suppose now
g
is strictly semistable of type
\mathrm{I}_{R}
for
R\geq 1
. In the previous subsection, we have shown that
g
is smooth if and only if
X_{s}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
\mathrm{II}
,
\cdots
, or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
. Hence
X_{s}
in this case necessarily has type
\mathrm{I}^{*}_{r}
for
r\geq 1
. Again the pullback homomorphism (
6.2.6
) must satisfy
\ker p^{*}=\mathbb{G}_{a}
and
\im p^{*}=Q_{t}^{\psi}=\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times A_{s}\subset Q_{t}
by
Theorem

6.1.4
. This means
Q_{t}
contains an abelian subvariety of dimension
n-1
, so
Q_{t}
has a torsion shear by
Corollary

4.1.8
. But more is true:

6.2.10
.

Q_{t}^{\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
.

Proof.

As shown,
p^{*}
induces an inclusion
A_{s}\hookrightarrow Q_{t}^{\circ}
. Take the Chevalley homomorphism
Q_{t}^{\circ}\longrightarrow B_{t}
to an abelian variety
B_{t}
of dimension
n-1
. We claim that the composition
A_{s}\hookrightarrow Q_{t}^{\circ}\twoheadrightarrow B_{t}
is an isomorphism. This will prove the desired splitting, which is unique because
\Hom(A_{s},\mathbb{G}_{m})=0
.

According to the classification in
Theorem

5.1.1
,
\Sing(Y_{t})
is a disjoint union of isomorphic copies of
B_{t}
. Recall that
A_{s}\subset\im p^{*}=Q_{t}^{\psi}\subset Q_{t}
acts on
\Sing(Y_{t})
, and commutes with
\varphi
by
Proposition

6.1.6
. Since the
A_{s}
-action on
\Sing(Y_{t})
is by translations, if
\varphi
fixes a connected component
B_{t}\subset\Sing(Y_{t})
then it acts on it by translations (because it commutes with all translations). Hence
\Sing(Y_{t})/\varphi
is a disjoint union of quotients of
B_{t}
. But such a quotient has a free
A_{s}
-action since
\frac{1}{d}\bar{X}_{s}
has a free
A_{s}
-action by
Lemma

6.2.7
. This forces the translation
A_{s}
-action on
B_{t}
to be free, or
A_{s}=B_{t}
as claimed.
∎

6.2.11
.

(1)

R
is even.

(2)

The inertia
\langle\psi\rangle
-action on
Q_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}/R\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
satisfies

\psi(0,z,a)=(0,\tfrac{1}{z},a),\quad\psi(\tfrac{R}{2},z,a)=(\tfrac{R}{2},\pm\tfrac{1}{z},a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(z,a)\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}.

Proof.

Fix an isomorphism
Q_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}/R\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
. Recall that
Q_{t}^{\psi}\cong\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times A_{s}
has a neutral component
A_{s}
. Hence
\psi
must act on
Q_{t}^{\circ}
by
\psi(0,z,a)=(0,z^{-1},a)
. From it we deduce
(Q_{t}^{\circ})^{\psi}\cong\mu_{2}\times A_{s}
and
|\pi_{0}(Q_{t}^{\circ})^{\psi}|=2
. Since
X_{s}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
, we have
|\pi_{0}(P_{s})|=|\pi_{0}(Q_{t}^{\psi})|=4
by
Theorem

5.2.1
(again, we have not computed
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
yet but know its order is
4
). Hence
Q_{t}^{\psi}
is contained in precisely one more component
\frac{R}{2}\in\pi_{0}(Q_{t})=\mathbb{Z}/R
(see
[
Edi92
, Remark 5.4.1]
) and
R
is even.

Write
\psi_{i}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
for the restriction of
\psi
to the
R/2
-th component of
Q_{t}
. Again
Q_{t}^{\psi}=\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times A_{s}
, so
(\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s})^{\psi_{i}}
consists of two copies of
A_{s}
. This means
\psi_{i}
acts trivially on the
A_{s}
-factor and fixes two points on
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Finally, since
\psi
respects group operations, we have an identity
\psi(R/2,z,a)^{2}=\psi(0,z^{2},2a)=(0,z^{-2},2a)
. This makes
\psi_{i}(z)^{2}=z^{-2}
for all
z\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
, or
\psi_{i}(z)=\pm z^{-1}
.
∎

6.2.12
.

There exists an isomorphism
Y_{t}\cong C\times A_{s}
for a Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{R}
semistable curve
C
. This induces an identification of
Q_{t}
with
Y_{t}^{\prime}=Y_{t}\setminus\Sing(Y_{t})
that respects the inertia
\langle\psi\rangle
- and
\langle\varphi\rangle
-actions.

Proof.

Since
R
is even by
Corollary

6.2.11
, let us temporarily write
R=2u
. Write
Y_{t}=\sum_{i=0}^{2u-1}E_{i}
, where every irreducible component
E_{i}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s}
as a compactification of
Q_{t}^{\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
. By
Lemma

6.2.5
(2),
\varphi:Y_{t}\longrightarrow Y_{t}
admits at least one fixed point
y
.

We first claim that every fixed point is contained in
Y_{t}^{\prime}=Y_{t}\setminus\Sing(Y_{t})
. This is similar to the proof of
Lemma

5.1.7
. Assume on the contrary that
\varphi
fixes a point
y\in E_{0}\cap E_{1}\subset\Sing(Y_{t})
(in fact,
\varphi
fixes the entire
E_{0}\cap E_{1}
by
Proposition

6.1.6
). If
\varphi
preserves
E_{1}
, then
Corollary

6.2.11
shows that
\varphi
swaps
0_{1}
and
\infty_{1}
, a contradiction. Hence
\varphi
swaps
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
, and hence
\varphi(E_{i})=E_{2u+1-i}
for all
i
. The fixed locus of
\varphi
is precisely
(E_{0}\cap E_{1})\sqcup(E_{u}\cap E_{u+1})=A_{s}\sqcup A_{s}
. But then for every point
y\in E_{0}\cap E_{1}
,
\varphi
acts around
y
trivially in the direction of
A_{s}
and swaps the two irreducible components
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
. This means
\varphi
acts on the tangent space
T_{y}Y\cong k^{n+1}
by fixing an
n
-dimensional subspace. Hence the quotient
Y/\varphi=\bar{X}
is regular by the Chevalley–Shephard–Todd theorem. This implies
\bar{X}\cong X
by the uniqueness of the minimal abelian fibration, a contradiction.

We may thus assume
\varphi(y)=y
for a point
y\in Y_{t}^{\prime}
. Assume without loss of generality
y\in E_{0}
. Then a similar argument as above trivializes the
Q_{t}
-torsor
Y_{t}^{\prime}
, identifying their inertia actions. Recall from
Corollary

6.2.11
that
\psi
acts trivially on the second factor of
Q_{t}^{\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
. Since
\varphi
preserves the component
E_{0}
, this proves
\varphi
acts trivially on the
A_{s}
-direction of
E_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s}
. This property propagates to every component
E_{i}
inductively along the intersections
E_{i}\cap E_{i+1}
. Note that
\varphi
fixes (at least) two components
E_{0}
and
E_{u}
by
Corollary

6.2.11
. Hence
\varphi
preserves every rational curve in
E_{0}
and
E_{u}
.

Start with a unique rational curve
C_{0}\subset E_{0}
containing
y
. Then
\varphi
swaps the two points
p_{1}=C_{0}\cap E_{1}
and
p_{u}=C_{0}\cap E_{2u-1}
. Take the rational curves
C_{1}\subset E_{1}
through
p_{1}
and
C_{2u-1}\subset E_{2u-1}
through
p_{2u}
. Set
p_{2}=C_{1}\cap E_{2}
and
p_{2u-1}=C_{2u-1}\cap E_{2u-2}
. Then
\varphi
swaps
p_{2}
and
p_{2u-1}
. This inductive process defines
p_{u}\in E_{u-1}\cap E_{u}
and
p_{u+1}\in E_{u+1}\cap E_{u}
that are swapped by
\varphi
. Now take the unique rational curves
C_{u},C_{u}^{\prime}\subset E_{u}
through
p_{u}
and
p_{u+1}
, respectively. Then
\varphi
swaps these two rational curves. But
\varphi
preserves every rational curve in
E_{u}
by
Corollary

6.2.11
(2), so this makes
C_{u}=C_{u}^{\prime}
. That is,
Y_{t}
has no shears.
∎

6.2.13
.

(1)

d
is even and
R
is divisible by
d
. Write
d=2k
and
R=2kr
for positive integers
k
and
r
.

(2)

There exists a primitive
2k
-th root of unity
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
such that the inertia
\langle\psi\rangle
-action on
Q_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2kr\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
is

\psi(i,z,a)=(-i,\tfrac{\zeta^{i}}{z},a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z,a)\in\mathbb{Z}/2kr\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}.

Proof.

We may from now on identify the
\langle\varphi\rangle
- and
\langle\psi\rangle
-actions by
Lemma

6.2.12
. We can now say
\psi
has order
d
because
\varphi
had order
d
by
Lemma

6.2.5
(1). This concludes
d
is even by
Corollary

6.2.11
, so write
d=2k
.

Note that
\varphi
either preserves or reverses the
orientation
of
C=\sum_{i=0}^{R-1}C_{i}
. Since
\psi(0,z,a)=(0,z^{-1},a)
by
Corollary

6.2.11
,
\varphi
must reverse the orientation. Shifting the index
i
appropriately, we can assume
\varphi(E_{i})=E_{-i}
. This makes
\psi
of the form

\psi(i,z,a)=(-i,\psi_{i}(z,a))\qquad\mbox{for}\quad\psi_{i}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}.

Since
\psi
respects group operations, we have a sequence of identities

\psi(i,z,a)=\psi(i,1,0)\cdot\psi(0,z,a)=(-i,\psi_{i}(1,0))\cdot(0,z^{-1},a)=(-i,\psi_{i}(1,0)\cdot(z^{-1},a)).

Hence
\psi_{i}(z,a)=\zeta_{i}\cdot(z^{-1},a)
for
\zeta_{i}\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
. Since
\psi(i,z^{i},ia)=\psi(1,z,a)^{i}=(1,\zeta_{1}\cdot(z^{-1},a))^{i}=(i,\zeta_{1}^{i}\cdot(z^{-i},ia))
, we have
\zeta_{i}=\zeta^{i}
for a single
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
. Since
\varphi
acted on the
A_{s}
-direction by translations,
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Since
\psi
had order
2k
,
\zeta
is a primitive
2k
-th root of unity. Finally,
\zeta_{R}=\zeta^{R}=1
shows
R
is divisible by
2k
.
∎

Notice that
\varphi^{2}(i,z,a)=(i,\zeta^{-2i}z,a)
acts on
Y_{t}
freely. Therefore, replacing
Y
with the intermediate quotient
g^{-}:Y^{-}=Y/\varphi^{2}\longrightarrow T^{-}=T/\mu_{k}
, we may assume
k=2
and
\varphi
is an involution. The fixed locus of the involution
\varphi
consists of
A_{s}\sqcup A_{s}\subset C_{0}\times A_{s}
and
A_{s}\sqcup A_{s}\subset C_{r}\times A_{s}
. These are reflected by the singular locus
\sqcup^{4}A_{s}\subset\bar{X}=Y/\varphi
, which are subsequently blown up into four
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times A_{s}
to yield a type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
of
X_{s}
. This completes the proof of
Proposition

6.2.1
(2).

6.2.3.
Néron component groups and Albanese stabilizers

We can finally complete the promised proof of the unstable case of
Theorem

5.3.2
and the classification of Albanese stabilizers in
Theorem

5.2.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.3.2
(2) and
Theorem

5.2.1
.

We need to compute
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
when
P
has no Albanese stabilizers. In this case,
P
admits a semistable base change to
Q\longrightarrow T
by
Proposition

6.2.1
with
Q_{t}=E\times A_{s}
or
\mathbb{Z}/2r\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
. The component group
\pi_{0}(P_{s})
can be computed by
\pi_{0}(Q_{t}^{\psi})
. In the former case, the inertia action is
\psi(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)
and hence
Q_{t}^{\psi}=E^{\omega}\times A_{s}
has the desired component groups. In the latter case, the inertia action is
\psi(i,z,y)=(-i,(-1)^{i}z^{-1},y)
, so again
Q_{t}^{\psi}
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}\times A_{s}
when
r
is even, and
\mathbb{Z}/4\times A_{s}
when
r
is odd.
∎

Let us now assume the Albanese stabilizer is nontrivial.
Corollary

6.2.4
follows from our discussions above and the following proof of
Proposition

6.2.2
, so we omit it.

Proof of
Proposition

6.2.2
.

Consider the untangle
\tilde{f}:\tilde{X}\longrightarrow S
of
X
and take its semistable base change
\tilde{g}:\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow T
using
Proposition

6.2.1
. Let
G\subset\tilde{P}_{s}=\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})\times\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}
be the Albanese stabilizer of
X
. The pullback
p^{*}:\tilde{P}_{s}\longrightarrow\tilde{Q}_{t}
has a kernel
\mathbb{G}_{a}
by
Theorem

6.1.4
, so it sends
G
isomorphically to a subgroup
p^{*}G\subset\tilde{Q}_{t}
. Hence we obtain a section
p^{*}G\subset\tilde{Q}
and a
p^{*}G
-action on
\tilde{Y}
. We claim this action is free. If so, then the quotient
g:Y=\tilde{Y}/p^{*}G\longrightarrow S
is a minimal abelian fibration and hence the semistable base change of the original
f
.

If
\tilde{g}
is smooth then
\tilde{Q}_{t}
acts on
\tilde{Y}_{t}
freely, so the
p^{*}G
-action is free. Suppose that
\tilde{g}
is strictly semistable, i.e.,
\tilde{f}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
and
\tilde{g}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{2r}
for
r\geq 1
. The only nontrivial stabilizer of
\tilde{Y}_{t}
is
\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset\tilde{Q}_{t}
by
Lemma

5.1.7
. From the description of
Proposition

6.2.1
(2), we have

\im p^{*}\cap\mathbb{G}_{m}=\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\tilde{\psi}}\cap\mathbb{G}_{m}=\{\pm 1\}.

Therefore, if
p^{*}G
acts non-freely on
\tilde{Y}_{t}
, then
G\subset\tilde{P}_{s}=\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})\times\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}
contains an element
f=(\alpha,0,0)
swapping the two arms
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
and
E_{4}
of the type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
curve
C=E_{1}+E_{2}+2(\sum_{i=0}^{r}F_{i})+E_{3}+E_{4}
. However, such
f
does not act freely on
\tilde{X}
because it does not have any translation on the
A_{s}
-direction, contradicting the fact that
\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
is a free
G
-quotient. This shows
p^{*}G
acts on
\tilde{Y}
freely.

(1) Since
\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow Y
is a free
p^{*}G
-quotient of non-multiple abelian fibrations, we can imitate the proof of
Proposition

4.2.4
(2). We caution the reader that the second part of the proof does not apply in this situation. or
p^{*}G
may have a nontrivial intersection with
\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\circ}
. This is because the proof was based on the observation
Q_{t}^{\circ}=\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\circ}
, which does not hold in this case.

(2) Recall from
Proposition

6.2.1
an isomorphism
\tilde{Q}_{t}=E\times A_{s}
. Write
p^{*}:G\hookrightarrow E\times A_{s}
by
p^{*}(g)=(\tau(g),\sigma(g))
for
\tau:G\longrightarrow E
and
\sigma:G\longrightarrow A_{s}
. By
Proposition

4.2.4
, we have
G\cap\tilde{P}_{s}^{\circ}=0
and hence
p^{*}G\cap(\{0\}\times A_{s})=0
. This means
\tau:G\longrightarrow E
is injective. If
\sigma(g)=0
for some nontrivial
g\in G
, then the quotient
\bar{Y}=\tilde{Y}/p^{*}(g)
would have had a central fiber
\bar{Y}_{t}=E/p^{*}(g)\times A_{s}
. This would mean that
\bar{X}=\tilde{X}/g
has a trivial Albanese stabilizer, a contradiction. Hence
\tau:E\longrightarrow A_{s}
is injective, too. Finally, because the
p^{*}G
-action on
E\times A_{s}
commutes with
\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)
, we have
\tau(g)\in E^{\omega}
.

(3) We have an isomorphism
\tilde{Q}_{t}=\mathbb{Z}/2r\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
and the classification of Albanese stabilizers
G
in
Theorem

5.2.1
. Similar to above, notice that
\im p^{*}\cap\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\circ}=\{\pm 1\}\times A_{s}
. This shows
p^{*}G\cap\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\circ}=0
or
\langle(-1,a)\rangle
for a
2
-torsion point
a\in A_{s}
. Since we have already proved
p^{*}G\cap\mathbb{G}_{m}=0
above,
a\neq 0
.

The
p^{*}G
-action on
\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}
is diagonal. If
r
is odd, then
1\in\mathbb{Z}/4=\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})
is
(r,\sqrt{-1},a)\in\tilde{Q}_{t}
for a torsion point
a\in A_{s}
of order
4
. This proves the claim. If
r
is even, then
G\subset(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}=\pi_{0}(P_{s})
is generated by
(0,1,a)
and
(r,1,b)
for
a,b\in A_{s}
of order
2
. Assume on the contrary
a=b
. Then
g=(r,1,0)\in\pi_{0}(P_{s})
acts on the
A_{s}
-factor of
\tilde{Y}_{t}
trivially. Since
\mathbb{Z}/2=\langle g\rangle
acts on
C
by preserving the order and without fixed points,
\bar{C}=C/g
is a Kodaira singular curve of type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
. But
\bar{Y}=\tilde{Y}/g\longrightarrow T
has a central fiber
\bar{Y}_{t}=\bar{C}\times A_{s}
and it is a semistable base change of
\bar{X}=\tilde{X}/g\longrightarrow S
, so
Proposition

6.2.1
shows that such
\bar{X}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{\frac{r}{2}}^{*}
. Contradiction.
∎

6.2.14
.

The dual of the
\langle\psi\rangle
-action on
Q_{t}
is the
\langle\check{\psi}^{-1}\rangle
-action on
\check{Q}_{t}
.

Proof.

Recall from
Proposition

6.1.1
how the inertia
\langle\psi\rangle
-action on
Q_{t}
was defined. Take the Galois action
\zeta:T\longrightarrow T
of the base, and consider an isomorphism
\Psi:Q\longrightarrow\zeta^{*}Q
of abelian schemes over
T
(with a cocycle condition). Its dual is an isomorphism of dual abelian schemes
\check{\Psi}:\zeta^{*}\check{Q}\longrightarrow\check{Q}
, whose inverse is an isomorphism
(\check{\Psi})^{-1}:\check{Q}\longrightarrow\zeta^{*}\check{Q}
that describes the
\langle\check{\psi}\rangle
-action by definition. Comparing the central fibers of the two descriptions yields the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.3.2
(3): unstable case.

Assume that
P
has an Albanese stabilizer
G
. Then
Proposition

6.2.2
shows
Q_{t}=(E\times A_{s})/G
or
(\mathbb{Z}/2r\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s})/G
. Suppose first that
Q_{t}=(E\times A_{s})/G
. Then the dual t-automorphism group
\check{P}
has a semistable base change
\check{Q}\longrightarrow T
, whence the central fiber
\check{Q}_{t}=((E\times A_{s})/G)^{\vee}
. By
Lemma

6.2.14
, its inertia action is
\check{\psi}^{-1}
. To compute its invariant group, notice that we have a further
G
-quotient
Q_{t}\longrightarrow E/G\times A_{s}/G
. Its dual is a
\hat{G}
-quotient

(E/G)^{\vee}\times(A_{s}/G)^{\vee}\longrightarrow\check{Q}_{t}=((E\times A_{s})/G)^{\vee}.

Since
\check{\psi}^{-1}
acts on
(E/G)^{\vee}\times(A_{s}/G)^{\vee}
by
\check{\psi}(\check{x},\check{y})=(\omega^{\pm 1}\check{x},\check{y})
, computation of the
\check{\psi}
-invariant shows its Albanese stabilizer is
\hat{G}
, which is isomorphic to
G
.

Suppose that
P
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/G
for odd
r
(so
Q_{t}=(\mathbb{Z}/2r\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s})/G
). In this case,
G=0
,
\mathbb{Z}/2
, or
\mathbb{Z}/4
. For each
G
, we can compute
\pi_{0}(Q_{t})
and
Q_{t}^{\circ}
as follows.

Type of
P

G

\pi_{0}(Q_{t})

Q_{t}^{\circ}

\pi_{0}(\check{Q}_{t})

\check{Q}_{t}^{\circ}

\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}

0

\mathbb{Z}/2r

\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}

\mathbb{Z}/2r

\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\check{A}_{s}

\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/2

\mathbb{Z}/2

\mathbb{Z}/2r

(\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s})/2

\mathbb{Z}/2r

\mathbb{G}_{m}\times(A_{s}/2)^{\vee}

\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/4

\mathbb{Z}/4

\mathbb{Z}/r

(\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s})/2

\mathbb{Z}/r

(\mathbb{G}_{m}\times(A_{s}/4)^{\vee})/2

To compute
\check{Q}_{t}
, notice that it has the same type as
Q_{t}
by the semistable version of the theorem, so
\pi_{0}(\check{Q}_{t})\cong\pi_{0}(Q_{t})
. The abelian variety parts of
Q_{t}
and
\check{Q}_{t}
are dual by
Proposition

2.4.2
, and
(A_{s}/G)^{\vee}\subset\check{Q}_{t}^{\circ}
by
Proposition

6.2.2
(3). This computes
\check{Q}_{t}^{\circ}
. For example, when
G=\mathbb{Z}/4
,
\check{Q}_{t}^{\circ}
has an abelian variety part
(A_{s}/2)^{\vee}
and contains
(A_{s}/4)^{\vee}
. Do the same computation for even
r
and collect all possible pairs
(Q_{t},\check{Q}_{t})
for every type of
P
. Direct comparison shows that
(Q_{t},\check{Q}_{t})\cong(\check{Q}^{\prime}_{t},Q^{\prime}_{t})
implies
G\cong G^{\prime}
. This concludes the theorem. For example, type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/2
and
\mathrm{I}_{2r}^{*}/2
are dual to each other for odd
r
, yet their
\pi_{0}
-groups are the same.
∎

6.2.15
.

The Albanese stabilizer
G
of an unstable fibration sits in the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow A_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}\longrightarrow 0
. Taking the dual, we obtain
0\longrightarrow\hat{G}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}^{\vee}=A_{s}^{*}\longrightarrow\check{A}_{s}=\Alb_{\check{X}_{s}}\longrightarrow 0
. This suggests that the Albanese stabilizers of
P
and
\check{P}
are (more precisely) Cartier dual to each other.

7.
Classification of multiple central fibers

Throughout this section, let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration whose central fiber
X_{s}
has multiplicity
m>1
. As usual,
P\longrightarrow S
is its t-automorphism scheme. We will see in a moment that there exists a preferred base change
g:Y\longrightarrow T
with a t-automorphism scheme
Q\longrightarrow T
.

Now the classification will be divided into three part. When
P
is semiabelian, we classify
X_{s}
in
Theorem

7.2.1
and
7.3.1
, and call them type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}_{r}
for
r\geq 0
. When
P
is not semiabelian (so
P_{s}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
), we divide the case by two depending on the behavior or
Q
. When
Q
is still semiabelian, we classify
X_{s}
in
Theorem

7.2.2
and
7.3.2
, and call them type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}_{r}^{\pm}
for
r\geq 0
. Finally, when both
P
and
Q
are not semiabelian, we classify
X_{s}
in
Theorem

7.4.1
. In each case, we will have to patiently analyze all numerically possibilities.

7.1.
Non-multiple base change

Take a finite totally ramified Galois morphism
p:T\longrightarrow S
of degree
m
, which is precisely the multiplicity of
X_{s}
. In this special case, the minimal model
g:Y\longrightarrow T
can be explicitly constructed as a normalized base change

(7.1.1)

7.1.2
.

Let
T\longrightarrow S
be a totally ramified base change of degree
m
and
Y
the normalized base change of
X
. Then

(1)

q
is étale.

(2)

g
is a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration that is non-multiple.

Proof.

This is essentially
[
Bar+04
, Proposition III.9.1]
and
[
HO11
, Proposition 3.1]
. (1) The statement is local on
X
, so do the local computation. (2) The total space
Y
is regular and
K_{Y}
is numerically
g
-trivial, so
g
is a minimal abelian fibration. It is
\delta
-regular by
Corollary

6.1.5
. The central fiber
Y_{t}
has multiplicity
1
by
Lemma

6.1.10
: the normalization clears up its multiplicity.
∎

This simplifies the diagram (
6.1.8
) with
\bar{X}=X
, so the results in §
6.1
apply directly to
X
:

7.1.3
.

(1)

The inertia
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
Y_{t}
is free of order
m
.

(2)

The quotient
\bar{X}=Y/\varphi
in (
6.1.8
) coincides with
X
. ∎

As in §
6.1
, define a closed subscheme
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}\subset X_{s}\subset X
either as a quotient
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}=Y_{t}/\varphi
or as a scheme associated to the Cartier divisor (
Lemma

6.1.10
):

\tfrac{1}{m}X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\tfrac{a_{i}}{m}\cdot E_{i}.

This constructs a sequence of morphisms

(7.1.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tfrac{1}{m}X_{s}}

where the former is a free

-quotient and the latter is an infinitesimal thickening. The following is simply

–

, but we state it again more explicitly for clarity.

7.1.5
.

The pullback of t-automorphisms
p^{*}:P_{s}\longrightarrow Q_{t}
is one of the following. Throughout,
A_{s}
and
B_{t}
are abelian varieties of dimension
n-1
.

(i)

An isomorphism between abelian varieties of dimension
n
.

(ii)

A homomorphism
P_{s}=\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times A_{s}\hookrightarrow Q_{t}
, where
Q_{t}
is an abelian variety of dimension
n
.

(iii)

An open immersion such that:

•

p^{*}:P_{s}^{\circ}\longrightarrow Q_{t}^{\circ}
is an isomorphism between a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-extension of
A_{s}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-extension of
B_{t}
.

•

\coker p^{*}=\mathbb{Z}/m
.

(iv)

A homomorphism
P_{s}=\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times A_{s}\hookrightarrow Q_{t}
, where
Q^{\circ}_{t}
is a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-extension of
B_{t}
and
A_{s}\hookrightarrow Q^{\circ}_{t}\twoheadrightarrow B_{t}
is an isogeny.

(v)

A homomorphism whose induced map on neutral components is
\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}\times B_{t}
, where
\mathbb{G}_{a}\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}
is either an isomorphism or
0
, and
A_{s}\longrightarrow B_{t}
is an isomorphism. ∎

.

Since
h^{0}(\frac{1}{m}X_{s},\mathcal{O})\leq h^{0}(Y_{t},\mathcal{O})=1
, we can apply
Theorem

A.2.2
–
A.2.3
. Note that we can say more about the pullback
i^{*}:\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\frac{1}{m}X_{s}}
in this case: when
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
has a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, then it splits as
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times\Alb_{s}^{\vee}
by
Proposition

4.1.6
. This implies that
i^{*}
induces an inclusion
\Alb_{s}^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\frac{1}{m}X_{s}}
whose cokernel is a unipotent group by
Lemma

A.1.8
.
∎

We end this subsection with a variant of
Lemma

4.2.1
for multiple fibers. This will be later used in
Theorem

7.3.2
and
7.4.1
to descend the untangle of
Y
to
X
. The statement is unfortunately a bit technical.

7.1.8
Improvement of
Lemma

4.2.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with
P_{s}
having a linear part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. Assume that

•

The Albanese morphism
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
in
Proposition

7.1.7
is a free
\varphi
-quotient of the Albanese morphism
Y_{t}\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}
.

Let
u:A\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
be an isogeny of abelian torsors such that
A\times_{\Alb_{s}}\Alb_{t}
is connected. Then the conclusions of
Lemma

4.2.1
hold for
u
.

When
f
is non-multiple, then the bullet assumption in the lemma is satisfied (because
m=1
and
Y=X
) and
u
can be arbitrary. Hence
Lemma

7.1.8
recovers its original version when
P_{s}
has a liner part
\mathbb{G}_{a}
. When
f
is multiple, the assumptions in the lemma will be verified for the cases (iv) and (v) of
Lemma

7.1.5
(see
Lemma

7.3.18
and
7.4.5
).

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where

is the pullback constructed in (

),

and

are pullbacks by (

), and

is the isogeny constructed in

. Moreover, the following holds.

and

.

\ker i^{*}=0
or
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, and
\ker\kappa\subset\mathbb{Z}/m
.

It is commutative over
T_{0}
and hence over

, and the equality on the right is from

2.4.3
. Restricting this diagram over

and decomposing

into

constructs the desired commutative diagram. To show (1), notice that
\ker q^{*}\subset\mathbb{Z}/m
is finite by
Lemma

A.1.7
,
\ker\kappa
is finite by

2.4.3
,
\ker i^{*}
is unipotent by

(in fact, it is either

or
\mathbb{G}_{a}
), and finally
\ker p^{*}
is unipotent by

. These force the conclusions in (1) to hold. Now (2) follows from
\ker\kappa=\ker q^{*}\subset\mathbb{Z}/m
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

7.1.8
.

Recall from the proof of
Lemma

4.2.1
that the isogeny
u:A\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
(with Galois group
G
) defines a subgroup
\hat{G}\subset\Alb_{s}^{\vee}
. The Albanese morphism
\alb:X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
defined an injection
\alb^{*}:\Alb_{s}^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
by
Theorem

A.2.2
. As pointed out in
Remark

4.2.2
, the only part in the original lemma where the non-multiple fiber assumption was used was to show
\alb^{*}(\hat{G})\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
is nontrivial over
S_{0}
. Recall from
Proposition

2.4.3
that
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
has a separated quotient
\kappa:\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow\check{P}^{\circ}
. A section of
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
that is trivial over
S_{0}
passes through the torsion points
\ker(\kappa:\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}\longrightarrow\check{P}^{\circ}_{s})
of the central fiber
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
. Therefore, it is enough to show that
\alb^{*}(\hat{G})\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
has a trivial intersection with
\ker\kappa\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
.

Take the diagram in
Lemma

7.1.9
. The claim
\alb^{*}(\hat{G})\cap\ker\kappa=0
in
\Pic^{\circ}_{X_{s}}
is equivalent to
\alb^{*}(\hat{G})\cap\ker q^{*}=0
in
\Pic^{\circ}_{\frac{1}{m}X_{s}}
by
Proposition

7.1.7
and
Lemma

7.1.9
. The given bullet assumption is equivalent to a cartesian diagram

so we have
\ker q^{*}=\ker r^{*}
and again
\alb^{*}(\hat{G})\cap\ker q^{*}=0
is equivalent to
\hat{G}\cap\ker r^{*}=0
in
\Alb_{s}
. Since
\hat{G}
is associated to
u:A\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
and
\ker r^{*}
is associated to
\Alb_{t}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
, this is equivalent to saying that the fiber product
A\times_{\Alb_{s}}\Alb_{t}
is connected (abelian torsor), which we are assuming.
∎

7.2.
Base change to smooth fibrations

The new minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
g:Y\longrightarrow T
may be smooth, strictly semistable, or unstable. We will analyze them separately throughout the remaining sections from now on.

The first case is when
g
is smooth, or the case (i) and (ii) in
Lemma

7.1.5
and
Table

6
. In this case, the central fiber
Y_{t}
is an abelian torsor of dimension
n
by
Lemma

2.3.4
.

7.2.1
Type
\mathrm{I}_{0}
.

In
Lemma

7.1.5
(i),
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
is an abelian torsor of dimension
n
.

7.2.2
Type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{+}
.

In
Lemma

7.1.5
(ii), there exist a unique positive integer
d
dividing
m
with the following properties.

(1)

d=2
,
3
,
4
, or
6
. Moreover,

(a)

d=2
if and only if
P
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}/2
, or
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}/4
.

(b)

d=6
if and only if
j(E)=0
and
P
has type
\mathrm{II}
or
\mathrm{II}^{*}
.

(c)

d=4
if and only if
j(E)=1728
and
P
has type
\mathrm{III}
,
\mathrm{III}/2
,
\mathrm{III}^{*}
, or
\mathrm{III}^{*}/2
.

(d)

d=3
if and only if
j(E)=0
and
P
has type
\mathrm{IV}
,
\mathrm{IV}/3
,
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}/3
.

(2)

\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
is isomorphic to
(E\times A_{s})/(\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle\times G)
, where
E
is an elliptic curve,
G
is the Albanese stabilizer of
P
described in
Proposition

6.2.2
(2), and

\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y+a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in E\times A_{s}.

Here
\omega\in\Aut_{0}(E)
is a group automorphism of order
d
, and
a\in A_{s}
is a torsion point of order
m

7.2.3
.

The
Kodaira type
of the above cases is the indicated type multiplied by
m
.

Proof of
Theorem

7.2.1
.

Since
p^{*}:P_{s}\longrightarrow Q_{t}
is an isomorphism of abelian varieties,
Lemma

2.3.4
already concludes the theorem. The following is a stronger version. By
Lemma

6.1.11
, the
P_{s}
-action on
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}=X_{s,\red}
is induced from the
Q_{t}
-action on
Y_{t}
. Hence
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
admits a transitive
P_{s}
-action, proving it is an abelian torsor. The
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
Y_{t}
is by translations and
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}=Y_{t}/\varphi
.
∎

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of
Theorem

7.2.2
.

Since
P
is unstable whose semistable base change
Q
is smooth,
P
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
\mathrm{II}
,
\cdots
,
\mathrm{IV}^{*}/3
by
Corollary

6.2.4
. Let us make this more precise. Let
f^{\sharp}:X^{\sharp}\longrightarrow S
be a minimal compactification of
P
and consider its Albanese stabilizer
G
. Shrinking
S
, we may assume that
g:Y\longrightarrow T
has a section. Then both
Y_{0}
and
X^{\sharp}_{T_{0}}
are isomorphic to
Q_{0}\longrightarrow T_{0}
. Hence
Y
and
X^{\sharp}_{T}
are birational, or
Y
is the (unique) minimal model of
X^{\sharp}_{T}
. By
Proposition

6.2.2
, the untangle
\tilde{X}^{\sharp}\longrightarrow X^{\sharp}
pulls back to a
G
-covering
\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow Y
with a central fiber
\tilde{Y}_{t}=E\times A_{s}
for an elliptic curve
E
. We have a diagram

(7.2.4)

Moreover,
Y_{t}=\tilde{Y}_{t}/G=(E\times A_{s})/G
is a free quotient by
G\hookrightarrow\tilde{Q}_{t}=E\times A_{s}
where
\tau:G\hookrightarrow E
and
\sigma:G\hookrightarrow A_{s}
are both injective. The
\mu_{m}=\langle\tilde{\psi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Q}_{t}=E\times A_{s}
is of the form
\tilde{\psi}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)
for
(x,y)\in E\times A_{s}
and
\omega\in\Aut_{0}(E)
a primitive
d
-th root of unity for
d=2,3,4,
or
6
.

Note that
\tilde{Y}
admits an inertia automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}^{\sharp}
as a semistable base change of
\tilde{X}^{\sharp}
. However, it is a priori unclear whether there exists an inertia automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
on
\tilde{Y}
lifting the inertia automorphism
\varphi
on
Y
. An equivalent statement is to fill in the topright corner of (
7.2.4
) by a new space
\tilde{X}
. It turns out this is always possible in the current case (ii), but not in case (iv) and (v) (
Lemma

7.3.20
and
7.4.9
).

7.2.5
.

Shrinking
S
étale locally, there exists a Galois étale morphism
\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
completing the topright corner of (
7.2.4
) to a cartesian diagram

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

In particular, there exists a
\mu_{m}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Y}
lifting
\varphi
on
Y
and commuting with
G
.

Proof.

Let us first lift
\varphi
to an automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\tilde{Y}_{t}
. Recall from
Proposition

6.2.1
–
6.2.2
we have an isomorphism
\tilde{Q}_{t}=\tilde{Y}_{t}=E\times A_{s}
, and automorphisms

\tilde{\psi}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)\mbox{ for }(x,y)\in\tilde{Q}_{t},\quad g(x,y)=(x+\tau(g),y+\sigma(g))\mbox{ for }(x,y)\in\tilde{Y}_{t}.

Here
\tau:G\hookrightarrow E^{\omega}
and
\sigma:G\hookrightarrow A_{s}
are injective homomorphisms. Hence we have a description of
\psi
on
Q_{t}=\tilde{Q}_{t}/G=(E\times A_{s})/G
. Now
\varphi
on
Y_{t}
is described by

\varphi(x,y)=\psi(x,y)+\varphi(0,0)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in Y_{t}=(E\times A_{s})/G.

Writing
\varphi(0,0)=(c,a)\in(E\times A_{s})/G
, we have
\varphi(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x+c,y+a)
. We may adjust the origin of the abelian torsor
Y_{t}
and assume
c=0
. This uniquely determines
a\in A_{s}
. Now we can lift
\varphi
to an automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y+a)
on
\tilde{Y}_{t}
. It commutes with
G
because
\tau(g)
is
\omega
-fixed.

Lifting the automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\tilde{Y}_{t}
to that of
\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow T
is formal. Recall from
Proposition

6.1.6
that the
\varphi
is modeled as an isomorphism
\Phi:Y\longrightarrow\zeta^{*}Y
of abelian torsors over
T
. Lift it to a unique isomorphism
\tilde{\Phi}:\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow\zeta^{*}\tilde{Y}
of abelian torsors using
Lemma

A.1.3
. This constructs an equivariant automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
on
\tilde{Y}
with condition
Proposition

6.1.6
(2). The
\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
- and
G
-actions commute because they commuted on
\tilde{Y}_{t}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.2.2
.

Lemma

7.2.5
showed that
\tilde{X}_{s}=\tilde{Y}_{t}/\tilde{\varphi}=(E\times A_{s})/\tilde{\varphi}
for
\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y+a)
. The original
X_{s}
is computed by a further quotient
\tilde{X}_{s}/G
. All statements follow immediately.
∎

7.3.
Base change to semistable fibrations

Let us study the case when
g:Y\longrightarrow T
is strictly semistable, or the cases (iii) and (iv) in
Lemma

7.1.5
.

7.3.1
Type
\mathrm{I}^{k}_{R}
.

In
Lemma

7.1.5
(iii),
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
is isomorphic to either a type
\mathrm{I}_{1}
semistable fiber in
Theorem

5.1.1
(
k=R=1
), or a cycle of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
p_{i}:E_{i}\longrightarrow A
with two sections
0_{i}
and
\infty_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,R\,(\geq 2)
that are successively identified by
0_{i+1}=\infty_{i}
. Moreover, the following holds.

(1)

k\mid m
and
k\mid R
. Write
m=kl
and
R=kr
for positive integers
l
and
r
.

(2)

The t-automorphism scheme
P
of
f
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
.

(3)

There exists a free
\mu_{k}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-equivariant
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
p:F\longrightarrow A_{s}
such that
p_{i}:E_{i}\longrightarrow A
is isomorphic to its free quotient by an automorphism
\zeta^{i}\circ\varphi
, where
\zeta
is an automorphism on
F
acting on every fiber of
p
by multiplication by a primitive
k
-th root of unity.

(4)

p_{i}
and
p_{j}
are isomorphic (preserving
0
and
\infty
) if and only if
i\equiv j\mod k
. Moreover,
A=A_{s}/a
for a torsion point
a\in A_{s}
of order
k
.

7.3.2
.

In
Lemma

7.1.5
(iv), the following holds.

(1)

2\mid m\mid 2R
. Write
m=2k
and
R=kr
for positive integers
k
and
r
.

(2)

The t-automorphism scheme
P
of
f
has type
\mathrm{I}^{*}_{r}
,
\mathrm{I}^{*}_{r}/2
, or
\mathrm{I}^{*}_{r}/4
.

(3)

\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
is isomorphic to
(C\times A_{s})/(\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle\times G)
, where
C
is a type
\mathrm{I}_{2kr}
Kodaira singular curve and the
\tilde{\varphi}
and
G
are given as one of the following.

(
\mathrm{I}^{+}_{R}
)

G
is the Albanese stabilizer of
P
and
\tilde{\varphi}
is an order
2k
automorphism (using the notation in
Proposition

6.2.1
(2))

\tilde{\varphi}(i,z,y)=(-i,\tfrac{\zeta^{i}}{z},y+a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z)\in C,\quad y\in A_{s}.

Here
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a a primitive
2k
-th root of unity and
a\in A_{s}
is a torsion point of order
2k
.

(
\mathrm{I}^{-}_{R}
)

G=0
or
\mathbb{Z}/2
, and
\tilde{\varphi}
is an order
4k
automorphism

\tilde{\varphi}(i,z,y)=(1-i,\tfrac{\varepsilon\zeta^{i}}{z},y+a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z)\in C,\quad y\in A_{s}.

Here
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a constant,
\zeta
is a primitive
2k
-th root, and
a\in A_{s}
has order
4k
.

7.3.3
.

The
Kodaira type
of
Theorem

7.3.1
is
m\cdot\mathrm{I}^{k}_{R}
. The Kodaira type of
Theorem

7.3.2
(a) is
m\cdot\mathrm{I}^{+}_{R}/|G|
where
|G|=1
,
2
, or
4
is the order of the Albanese stabilizer
G
. The Kodaira type of
Theorem

7.3.2
(b) is
m\cdot\mathrm{I}^{-}_{R}/|G|
for
|G|=1
or
2
.

7.3.4
.

In
Theorem

7.3.1
(4),
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
may be isomorphic even when
i\not\equiv j\mod k
, but this isomorphism cannot preserve
0
and
\infty
. In
Theorem

7.3.2
, the quotient
(C\times A_{s})/(\langle\tilde{\varphi}^{2}\rangle\times G)
has type
\mathrm{I}^{k}_{R}
for
R=kr
or
2kr
. Hence
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
is a
\mu_{2}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-quotient of a (very special) type
\mathrm{I}^{k}_{R}
fiber. This is the description used in
Theorem

1.0.1
.

The rest of this subsection proves these two theorems.

In both (iii) and (iv),
g
is strictly semistable and
Q^{\circ}_{t}
is a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-extension of an abelian variety
B_{t}
of dimension
n-1
. In particular, the central fiber
Y_{t}
is reduced and hence its quotient
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
is reduced, too. Note that
Y_{t}=\sum_{i}F_{i}
is a cycle of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles over
B_{t}
, so we may consider the
orientation
of this cycle, a preferred direction of the cycle of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibers.

7.3.5
.

The following properties characterize the two cases.

(iii)

\psi(z)=z
for
z\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset Q_{t}
, and
\varphi
preserves the orientation of
Y_{t}
.

(iv)

\psi(z)=1/z
for
z\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset Q_{t}
, and
\varphi
reverses the orientation of
Y_{t}
.

Proof.

The inertia group automorphism
\psi:Q^{\circ}_{t}\longrightarrow Q^{\circ}_{t}
induces that on the linear part
\psi:\mathbb{G}_{m}\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}
, which is either
\psi(z)=z
or
1/z
. By
Theorem

6.1.4
, case (iii) is when
\psi(z)=z
and the case (iv) is when
\psi(z)=1/z
. Recall from
Proposition

6.1.6
the equality
\varphi(z\cdot y)=\psi(z)\cdot\varphi(y)
for
z\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
and
y\in Y_{t}
. Say
\varphi
sends a component
F_{i}
to
F_{j}
. If
\psi(z)=z
, then we have
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}(z\cdot y)=0_{i}
and
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\varphi(z\cdot y)=\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}z\cdot\varphi(y)=0_{j}
. Hence
\varphi
preserves the orientation of the cycle. Similarly, if
\psi(z)=1/z
then
\varphi
reverses the orientation.
∎

This observation already relates case (iv) to (iii).

7.3.6
.

In case (iv),
m
is even and (
7.1.1
) admits a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{m/2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}}

with the following properties:

(1)

The two horizontal arrows in the first row are étale.

(2)

h:Z\longrightarrow U
is a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with multiplicity
m/2
.

(3)

The left commutative diagram falls into the case
Lemma

7.1.5
(iii).

Proof.

In case (iv),
\ord\psi
is even and hence
m
is even. The quotient
Z=Y/\varphi^{2}
constructs the diagram. The middle morphism
h
is minimal because
Z
is regular. To show the left diagram is case (iii), notice that its Galois group is
\mu_{m/2}=\langle\varphi^{2}\rangle
and
\psi^{2}(z)=z
for
z\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset Q_{t}
.
∎

7.3.1.
Case (iii)

Let us first study the case (iii). By
Lemma

7.1.5
(iii),
P_{s}^{\circ}=Q_{t}^{\circ}
so let us write
B_{t}=A_{s}
for notational simplicity. Assume that
P_{s}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
for a positive integer
r
and hence
Q_{t}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{mr}
(because
\coker p^{*}=\mathbb{Z}/m
). The central fiber
Y_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{mr}F_{i}
has Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{mr}
, too. Since
\varphi
preserves the orientation of
Y_{t}
by
Lemma

7.3.5
, there exists a positive integer
R\mid mr
(say
mr=lR
) such that
\varphi(F_{i})=F_{i+R}
for every
1\leq i\leq mr=lR
. In particular,
\varphi^{l}
preserves every component
F_{i}
and
m=\ord\varphi
is divisible by
l
. We may thus again set
m=kl
for a positive integer
k
and yield
R=kr
. In summary, we have

(7.3.7)

Y_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{klr}F_{j},\quad\varphi(F_{j})=F_{j+kr},\quad R=kr,\quad\mbox{and}\quad m=kl.

7.3.8
.

In case (iii), (
7.1.1
) admits a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{l}}

with the following properties:

(1)

The two horizontal arrows in the first row are étale.

(2)

f^{+}:X^{+}\longrightarrow S^{+}
is a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with multiplicity
k
. Moreover, the central fiber
\frac{1}{k}X_{s}^{+}=\sum_{j=1}^{klr}E_{j}^{+}
satisfies the following.

(a)

The inertia
\mu_{k}=\langle\varphi^{l}\rangle
-action on
Y_{t}
preserves every component
F_{j}
. In particular,
E_{j}^{+}
is a free
\mu_{k}
-quotient of
F_{i}
for every
i
.

(b)

The inertia
\mu_{l}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
X_{s}^{+}
induces an isomorphism
\varphi:E_{j}^{+}\longrightarrow E_{j+kr}^{+}
for every
i
.

Proof.

The new fibration
f^{+}
is obtained by a
\mu_{k}=\langle\varphi^{l}\rangle
-quotient
X^{+}=Y/\varphi^{l}
. Note that
\varphi^{l}
preserves every irreducible component
F_{i}
.
∎

7.3.9
.

\frac{1}{m}X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{kr}E_{i}
has
kr
number of irreducible components. Moreover, we have an isomorphism
E_{j}^{+}\cong E_{j\mod kr}
for
j=1,\cdots,klr
. ∎

To simplify the numerics, we will replace
X
with
X^{+}
and assume
l=1
in (
7.3.7
). That is, from now on we assume
l=1
,
m=k
,
Y_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{kr}F_{i}
, and that the inertia
\mu_{k}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
Y_{t}
preserves every
F_{i}
, so that
E_{i}=F_{i}/\varphi
. It remains to describe the inertia automorphism
\varphi
(and
\psi
). Take a normalization

\tilde{Y}_{t}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{kr}\tilde{F}_{i}\qquad\mbox{for isomorphic copies of }\mathbb{P}^{1}\mbox{-bundles }\tilde{p}_{i}:\tilde{F}_{i}\longrightarrow B_{t}.

When
r\geq 2
, note that
F_{i}=\tilde{F}_{i}
is already smooth.

7.3.10
.

The
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
\tilde{F}_{i}
descends to an action on
B_{t}
via
\tilde{p}_{i}
. Moreover, the action is a translation by a torsion point
a\in A_{s}
of order
k
.

Proof.

Since the
Q_{t}^{\circ}
- and
\langle\varphi\rangle
-actions on
\tilde{F}_{i}
commute, they descend to commuting actions on the base
B_{t}
by the universal property of Albanese morphism. An action commuting with every translation is a translation, so the
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
A_{s}
is a translation by a torsion point
a_{i}\in A_{s}
. If
\varphi^{i}
for
i<k
acts as the identity on
A_{s}
, then it acts on the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
fibers of
\tilde{p}_{i}
and hence necessarily has a fixed point on
\tilde{F}_{i}
, a contradiction. Hence
a_{i}
has an order
k
.

To show all
a_{i}
are the same, note that the two sections
0_{i}
and
\infty_{i}
of
\tilde{p}_{i}
are preserved by
\varphi
(
Lemma

7.3.5
). Since
\tilde{p}_{i}
is equivariant,
\varphi
acts on them as a translation by
a_{i}
. The identification
\infty_{i}=0_{i+1}
shows
a_{i}=a_{i+1}
.
∎

7.3.11
.

(1)

The
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
\Sing(Y_{t})=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{kr}(F_{i}\cap F_{i+1})
is a translation by
a\in A_{s}
on every component.

(2)

\tilde{E}_{i}\longrightarrow A_{s}/a
is a free
\varphi
-quotient of
\tilde{F}_{i}\longrightarrow A_{s}
. In particular, it is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle.∎

7.3.12
.

There exists a primitive
k
-th root of unity
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset Q_{t}^{\circ}
such that the inertia
\langle\psi\rangle
-action on
Q_{t}
is

\psi(f_{i})=\zeta^{i}\cdot f_{i}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad f_{i}\in Q_{t}\mbox{ with }\pi_{0}(f_{i})=i\ \in\ \pi_{0}(Q_{t})=\mathbb{Z}/kr.

Proof.

We may write
Q_{t}=\mathbb{Z}/kr\times Q_{t}^{\circ}
by
Lemma

A.1.2
, and write its element by
(i,g)\in\mathbb{Z}/kr\times Q_{t}^{\circ}
. Recall the equality
Q_{t}^{\psi}=P_{s}
and
\pi_{0}(P_{s})=k\cdot\mathbb{Z}/kr\subset\mathbb{Z}/kr=\pi_{0}(Q_{t})
, so we already have
\psi(i,g)=(i,g)
when
k\mid i
. Recall the equality
\varphi((i,g)\cdot y)=\psi(i,g)\cdot\varphi(y)
of
Proposition

6.1.6
. Since
\varphi
preserves all
F_{i}
by assumption and
\pi_{0}(Q_{t})=\mathbb{Z}/kr
cycles the components
F_{i}
(
Corollary

5.3.9
), this shows that
\psi
is of the form

\psi(i,g)=(i,\psi_{i}(g))\qquad\mbox{for}\quad\psi_{i}:Q_{t}^{\circ}\longrightarrow Q_{t}^{\circ}.

Since
\psi
acted trivially on
Q_{t}^{\circ}
, we have
\psi(i,g)=\psi(0,g)\cdot\psi(i,1)=(0,g)\cdot(i,\psi_{i}(1))=(i,\psi_{i}(1)\cdot g)
. This shows
\psi_{i}(g)=\zeta_{i}\cdot g
for
\zeta_{i}=\psi_{i}(1)\in Q_{t}^{\circ}
. The equality
\psi(i,g^{i})=\psi(1,g)^{i}
shows
\zeta_{i}=\zeta^{i}
for a single
\zeta\in Q_{t}^{\circ}
. Furthermore,
\zeta
is
k
-torsion because
\psi^{k}=\id
. Again
Q_{t}^{\psi}
is precisely
P_{s}
, so this implies that
\zeta
has order precisely
k
.

It remains to prove that
\zeta\in Q_{t}^{\circ}
is contained in
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Take a point
y\in F_{1}\cap F_{kr}
, a unique rational curve
R\subset F_{1}
through
y
, and a unique point
y^{\prime}=R\cap F_{2}\subset F_{1}\cap F_{2}
. Recall from
Lemma

5.1.7
that
Q_{t}
acts on
\Sing(Y_{t})
transitively by cycling the components (with stabilizer
\mathbb{G}_{m}
). Hence there exists
f_{1}\in Q_{t}
such that
\pi_{0}(f_{1})=1
and
y^{\prime}=f_{1}\cdot y
.

Now apply
\varphi
to
y,y^{\prime}\in R\subset F_{1}
to yield two points on a rational curve
\varphi(y),\varphi(y^{\prime})\in\varphi(R)\subset F_{1}
. By
Corollary

7.3.11
, if we fix any
f_{0}\in Q_{t}^{\circ}
whose image under
Q_{t}^{\circ}\longrightarrow A_{s}
is
a
, then
\varphi(y)=f_{0}\cdot y
and
\varphi(y^{\prime})=f_{0}\cdot y^{\prime}
. We now have a sequence of identities

\displaystyle\varphi(y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\varphi(f_{1}\cdot y)=\psi(f_{1})\cdot\varphi(y)=(\zeta\cdot f_{1})\cdot(f_{0}\cdot y)

\displaystyle=\zeta\cdot(f_{0}\cdot(f_{1}\cdot y))=\zeta\cdot(f_{0}\cdot y^{\prime})=\zeta\cdot\varphi(y^{\prime}).

But
\varphi(y^{\prime})\in F_{1}\cap F_{2}
has a stabilizer
\mathbb{G}_{m}
again by
Lemma

5.1.7
. This means
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset Q_{t}^{\circ}
.
∎

The (normalized) irreducible component
\tilde{F}=\tilde{F}_{i}\longrightarrow A_{s}
is a compactification of the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-torsor
Q_{t}^{\circ}\longrightarrow A_{s}
, or equivalently a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle with two sections
0
and
\infty
. Hence
\tilde{F}\setminus\infty\longrightarrow A_{s}
can be considered as a line bundle with the zero section
0
. In this sense, we call
\tilde{F}=(\tilde{F},0,\infty)\longrightarrow A_{s}
a line bundle.

7.3.13
.

There exists an isomorphism
f:\tilde{F}_{i}\longrightarrow\tilde{F}_{j}
of line bundles (i.e.,
f(0_{i})=0_{j}
and
f(\infty_{i})=\infty_{j}
) such that the
\langle\varphi\rangle
-actions on
\tilde{F}_{i}
and
\tilde{F}_{j}
are related via

\varphi(f(y))=\zeta^{j-i}\cdot f(\varphi(y)).

Proof.

Take any
f=f_{j-i}\in Q_{t}
with
\pi_{0}(f)=j-i\in\pi_{0}(Q_{t})=\mathbb{Z}/kr
. Then it induces an orientation-preserving isomorphism
f:F_{i}\longrightarrow F_{j}
. The claim is the equality

\varphi(f\cdot y)=\psi(f)\cdot\varphi(y)=\zeta^{j-i}\cdot(f\cdot\varphi(y)).\qed

7.3.14
.

\tilde{E}_{i}=\tilde{F}_{i}/\varphi
and
\tilde{E}_{j}=\tilde{F}_{j}/\varphi
are isomorphic as line bundles over
A=A_{s}/a
if and only if
i\equiv j\mod k
.

Proof.

Write
\tilde{F}=\tilde{F}_{i}
for simplicity.
Lemma

7.3.10
constructed a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.69649pt}{-8.5847pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

or equivalently an isomorphism
\tilde{F}\longrightarrow t_{a}^{*}\tilde{F}
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles over
A_{s}
(with a cocycle condition). Such an isomorphism respects
0
and
\infty
, so it is an isomorphism of line bundles. That is, the
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
\tilde{F}
defines a
\mathbb{Z}/m
-equivariant line bundle structure on
\tilde{F}\setminus\infty\longrightarrow A_{s}
.

Consider a left exact sequence (e.g.,
[
Dol03
, §7.1]
)

0\longrightarrow\Hom(\mathbb{Z}/k,\mathbb{G}_{m})=\mu_{k}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\mathbb{Z}/k}(A_{s})\longrightarrow\Pic(A_{s}).

For each
i
, the datum
(\tilde{F}_{i},\varphi)
in
\Pic^{\mathbb{Z}/k}(A_{s})
has the same image in
\Pic(A_{s})
. The previous lemma says that
(\tilde{F}_{i},\varphi)
and
(\tilde{F}_{j},\varphi)
differ by
\zeta^{j-i}\in\mu_{k}
. This shows these
\mathbb{Z}/k
-equivariant structures are isomorphic if and only if
i\equiv j\mod k
. In other words, the quotients
\tilde{E}_{i}=\tilde{F}_{i}/\varphi
and
E_{j}=\tilde{F}_{j}/\varphi
are isomorphic as line bundles over
A=A_{s}/a
if and only if
i\equiv j\mod k
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.3.1
.

We have seen in general that
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{kr}E_{i}
consists of
R=kr
number of irreducible components. Thanks to
Lemma

A.3.5
–
A.3.6
, we may consider
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
as an identification of
\tilde{E}_{i}\longrightarrow A
’s by
\infty_{i}=0_{i+1}
. Note that
\tilde{E}_{i}\longrightarrow A
isomorphic to
\tilde{E}_{i}^{+}\longrightarrow A
for
i=1,\cdots,kr
, and
\tilde{E}_{i}^{+}\longrightarrow A
is a free
\mu_{k}
-quotient of
\tilde{F}_{i}\longrightarrow A_{s}
by the inertia action
\varphi
, which differs in cycle
k
.
∎

7.3.2.
Case (iv)

Let us now study the case (iv). In
Lemma

7.3.6
, we have factorized the original diagram (
7.1.1
) into
T\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow S
of degrees
m/2
and
2
. Since
h:Z\longrightarrow U
is a semistable-like multiple fibration, it has type
\frac{m}{2}\cdot\mathrm{I}^{k}_{R}
for
k\mid\frac{m}{2}
and
k\mid R
by
Theorem

7.3.1
. Letting
\frac{m}{2}=kl
,
g:Y\longrightarrow T
is semistable of type
\mathrm{I}_{Rl}
. In fact,
l
is trivial in this case.

7.3.15
.

l=1
, or equivalently
m=2k
.

Proof.

Consider the
\mu_{m}=\mu_{2kl}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
Y
. By
Lemma

7.3.5
,
\varphi
reserves the orientation of the cycle
Y_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{Rl}F_{i}
. Up to reindexing
F_{i}
, we may assume
\varphi(F_{i})=F_{-i}
or
F_{1-i}
. Note that
\varphi^{2}
preserves all
F_{i}
in either case. But recall from (
7.3.7
) that
\mu_{m/2}=\mu_{kl}=\langle\varphi^{2}\rangle
-action on
Y_{t}
is of the form
\varphi^{2}(F_{i})=F_{i+R}
. This shows
l=1
.
∎

Therefore, we have a factorization

\hbox to104.85pt{\vbox to37.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.4242pt\lower-18.65274pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

where
g
is semistable of type
\mathrm{I}_{R}
and
h
is semistable-like multiple of type
k\cdot\mathrm{I}^{k}_{R}
.

7.3.16
.

P\longrightarrow S
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
,
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/2
, or
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/4
for a positive integer
r
.

Proof.

The t-automorphism scheme
P
is unstable, and the degree
2
morphism
U\longrightarrow S
reduces it to semistable t-automorphism scheme of
h
. Apply
Proposition

6.2.1
–
6.2.2
to the minimal compactification
f^{\sharp}:X^{\sharp}\longrightarrow S
of
P
.
∎

By
Theorem

5.2.1
and
5.3.2
,
P
admits an untangle to a type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
group

0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\tilde{P}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0,

where
G\subset\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})=(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
for even
r
and
\mathbb{Z}/4
for odd
r
. One similarly tries to
untangle

X
into a
G
-covering
\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
as in
Proposition

4.2.4
or
Lemma

7.2.5
. This is not always possible, so we need to study this carefully.

Let
G
be the Albanese stabilizer of
P
. A similar process to (
7.2.4
) constructs a
G
-covering
\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow Y
with a central fiber
\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}
for a Kodaira singular curve
C
of type
\mathrm{I}_{2kr}
:

(7.3.17)

\hbox to104.47pt{\vbox to37.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.23602pt\lower-18.8472pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-52.23602pt}{-15.18747pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{

{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We want to check whether
\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow Y
descends to
\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
. To do so, recall the homomorphism
p^{*}:P_{s}^{\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}\twoheadrightarrow A_{s}\subset Q_{t}^{\circ}
produces an
(n-1)
-dimensional abelian subvariety of
Q_{t}^{\circ}
. This means that
Y_{t}
has an
(n-1)
-dimensional Albanese torsor by
Corollary

4.1.8
:

\alb:Y_{t}\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}=\Alb_{Y_{t}}.

7.3.18
.

The Albanese morphism
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
is a free
\varphi
-quotient of the Albanese morphism
Y_{t}\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}
.

Proof.

Similar to
Lemma

7.3.10
, the
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action descends to a translation action on
\Alb_{t}
. Suppose that
\varphi^{i}
for
i<m=2k
acts trivially on
\Alb_{t}
. Then it acts on the fibers
C
of
Y_{t}\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}
. But every action on a cycle of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s reversing the orientation has a fixed point, so this contradicts the fact that the
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
Y_{t}
is free. Now the free quotient

\alpha:\tfrac{1}{2k}X_{s}=Y_{t}/\varphi\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}/\varphi

is an
A_{s}
-equivariant
C
-fiber bundle. The Albanese torsor of
\frac{1}{2k}X_{s}
is
\Alb_{s}
(
Proposition

7.1.7
), so
\alpha
factors through
\frac{1}{2k}X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}/\varphi
. The latter morphism
\Alb_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}/\varphi
is an isogeny of abelian torsors and hence it is finite. But
\alpha
already has connected fibers
C
, so
\Alb_{s}=\Alb_{t}/\varphi
and
\alpha
is the Albanese morphism.
∎

Combine
Proposition

6.2.2
(3) with
Lemma

7.3.18
to form a cartesian diagram

\hbox to154.61pt{\vbox to40.42pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 77.3056pt\lower-20.2083pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-77.3056pt}{-15.04857pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

The composition
A_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
is an isogeny of abelian torsors, so it is automatically Galois. Its Galois group
H
sits in a short exact sequence

(7.3.19)

0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow\mu_{2k}\longrightarrow 0.

The following answers the question whether the
G
-covering of
X
exists.

7.3.20
.

The sequence (
7.3.19
) splits if and only if there exists a Galois étale morphism
\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
that completes (
7.3.17
) into a cartesian diagram

In this case, the
\mu_{2k}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
Y
lifts to a
\mu_{2k}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Y}
commuting with
G
.

Proof.

If
H\cong G\times\mu_{2k}
, then take a quotient by
\mu_{2k}
first to obtain a cartesian diagram of abelian torsors (where
a\in A_{s}
has order
2k
)

Hence we can apply
Lemma

7.1.8
(together with
Lemma

7.3.18
) to the isogeny
u:A_{s}/a\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
and construct
\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X
as claimed. Conversely, if we can fill in the topright corner of (
7.4.6
), then the inertia
\mu_{2k}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Y}
satisfies
\mu_{2k}\cap G=0
and is a lifting of
\varphi
. That is, this induces a splitting
H\cong G\times\mu_{2k}
.
∎

7.3.21
.

Assume the sequence (
7.3.19
) splits. Then the
\mu_{2k}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
Y_{t}=C\times A_{s}
for a type
\mathrm{I}_{2kr}
Kodaira curve
C
is

\tilde{\varphi}(i,z,y)=(-i,\tfrac{\zeta^{i}}{z},y+a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z)\in C,\quad y\in A_{s},

where
\zeta
is a primitive
2k
-th root of unity and
a\in A_{s}
is a torsion point of order
2k
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

A.1.6
,
\tilde{\varphi}
acts (freely and) diagonally on
\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}
. Hence it acts on
A_{s}
by a translation by a torsion point
a\in A_{s}
of order
2k
, and acts on the curve
C
by reversing the orientation.

Shrinking
S
, the torsion point
a\in A_{s}\subset\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A_{s}
extends to a torsion section
a:T\longrightarrow\tilde{Q}
of order
2k
. This defines a free
\mu_{2k}
-action on
\tilde{Y}
, so its quotient
\bar{g}:\bar{Y}=\tilde{Y}/a\longrightarrow S
is a minimal abelian fibration with
\bar{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}/a
. The
\langle a\rangle
- and
\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-actions on
\tilde{Y}
commute by
Proposition

6.1.6
, so
\tilde{\varphi}
descends to a
\mu_{2k}=\langle\bar{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\bar{Y}
. Now
Proposition

6.2.1
(or more precisely
Corollary

6.2.13
) determines
\bar{\varphi}(i,z,\bar{y})=(-i,\zeta^{i}z^{-1},\bar{y})
, so the free action
\tilde{\varphi}
must be as claimed.
∎

7.3.22
.

Assume the sequence (
7.3.19
) does not split. Then
H\cong\mathbb{Z}/4k
or
\mathbb{Z}/4k\times\mathbb{Z}/2
. Moreover, the generator
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\mathbb{Z}/4k
is of the form

\tilde{\varphi}(i,z,y)=(1-i,\tfrac{\varepsilon\zeta^{i}}{z},y+a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z)\in C,\quad y\in A_{s},

where
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a constant,
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a primitive
2k
-th root of unity, and
a\in A_{s}
is a torsion point of order
4k
.

Proof.

Let us first show that
H
is isomorphic to the desired group. If
G=\mathbb{Z}/2
, then
H=\mathbb{Z}/4k
is the only nontrivial
\mathbb{Z}/2
-extension of
\mu_{2k}
. For the remaining possibilities we need to divide the case by parity of
r
. When
r
is odd, we can have
G=\mathbb{Z}/4
. Take an arbitrary lift
\tilde{\varphi}\in H
of
\varphi\in\mu_{2k}
. Then
\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}
is a nontrivial element in
G
because the sequence does not split. Since
\tilde{\varphi}
acts on the type
\mathrm{I}_{2kr}
curve
C=\sum_{i=1}^{2kr}C_{i}
by reversing the orientation,
\tilde{\varphi}(C_{i})=C_{-i}
or
C_{1-i}
and hence
\varphi^{2k}
always preserves every
C_{i}
. Hence
\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}\in G=\mathbb{Z}/4
must be
2\in\mathbb{Z}/4
by the description of
Proposition

6.2.1
–
6.2.2
. This means
\tilde{\varphi}
has order
4k
and hence
H\cong\mathbb{Z}/4k\times\mathbb{Z}/2
as claimed. Similarly when
r
is even,
G=(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
contains a unique nontrivial element
g
that preserves every
F_{i}
. This shows
\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}=g
and again
H\cong\mathbb{Z}/4k\times\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Let us now determine the generator
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\mathbb{Z}/4k
. In any case above,
\tilde{\varphi}
is the lift of
\varphi\in\mu_{2k}
such that
\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}
is a t-automorphism acting as

\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}(i,z,y)=(i,-z,y+\sigma)

for a nonzero
2
-torsion point
\sigma\in A_{s}
(
Proposition

6.2.2
(3)). Assume without loss of generality
G=\mathbb{Z}/2
. The automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
on
C\times A_{s}
is free and diagonal as usual, so
\tilde{\varphi}
must act on
A_{s}
as a translation by a torsion point
a\in A_{s}
of order
4k
(with
\sigma=2ka
). It acts on
C
by
\tilde{\varphi}(C_{i})=C_{-i}
or
C_{1-i}
as above. In the former case, one can show that
\tilde{\varphi}(i,z,y)=(-i,\zeta^{i}z^{-1},y+a)
or
(-i,-\zeta^{i}z^{-1},y+a+\tau)
as usual. But such
\tilde{\varphi}
can never have the
2k
-th power
\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}
as described above. Hence
\tilde{\varphi}
sends
C_{i}
to
C_{1-i}
.

We have already seen in
Corollary

6.2.13
that
\tilde{\psi}(i,z,y)=(-i,\zeta^{i}z^{-1},y)
. Now
Proposition

6.1.6
holds on
Y_{t}
, so on its double cover
\tilde{Y}_{t}
we have at least

\tilde{\varphi}(i,z,y)=(-i,\zeta^{i}z^{-1},y)\cdot\tilde{\varphi}(0,1,0)\quad\mbox{or}\quad(-i,-\zeta^{i}z^{-1},y+\tau)\cdot\tilde{\varphi}(0,1,0).

But the latter case is impossible because we have already assumed that
\tilde{\varphi}
acts on
A_{s}
as a translation by
a
(not
a+\tau
). Writing
\tilde{\varphi}(0,1,0)=(1,\varepsilon,a)
, this concludes the desired description of
\tilde{\varphi}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.3.2
.

We have already proved (1) and (2) along the way. When (
7.3.19
) splits,
Lemma

7.3.20
–
7.3.21
applies and (3a) follows from the isomorphism

\tfrac{1}{2k}X_{s}=\tfrac{1}{2k}\tilde{X}_{s}/G=(C\times A_{s})/(\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle\times G).

Even when (
7.3.19
) does not split, we still have an isomorphism
\tfrac{1}{2k}X_{s}=(C\times A_{s})/H
, and
Lemma

7.3.22
concludes.
∎

7.4.
Base change to unstable fibrations

Finally, let us consider the case when
g:Y\longrightarrow T
is unstable, the last remaining case
Lemma

7.1.5
(v).

7.4.1
.

In
Lemma

7.1.5
(v),
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
is isomorphic either any unstable fibers in
Theorem

5.2.1
except
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
,
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/2
, and
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}/4
for
r\geq 1
, or a quotient
(C\times A_{s})/(\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle\times G)
for two commuting free actions by
\tilde{\varphi}
and
G
as follows.

(
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{*}
)

C=E_{1}+E_{2}+2(\sum_{j=0}^{mr}F_{j})+E_{3}+E_{4}
is a type
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{*}
Kodaira singular curve for
R=mr
, the group
G=0
,
\mathbb{Z}/2
,
\mathbb{Z}/4
, or
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\times 2}
acts on
C\times A_{s}
as Albanese stabilizers, and
\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle=\mu_{m}
acts on
E_{i}\times A_{s}
and
F_{j}\times A_{s}
as

\tilde{\varphi}(z_{i},y)=(z_{i},y+a),\ \tilde{\varphi}(z_{j},y)=(\zeta^{j}z_{j},y+a)\quad\mbox{for}\quad(z_{i},y)\in E_{i}\times A_{s},\ (z_{j},y)\in F_{j}\times A_{s}.

Here
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a primitive
m
-th root of unity and
a\in A_{s}
has order
m
.

\bullet

\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle=\mu_{m}
or
\mu_{2m}
, and
G=\langle g\rangle=0
or
\mathbb{Z}/2
. They act freely on
C\times A_{s}
as

\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=(\alpha(x),y+a),\quad g(x,y)=(\beta(x),y+b)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in C\times A_{s},

where
a,b\in A_{s}
are torsion points. All possibilities for
(C,\alpha,\beta)
are described in
Table

7
, where we use the notation
C=\sum_{i=1}^{4}E_{i}+2F
for type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
C=\sum_{i=1}^{3}E_{i}
for type
\mathrm{IV}
, and
C=\sum_{i=1}^{3}(E_{i}+2F_{i})+3H
for type
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, respectively.

Table 7.
The additional unstable-like multiple fibers (multiplicity
m
is suppressed in the type for simplicity).

7.4.2
.

The
Kodaira type
of
Theorem

7.4.1
is either an unstable type or the additional types in
Table

7
, both multiplied by
m
.

7.4.3
.

The additional possibilities in
Table

7
were first observed by Matsushita
[
Mat01
, Table 4]
in the context of Lagrangian fibrations of symplectic fourfolds, using a different method. Some items in Matsushita’s table are divided into two in our descriptions. For example, both type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}/4
and
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-a
/2
correspond to Matsushita’s type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-
4
.

The additional types are named after the type of the curve
C
in the theorem (equivalently,
characteristic cycle
in Hwang–Oguiso’s terminology). Note however that the type of its t-automorphism group
P
can be very different.

7.4.4
.

In
Theorem

7.4.1
, the multiplicity
m
should satisfy the following. Let
T
be the type of
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}
.

(1)

If
T
is isogenous to
\mathrm{II}
or
\mathrm{II}^{*}
, then
\gcd(6,m)=1
.

(2)

If
T
is isogenous to
\mathrm{III}
or
\mathrm{III}^{*}
, then
2\nmid m
.

(3)

If
T
is isogenous to
\mathrm{IV}
or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, then
3\nmid m
.

(4)

If
T
is isogenous to
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
, then
2\nmid m
.

(5)

If
T
is isogenous to
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{*}
for
R\geq 1
, then
2\nmid m
.

(6)

For the additional types
T
, see
Table

7
.

The rest of this subsection will be devoted to the proof of
Theorem

7.4.1
and
Proposition

7.4.4
. Recall from
Lemma

7.1.5
(v) that we have an isomorphism (write
B_{t}=A_{s}
for notational simplicity)

Q_{t}^{\circ}=P_{s}^{\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}.

Both Albanese torsors have dimension
n-1
(
Proposition

4.1.6
and
7.1.7
):

\alb:Y_{t}\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}=\Alb_{Y_{t}},\qquad\alb:\tfrac{1}{m}X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}.

The former is a locally trivial bundle with fiber
C
, an unstable Kodaira singular curve by
Theorem

5.2.1
. The latter is also a locally trivial
C
-fiber bundle by the following:

7.4.5
.

The Albanese morphism
\frac{1}{m}X_{s}\longrightarrow\Alb_{s}
is a free
\varphi
-quotient of the Albanese morphism
Y_{t}\longrightarrow\Alb_{t}
.

Proof.

Same as
Lemma

7.3.18
, but using the following fact instead: every automorphism of an unstable Kodaira singular curve
C
has a fixed point. The proof of this latter claim is a case-by-case analysis. For example, a type
\mathrm{II}
curve has one singular point, so any automorphism of
C
has to fix it.
∎

Since
g:Y\longrightarrow T
is an unstable fibration, it has the Albanese stabilizer
G
and untangle
\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow Y
in
Proposition

4.2.4
, so that
\tilde{g}:\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow T
has a central fiber
\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}
. Applying
Proposition

6.2.1
–
6.2.2
to
Y
, we obtain an additional base change
U\longrightarrow T
of degree
d=2
,
3
,
4
, or
6
with a commutative diagram

(7.4.6)

\hbox to104.85pt{\vbox to60.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.4242pt\lower-30.42355pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-52.4242pt}{-26.76382pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}

Let
R\longrightarrow U
and
\tilde{R}\longrightarrow U
be the t-automorphism schemes of
h
and
\tilde{h}
, respectively. Since
U\longrightarrow S
is Galois with Galois group
\mu_{dm}
,
R
admits a
\mu_{dm}=\langle\psi\rangle
-action by
Proposition

6.1.1
and
Z
admits a
\mu_{dm}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action by
Proposition

6.1.6
. Similarly,
\tilde{R}
admits a
\mu_{d}=\langle\tilde{\psi}^{m}\rangle
-action and
\tilde{Z}
admits a
\mu_{d}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}^{m}\rangle
-action, the lifts of
\psi^{m}
and
\varphi^{m}
. Note however that
\varphi
(resp.
\psi
) may
not
lift to
\tilde{Z}
(resp.
\tilde{R}
) because the object in the topright corner of (
7.4.6
) is missing (similar problem to case (iv) in the diagram (
7.3.17
)). Note also that
G
- and
\langle\tilde{\varphi}^{m}\rangle
-actions on
\tilde{Z}
commute because the upper left square of (
7.4.6
) is cartesian.

Proof of
Proposition

7.4.4
(1–5).

Suppose that
h
is smooth, or equivalently
\tilde{Y}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
\cdots
, or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
. Recall from
Proposition

6.2.2
(2) that
\psi^{m}
acts on
R_{u}=(E\times A_{s})/G
by
\psi^{m}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)
for
(x,y)\in R_{u}
. By
Theorem

6.1.4
,
\psi
acts trivially on
\{0\}\times A_{s}\subset R_{u}
and hence induces a nontrivial action on their quotient
E/G
. This
\psi
-action on
E/G
satisfies
\psi^{m}(x)=\omega^{\pm 1}x
. For example, let us consider the types
\mathrm{II}
and
\mathrm{II}^{*}
. In this case, the automorphism
\psi
acts on
E/G
(with
j
-invariant
0
) such that its
m
-th power
\psi^{m}
acts as a primitive
6
-th root of unity. This is impossible when
m
is divisible by
2
or
3
, so
m
has to be coprime to
6
and concludes (2). Same argument proves (1–4) with a single exception: when
C
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
and
j(E)=1728
, we have
\Aut_{0}(E)=\mu_{4}
whereas
\psi^{m}
acts on
E/G
by
-1
. Therefore,
m
may be even in this case only when
j(E/G)=1728
so that
\Aut(E/G)=\mu_{4}
(i.e., when
G=0
,
E^{\sqrt{-1}}
, or
E[2]
). Even so,
m
is still not divisible by
4
. This case will fall into the first three additional cases in
Table

7
.

Same idea applies to when
h
is strictly semistable. In this case, notice that
\psi
fixes
A_{s}\subset R_{u}^{\circ}
and induces an action on its quotient
\mathbb{G}_{m}
such that
\psi^{m}(z)=1/z
. Since this is the only nontrivial group automorphism of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
,
m
cannot be even.
∎

Let us rephrase the above proof in the following more precise way:

7.4.7
.

d
and
m
are coprime except for the following single case:
C
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
j(E)=1728
,
d=2
, and
m\equiv 2\mod 4
. Moreover, in this case
\tau:G\hookrightarrow E
has the image
0
,
E^{\sqrt{-1}}
, or
E[2]
. ∎

The next step is to imitate the untangling process in
Lemma

7.3.20
. Take the diagram in
Lemma

4.2.1
and
Lemma

7.4.5
to form a cartesian diagram

In this case, the
\mu_{dm}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
Z
(resp.
\langle\psi\rangle
-action on
R
) lifts to a
\mu_{dm}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Z}
(resp.
\langle\tilde{\psi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{R}
) commuting with
G
.

(2)

If
d
and
m
are coprime (see
Corollary

7.4.7
), then the sequence (
7.4.8
) uniquely splits.

Proof.

(1) Same as
Lemma

7.3.20
. (2) Recall the inclusion
G\subset\pi_{0}(\tilde{Q}_{t})
. By
Theorem

5.3.2
and
Proposition

6.2.1
, the prime divisors of
|\pi_{0}(\tilde{Q}_{t})|
are always dominated by those of
d
in every unstable Kodaira type. Therefore,
\gcd(d,m)=1
implies
\gcd(|G|,m)=1
, whence the unique splitting
H=G\times\mu_{m}
.
∎

Lemma

7.4.10
–
7.4.14
will study the case when
H
splits.
Lemma

7.4.15
–
7.4.17
will study the case when
H
does not split.

7.4.10
.

If the sequence (
7.4.8
) splits, then the statement of
Proposition

4.2.4
(2) holds. That is, there exists a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\tilde{P}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0\quad\mbox{such that}\quad G\cap\tilde{P}^{\circ}=0.

In particular,
0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(P_{s})\longrightarrow 0
.

Proof.

The left exact sequence
0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\tilde{P}_{s}\longrightarrow P_{s}
is already established in
Lemma

7.4.9
(it uses
Lemma

7.1.8
), so it only remains to prove its surjectivity. Since
\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\circ}=Q_{t}^{\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}
, we have
\tilde{P}_{s}^{\circ}=P_{s}^{\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}
and hence
G\cap\tilde{P}_{s}^{\circ}=0
. Therefore, it is enough to show
\pi_{0}(P_{s})=\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s}/G)
. Consider the diagram
