where
p^{*}
are pullbacks of t-automorphisms. Their kernels are
0
or
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, and their images are the inertia-invariant subgroups by
Theorem

6.1.4
. We thus have isomorphisms
\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})=\pi_{0}(\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\tilde{\psi}})
and
\pi_{0}(P_{s})=\pi_{0}(Q_{t}^{\psi})
. Note that the
\mu_{m}=\langle\tilde{\psi}\rangle
- and
G
-actions on
\tilde{Q}_{t}
commute, so
G\subset\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\tilde{\psi}}
. This shows

\pi_{0}(P_{s})=\pi_{0}(Q_{t}^{\psi})=\pi_{0}((\tilde{Q}_{t}/G)^{\tilde{\psi}})=\pi_{0}(\tilde{Q}_{t}^{\tilde{\psi}}/G)=\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s}/G).\qed

7.4.11
.

Assume that the sequence (
7.4.8
) splits and
h
is smooth. Then there exists an isomorphism
\tilde{Z}_{u}\cong E\times A_{s}
such that the inertia action is

\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y+a),\quad(\omega^{\pm 1/2}x,y+a),\ \ \mbox{or}\ \ (\omega^{\pm 1/3}x,y+a)\quad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in E\times A_{s}.

Here
\omega
is a primitive
d
-th root of unity as in
Proposition

6.2.1
and
a\in A_{s}
is a torsion point of order
m
. The latter two only arise in the following four cases:

(1)

\tilde{Y}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
d=2
,
j(E)=1728
, and
m\equiv 2\mod 4
.

(2)

\tilde{Y}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
d=2
,
j(E)=0
, and
m\equiv 3\mod 6
.

(3)

\tilde{Y}
has type
\mathrm{IV}
,
d=3
,
j(E)=0
, and
m\equiv\pm 2\mod 6
.

(4)

\tilde{Y}
has type
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
,
d=3
,
j(E)=0
, and
m\equiv\pm 2\mod 6
.

Proof.

Let us first show that
\tilde{\varphi}
can be made into a diagonal automorphism. This is similar to the proof of
Proposition

6.2.1
, but keep in mind that
Lemma

6.2.5
(2) is false in this case (so
\tilde{\varphi}
cannot be identified with
\tilde{\psi}
). Use
Proposition

6.2.1
(1) to fix an isomorphism
\tilde{R}_{u}\cong E\times A_{s}
for an elliptic curve
E
such that the
\mu_{d}=\langle\tilde{\psi}^{m}\rangle
-action is
\tilde{\psi}^{m}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)
for
(x,y)\in E\times A_{s}
. The first part of the proof of
Lemma

6.2.9
still applies to
\tilde{\psi}
, so we have

\tilde{\psi}(x,y)=(\alpha x,y+\xi(x))\qquad\mbox{for}\quad\alpha\in\Aut_{0}(E),\quad\xi:E\longrightarrow A_{s}\mbox{ homomorphism}.

Comparing
\tilde{\psi}^{m}
, we have
\alpha^{m}=\omega^{\pm 1}
and
\xi((1+\alpha+\cdots+\alpha^{m-1})x)=0
for every
x\in E
. The latter means either
\xi\equiv 0
or
1+\alpha+\cdots+\alpha^{m-1}=0
.

We claim
\alpha=\omega^{\pm 1}
,
\omega^{\pm 1/2}
, or
\omega^{\pm 1/3}
, and
\alpha^{m}
is never
1
. This will show
\xi\equiv 0
and hence
\tilde{\psi}(x,y)=(\alpha x,y)
. In type
\mathrm{II}
,
\mathrm{II}^{*}
,
\mathrm{III}
,
\mathrm{III}^{*}
, or
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
with
j(E)\neq 0,1728
, we have
\Aut_{0}(E)=\mu_{d}
. Then
\alpha^{m}=\omega^{\pm 1}
is a generator of
\Aut_{0}(E)
, so this forces
\alpha=\omega^{\pm 1}
(and
\gcd(d,m)=1
in
Corollary

7.4.7
). This also means
\alpha^{m}=\omega^{\pm m}\neq 1
. In the remaining types
\mathrm{IV}
,
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, or
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
with
j(E)=0
or
1728
, we have
\Aut_{0}(E)\supsetneq\mu_{d}
. For example, if
\tilde{Y}
has type
\mathrm{IV}
or
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, then
d=3
and
\Aut_{0}(E)=\mu_{6}
. Hence
\alpha^{m}=\omega^{\pm 1}
is a primitive third root of unity, concluding
3\nmid m
. When
m
is odd,
\alpha=\omega^{\pm 1}
is the only possibility. When
m
is even,
\alpha
can be either
\omega^{\pm 1}
or
\omega^{\pm 1/2}
. In any case,
\alpha^{m}\neq 1
because
3\nmid m
. Same holds for type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
with
j(E)=0
or
1728
.

Now that we know
\tilde{\psi}(x,y)=(\alpha x,y)
for the desired
\alpha
,
Proposition

6.1.6
says

\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=\tilde{\psi}(x,y)+\tilde{\varphi}(0,0)=(\alpha x,y)+\tilde{\varphi}(0,0).

Since
\alpha\neq 1
, we can adjust the origin of the torsor
\tilde{Z}_{u}\cong E\times A_{s}
to make
\tilde{\varphi}(0,0)=(0,a)
and hence
\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=(\alpha x,y+a)
. Since
\tilde{\varphi}^{m}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)
by
Proposition

6.2.1
(1),
a\in A_{s}
is
m
-torsion. It has order precisely
m
because the induced
\mu_{m}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}
is free.
∎

Recall that
\tilde{Q}
is untangled by definition. The following computes the Kodaira type of
\tilde{P}
. Recall from
Definition

6.2.3
the conjugate of a Kodaira type.

7.4.12
.

Keep the notation and assumption of
Lemma

7.4.11
. In the first case of
\tilde{\varphi}
, the Kodaira type of
\tilde{P}
is that of
\tilde{Q}
or its conjugate. In the four cases (1–4), the following holds.

(1)

\tilde{Q}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
and
\tilde{P}
has type
\mathrm{III}
or
\mathrm{III}^{*}
.

(2)

\tilde{Q}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
, and
\tilde{P}
has type
\mathrm{II}
or
\mathrm{II}^{*}
.

(3)

\tilde{Q}
has type
\mathrm{IV}
, and
\tilde{P}
has type
\mathrm{II}
or
\mathrm{II}^{*}
.

(4)

\tilde{Q}
has type
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, and
\tilde{P}
has type
\mathrm{II}
or
\mathrm{II}^{*}
.

Proof.

In
Lemma

7.4.11
, we have deduced

\tilde{\psi}^{m}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y),\qquad\tilde{\psi}(x,y)=(\alpha x,y)\quad\mbox{for}\quad\alpha=\omega^{\pm 1},\omega^{\pm 1/2},\mbox{ or }\omega^{\pm 1/3}.

Corollary

6.2.4
computes the type of
\tilde{P}
from that of
\tilde{R}
.
∎

7.4.13
.

Assume that the sequence (
7.4.8
) splits and
h
is strictly semistable (so
d=2
and
m
is odd). Then

(1)

\tilde{P}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{r}^{*}
for
r\geq 1
,
\tilde{Q}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{mr}^{*}
, and
\tilde{R}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{2mr}
.

(2)

\tilde{Z}_{u}=D\times A_{s}
for a type
\mathrm{I}_{2mr}
curve
D
and the inertia action is

\tilde{\varphi}(i,z,y)=(-i,\tfrac{\zeta^{i}}{z},y+a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z)\in D,\ y\in A_{s},

where
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a primitive
2m
-th root of unity and
a\in A_{s}
has order
m
.

Compare this statement with the type
\mathrm{I}^{+}_{kr}
in
Theorem

7.3.2
: the only difference in their inertia actions is the order of
a\in A_{s}
.

Proof.

(1) This is again
Corollary

6.2.4
, noticing that the central fiber
\tilde{Z}_{u}=D\times A_{s}
is trivial as a base change of
\tilde{Y}
by
Proposition

6.2.1
(2). (2) By the same proposition,
\tilde{\psi}(i,z,y)=(-i,\zeta^{i}z^{-1},y)
for a primitive
2m
-th root of unity
\zeta
. Writing
\varphi(0,1,0)=(i_{0},\varepsilon,a)
, we have as usual

\tilde{\varphi}(i,z,y)=\tilde{\psi}(i,z,y)\cdot\tilde{\varphi}(0,1,0)=(i_{0}-i,\varepsilon\zeta^{i}z^{-1},y+a)\quad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z,y)\in D\times A_{s}.

Now use
\tilde{\varphi}^{m}(i,z,y)=(-i,(-1)^{i}z^{-1},y)
from the same proposition (and
m
is odd) to conclude that
i_{0}=0
,
\varepsilon=1
, and
ma=0
. The freeness of
\tilde{\varphi}
says
\ord a=m
.
∎

The following descends
Lemma

7.4.11
–
7.4.13
to
\tilde{Y}
.

7.4.14
.

Assume (
7.4.8
) splits. Then the induced
\mu_{m}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}
is diagonal, acting on
A_{s}
as a translation by a torsion point
a\in A_{s}
of order
m
, and acting on
C
as follows.

(1)

When
h
is smooth,
\tilde{\varphi}
acts on
C
either trivially or as the cases
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-a,
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-c,
\mathrm{IV}
-a, and
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
-a in
Theorem

7.4.1
and
Table

7
.

(2)

Suppose
h
is strictly semistable and write the Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{mr}^{*}
curve
C
by
E_{0}+E_{1}+2\big(\sum_{j=0}^{mr}F_{j}\big)+E_{2}+E_{3}
. Then
\tilde{\varphi}
acts on
E_{i}
trivially and on
F_{j}
by
\tilde{\varphi}(z)=(-\zeta)^{j}z
for
z\in F_{j}=\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

(1) Recall from
Proposition

6.1.9
that
\tilde{Z}/\tilde{\varphi}^{m}
and
\tilde{Y}
are
\tilde{Q}
-equivariantly birational over
T
.
Lemma

7.4.11
classified the inertia action on
\tilde{Z}_{u}
. In the non-exceptional case,
\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y+a)
and
\tilde{\varphi}^{m}(x,y)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y)
on
\tilde{Z}_{u}=E\times A_{s}
. Hence the induced
\mu_{m}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
on
\tilde{Z}_{u}/\tilde{\varphi}^{m}=\bar{C}\times A_{s}
is a translation by
a
on
A_{s}
, so it acts on
\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}
the same way.

For the exceptional cases, let us only consider type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
j(E)=1728
, and
m\equiv 2\mod 4
(others are similar). The four ramification points of
E\longrightarrow F=E/\pm 1=\mathbb{P}^{1}
are
E[2]
. The action
\tilde{\varphi}=\pm\sqrt{-1}
on
E
fixes two of them and swaps the other two. Hence, the induced
\tilde{\varphi}
on
F
fixes two branch points and swaps the other two. The four branch points are
E_{i}\cap F
in
\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}
for
C=\sum_{i=1}^{4}E_{i}+2F
. We also note that
\tilde{\varphi}
acts on the infinitesimal direction of
2F\subset C
by
\pm\sqrt{-1}
, so this determines the automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
on
C
uniquely up to conjugate.

(2)
Lemma

7.4.13
describes the
\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Z}_{u}=D\times A_{s}
for
D=\sum_{i=1}^{2mr}D_{i}
. The action
\tilde{\varphi}^{m}(i,z)=(-i,(-1)^{i}z^{-1})
induces an isomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}^{m}:D_{i}\longrightarrow D_{-i}
. Since
\tilde{\varphi}(i,z)=(-i,\zeta^{i}z^{-1})=(0,(-\zeta)^{i})\cdot(-i,(-1)^{i}z^{-1})
, we conclude that the induced
\mu_{m}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
F_{i}\subset D/\tilde{\varphi}^{m}
is given by multiplying
(-\zeta)^{i}
for
i\neq 0,mr
. The two components
D_{2mr}
and
D_{mr}
are preserved by
\tilde{\varphi}
, and their quotients are the two ends of the string
F_{0}
and
F_{mr}
, which are
\mathbb{P}^{1}
where
\tilde{\varphi}
acts trivially.
∎

Let us now consider the case when (
7.4.8
) is non-split. By
Corollary

7.4.7
and
7.4.9
,
\tilde{Y}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
,
j(E)=1728
,
d=2
, and
m\equiv 2\mod 4
. Let us write

m=2k\quad\mbox{for an odd integer }k.

Recall that
H
is an extension of
\mu_{m}=\mu_{2k}
by
G
. Since
H
is non-split, we have two choices
G=E^{\sqrt{-1}}
or
E[2]
. In any case,
G
contains a unique
\sqrt{-1}
-fixed element
\tau\neq 0
. Note that we can write
\tau=(1+\sqrt{-1})g
for any
g\in E[2]\setminus E^{\sqrt{-1}}
. The Albanese stabilizer
G\hookrightarrow E\times A_{s}
induces the another inclusion
\sigma:G\hookrightarrow A_{s}
.

7.4.15
.

Assume (
7.4.8
) does not split. Then there exists an isomorphism
Z_{u}\cong(E\times A_{s})/G
such that its inertia action is

\varphi(x,y)=(\pm\sqrt{-1}x+g,y+a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in(E\times A_{s})/G.

Here
g\in E[2]\setminus E^{\sqrt{-1}}
and
a\in A_{s}
is a torsion point of order
4k
such that
2ka=\sigma((1+\sqrt{-1})g)
.

Proof.

Let us first compute the inertia automorphism
\psi
on
R_{u}
. Recall that
\tilde{R}_{u}=E\times A_{s}
and
R_{u}=(E\times A_{s})/G
, where
G=E^{\sqrt{-1}}
or
E[2]
and the
G
-action is given by translations by combining
G\subset E
and
\sigma:G\hookrightarrow A_{s}
(
Proposition

6.2.2
(2)). Since
\psi
fixes
\{0\}\times A_{s}\subset R_{u}
, there exists an induced group automorphism
\psi
on the quotient
R_{u}/(\{0\}\times A_{s})=E/G
, whose
j
-invariant is again
1728
. Hence
\psi
on
E/G
is a multiplication by
\alpha=-1
or
\pm\sqrt{-1}
. In any case, it admits a unique lift to
E\times\{0\}\subset R_{u}
by the same multiplication. This shows that
\psi
is of the form

\psi(x,y)=(\alpha x,y+\xi(x))\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in R_{u}=(E\times A_{s})/G,

where
\xi:E\longrightarrow A_{s}
is a homomorphism. Since we already know
\psi^{2k}(x,y)=(-x,y)
by
Proposition

6.2.1
(1),
\alpha^{2k}=-1
and
\xi((1+\alpha+\cdots+\alpha^{2k-1})x)=0
for every
x\in E
. Recalling
k
is odd, we obtain
\alpha=\pm\sqrt{-1}
and
\xi\equiv 0
. This shows
\psi(x,y)=(\pm\sqrt{-1}x,y)
for
(x,y)\in R_{u}=(E\times A_{s})/G
.

Writing
\varphi(0,0)=(g,a)\in Z_{u}=(E\times A_{s})/G
, we have
\varphi(x,y)=(\pm\sqrt{-1}x+g,y+a)
. Since
\varphi
is free and has order
4k
,
a\in A_{s}
has order
4k
. Since
\varphi^{2k}(x,y)=(-x,y)
, we have
((1+\sqrt{-1})g,2ka)\in G
. When
G=E^{\sqrt{-1}}
, this makes
g\in E[2]\setminus E^{\sqrt{-1}}
as claimed. When
G=E[2]
, there are more freedom of
g
, but we can choose
g
of the desired form.
∎

7.4.16
.

Even when (
7.4.8
) does not split, there exists a preferred free
\mu_{4k}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Z}
lifting the
\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
Z
. This
\tilde{\varphi}
commutes with
\tau\in G
and
\tilde{Z}\dashrightarrow\tilde{Y}
is given by the
(\tau\circ\tilde{\varphi}^{2k})
-quotient.

Proof.

Simply take the automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}(x,y)=(\pm\sqrt{-1}x+g,y+a)
of
\tilde{Z}_{u}=E\times A_{s}
, the same formula as
Lemma

7.4.15
. Computation shows such
\tilde{\varphi}
has an order
4k
and commutes with
\tau=(1+\sqrt{-1})g
. The extension of
\tilde{\varphi}
to an automorphism of
\tilde{Z}
is formal as in
Lemma

7.2.5
(use
Lemma

A.1.3
). Finally,
\tau\circ\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}(x,y)=\tau(-x+(1+\sqrt{-1})g,y+2ka)=(-x,y)
is the automorphism describing the quotient
\tilde{Z}\longrightarrow\tilde{Z}/\mu_{2}
whose crepant resolution is
\tilde{Y}
.
∎

7.4.17
.

If (
7.4.8
) does not split, then
G=E^{\sqrt{-1}}=\mathbb{Z}/2
. Moreover,

(1)

Q
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}/2
and
P
has type
\mathrm{III}/2
or
\mathrm{III}^{*}/2
.

(2)

H\cong\mathbb{Z}/4k=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
acts on
\tilde{Y}_{t}=C\times A_{s}
as the case
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-b in
Table

7
.

Proof.

Take the automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
on
\tilde{Z}
in
Lemma

7.4.16
. Noticing
\tilde{\varphi}
and
\tau
commute, take the free quotients
\bar{Z}=\tilde{Z}/\tau
and
\bar{Y}=\tilde{Y}/\tau
. Then
\tilde{\varphi}
descends to the
\mu_{4k}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\bar{Z}
, and
\bar{Z}\dashrightarrow\bar{Y}
is a rational quotient by
\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}
. Taking the further quotient
\bar{X}=\bar{Y}/\tilde{\varphi}
, we obtain the following commutative diagram:

The lower half of the diagram plays the same role with
Lemma

7.2.5
. Therefore, taking
G^{\prime}=G/\langle\tau\rangle=0
or
\mathbb{Z}/2
, we have a left exact sequence
0\longrightarrow G^{\prime}\longrightarrow\bar{P}\longrightarrow P
with
G^{\prime}\cap\bar{P}^{\circ}=0
. This shows the inclusion
G^{\prime}\subset\pi_{0}(\bar{P}_{s})
.

(1) Let us now study the upper half of the diagram, or equivalently assume
G=E^{\sqrt{-1}}=\langle\tau\rangle
. By
Lemma

7.4.16
, we have a
\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle\times\langle\tau\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Z}
. Such action descends to the
\mu_{4k}=\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Y}
, which describes the
H
-action.
Proposition

6.2.2
shows that
Q
has type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}/2
, and
P
is isogenous to type
\mathrm{III}
or
\mathrm{III}^{*}
. By
Lemma

7.4.15
,
R_{u}^{\psi}=(E^{\sqrt{-1}}\times A_{s})/\tau\cong A_{s}
has a trivial
\pi_{0}
. By
Theorem

6.1.4
, this shows
\pi_{0}(P_{s})=0
as claimed. Now that we know
\pi_{0}(\bar{P}_{s})=0
, this forces
G^{\prime}=0
. That is, the case
G=E[2]
is impossible.

(2) By definition of
\tilde{\varphi}(x)=\pm\sqrt{-1}x+g
for
x\in E
, it cycles
\tilde{\varphi}:0\longmapsto g\longmapsto\tau=(1+\sqrt{-1})g\longmapsto\sqrt{-1}g\longmapsto 0
. These are the four ramification points of
E\longrightarrow F=E/\pm 1=\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Hence
\tilde{\varphi}
descends to an automorphism on
F
that cycles four branch points, which are
E_{i}\cap F
in the type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
curve
C=\sum_{i=1}^{4}E_{i}+2F
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.4.1
.

Suppose that the sequence (
7.4.8
) splits. In this case, we have an isomorphism
\tfrac{1}{m}X_{s}=\tfrac{1}{m}\tilde{X}_{s}/G=\tilde{Y}_{t}/(\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle\times G)=(C\times A_{s})/(\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle\times G)
and the
\langle\tilde{\varphi}\rangle
-action on
\tilde{Y}_{t}
is classified in
Corollary

7.4.14
. Note that the Albanese stabilizer
G
is a subgroup of
\pi_{0}(\tilde{P}_{s})
by
Lemma

7.4.10
, which is again classified in
Lemma

7.4.12
and
Lemma

7.4.13
(1).

If
h
is smooth and
\tilde{\varphi}
is not exceptional, then
\tilde{\varphi}
acts trivially on the
C
factor, so
\frac{1}{m}\tilde{X}_{s}=(C\times A_{s}/a)/G
is isomorphic to the unstable fibers in
Theorem

5.2.1
. If
h
is smooth and
\tilde{\varphi}
is exceptional, then we obtain the types in
Table

7
except
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-b. If
h
is strictly semistable, this is the description of the type
\mathrm{I}_{R}^{*}
for
R=mr
.

Even when the sequence (
7.4.8
) does not split, we still have an isomorphism
\tfrac{1}{m}X_{s}=(C\times A_{s})/H
. The
H
-action on
\tilde{Y}_{t}
is described in
Corollary

7.4.17
. This is the remaining case
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-b.
∎

8.
Examples

Let us realize all central fibers
X_{s}
classified in
Theorem

5.1.1
,
5.2.1
, and those in §
7
. Throughout, we assume that
E
is an elliptic curve,
A
is an abelian variety of dimension
n-1
, and
\omega,\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
are roots of unity.

8.0.1
.

Let
X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal elliptic fibration and
\mathfrak{A}\longrightarrow S
an abelian scheme of relative dimension
n-1
. Then
X\times_{S}\mathfrak{A}\longrightarrow S
is a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with a central fiber
X_{s}\times\mathfrak{A}_{s}=X_{s}\times A
.

8.0.2
Semistable fibrations with torsion shears.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be any minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with a t-automorphism scheme
P\longrightarrow S
. Let
G\subset P_{s}
be a finite subgroup that
acts freely on
X_{s}
, i.e.,
G
has a trivial intersection with every possible stabilizer of
X_{s}
. Shrinking
S
, we may extend it to torsion sections
G\subset P
. The quotient
\bar{f}:\bar{X}=X/G\longrightarrow S
is a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration with a central fiber
\bar{X}_{s}=X_{s}/G
.

If
f
is as in the previous example, then
X_{s}=C\times A
and
\bar{X}_{s}=(C\times A)/G
. Suppose that
C
is a type
\mathrm{I}_{er}
semistable curve for positive integers
e
and
r
. Then
P_{s}\cong\mathbb{Z}/er\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times A
and the only nontrivial stabilizer is
\{0\}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\{0\}
by
Lemma

5.1.7
. Take any torsion element
(r,\zeta,a)\in P_{s}
of order
e
and
G\subset P_{s}
a cyclic group generated by it. This yields a semistable
\bar{X}_{s}=(C\times A)/G
of type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
with a torsion shear
a\in A
and Albanese torsor
\Alb_{\bar{X}_{s}}=A/a
.

8.0.3
Unstable fibrations.

In
Example

8.0.2
, suppose that
C
is an unstable Kodaira curve. Then
P_{s}\cong\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times\mathbb{G}_{a}\times A_{s}
and every stabilizer is contained in
\pi_{0}(P_{s})\times\mathbb{G}_{a}\times\{0\}
. If
G
is a finite abelian group with two inclusions
G\subset\pi_{0}(P_{s})
and
G\subset A_{s}
, then
G\subset P_{s}
yields a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
\bar{X}=X/G\longrightarrow S
with Albanese stabilizer
G
.

8.0.4
Semistable fibrations with non-torsion shears.

Nakamura
[
Nak77
]
and Hwang–Oguiso
[
HO16
]
constructed explicit examples of minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibrations
f:X\longrightarrow S
of type
\mathrm{I}_{1}
with non-torsion shears. We refer to the original articles for their (analytic) constructions. If we take a degree
r
totally ramified base change
T\longrightarrow S
and a crepant resolution
Y\longrightarrow X_{T}\longrightarrow T
, this constructs a type
\mathrm{I}_{r}
fiber with non-torsion shears.

8.0.5
Unstable isotrivial fibrations.

Many of the examples in
[
Mat01
, §6]
,
[
HO11
, §6]
,
[
KLM26
]
are isotrivial. Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration that is non-multiple and isotrivial (all smooth fibers are isomorphic to an abelian variety
B
). A standard level structure argument shows that there exists a finite base change
T\longrightarrow S
such that
f_{T}
is birational to a trivial family
g:Y=B\times T\longrightarrow T
. This means that
f
is unstable (e.g.,
Theorem

6.1.4
) and moreover, it cannot be
\mathrm{I}_{\geq 1}^{*}
-type (
Proposition

6.2.1
). This generalizes
[
KLM26
, Proposition 4.20]
.

Conversely, start with an elliptic curve
E
, abelian variety
A
of dimension
n-1
, and trivial family
Y=(E\times A)\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Endow it with a
\mu_{d}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action by

\varphi(x,y,t)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y,\omega t)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y,t)\in Y=E\times A\times\mathbb{A}^{1}

described in
Proposition

6.2.1
. Its quotient is a klt-minimal abelian fibration
\bar{X}=Y/\varphi\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
whose minimal model
X\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
can be any non-
\mathrm{I}_{\geq 1}^{*}
-type unstable fibration (with a trivial Albanese stabilizer). To produce examples with nontrivial Albanese stabilizers, use
Example

8.0.2
.

8.0.6
Type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}/4
.

Given a variety
Y
, we denote by
Y^{[2]}
and
Y^{(2)}
their Hilbert and symmetric squares, respectively. Take a
smooth
elliptic fibration
Y\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
and

Y^{[2]}\longrightarrow Y^{(2)}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{(2)}\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}.

It is singular along the diagonal
\mathbb{A}^{1}\subset(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{(2)}
. Take a general trait
S
on the base that intersects transversally with
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. The restriction of
Y^{[2]}
over
S
is a minimal
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
f:X\longrightarrow S
whose central fiber
X_{s}
is a union of two
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles over an elliptic curve
E
, one with multiplicity
1
and the other one with multiplicity
2
. An interesting computation of
X_{s}
is left to the reader, but even without it,
X_{s}
must be type
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}/4
according to our classification in
Theorem

5.2.1
.

The following examples construct multiple central fibers. Note that our classifications of multiple fibrations have explicit descriptions of the inertia action
\varphi
(or
\tilde{\varphi}
), so constructing examples is easy.

8.0.7
Multiple fibers via nontrivial torsors.

Let
S=\Spec R
and
S_{0}=\Spec K
be the Spec of a strictly henselian dvr and its field of fractions. Let
P\longrightarrow S
be any
\delta
-regular Néron model of an abelian variety over
S_{0}
. Any
\alpha\in H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(S_{0},P_{0})
defines a
P_{0}
-torsor
X_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
and its unique minimal model
f:X\longrightarrow S
. If
\alpha\neq 0
, then
f
cannot have any sections, or the central fiber
X_{s}
is a multiple fiber. The group
H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(S_{0},P_{0})
is torsion, so suppose that
\alpha
has order
m
. The multiple fiber
X_{s}
then has a multiplicity
m
since the degree
m
totally ramified base change
T\longrightarrow S
kills
\alpha
. Though this interpretation was not used in our proofs, this is an important viewpoint to think about multiple fibrations.

8.0.8
Type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}_{0}
.

Let
B
be an abelian variety of dimension
n
and
B\times T\longrightarrow T=\mathbb{A}^{1}
a trivial family. Take its free
\mu_{m}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action given by

\varphi(x,t)=(x+b,\zeta t)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,t)\in B\times T,

where both
b\in B
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
have order
m
. The quotient is a minimal fibration
f:X=(B\times T)/\varphi\longrightarrow S
with
X_{s}=m\cdot(B/b)
. See
Theorem

7.2.1
.

8.0.9
Type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}_{0}^{+}
.

Take a free
\mu_{m}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action on
E\times A\times T
by

\varphi(x,y,t)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y+a,\zeta t)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y,t)\in E\times A\times T.

Here we choose
m=dk
for
d=2,3,4,
or
6
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a primitive
dk
-th root of unity,
\omega=\zeta^{k}\in\Aut_{0}(E)
has order
d
, and
a\in A
has order
dk
. The quotient has a central fiber
X_{s}=dk\cdot(E\times A)/\varphi
. See
Theorem

7.2.2
.

8.0.10
Type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}^{k}_{R}
for
m=kl
and
R=kr
.

Start with a minimal elliptic fibration
X\longrightarrow S=\mathbb{A}^{1}
with a multiple central fiber
X_{s}
of type
l\cdot\mathrm{I}_{r}
. Take a sequence of ramified coverings of
S
and minimal base changes over them

\hbox to112.64pt{\vbox to39.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.31935pt\lower-19.78168pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Then
X_{s}^{+}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{lr}
and
Y_{t}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{klr}
. Moreover,
Y
admits a
\mu_{kl}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action. By
Lemma

7.3.12
, the inertia action is of the form

\varphi(i,z)=(i+kr,\zeta^{i}z)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z)\in Y_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{klr}C_{i},

where
\zeta
is a primitive
kl
-th root. Take an abelian variety
A
of dimension
n-1
and construct a
\mu_{kl}
-action on
Y\times A
by

\varphi(x,y)=(\varphi(x),y+a)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y)\in Y\times A,

where
a\in A
has order
kl
. The quotient
(Y\times A)/\mu_{kl}\longrightarrow S
has a central fiber of type
kl\cdot\mathrm{I}^{k}_{kr}
. The t-automorphism
P
of the quotient is the
\psi
-invariant of the t-automorphism of
Y\times A
. See
Theorem

7.3.1
.

Then
Z_{u}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{2r}
and
Y_{t}
has type
\mathrm{I}_{2kr}
. Moreover,
Y
admits a
\mu_{2k}=\langle\varphi\rangle
-action, which is described on
Y_{t}
by

\varphi(i,z)=(-i,\tfrac{\zeta^{i}}{z})\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z)\in Y_{t},

where
\zeta
is a primitive
2k
-th root. Now combine it with a translation automorphism by
a\in A
of order
2k
to form a diagonal
\mu_{2k}
-action on
Y\times A\longrightarrow T
. Its quotient is
(Y\times A)/\varphi\longrightarrow S
with a central fiber of type
2k\cdot\mathrm{I}^{+}_{kr}
. See
Theorem

7.3.2
.

8.0.12
Type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}^{-}_{R}
for
m=2k
and
R=kr
.

Take the same type
\mathrm{I}_{2kr}
minimal elliptic fibration
Y\longrightarrow T
in
Example

8.0.11
. It has a central t-automorphism group
\mathbb{Z}/2kr\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
, so take any t-automorphism
\varepsilon_{1}
of
Y
that acts on
Y_{t}
by
(1,\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{Z}/2kr\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Then
\tilde{\varphi}=\varepsilon_{1}\circ\varphi
is an automorphism on
Y
that acts on
Y_{t}
by

\tilde{\varphi}(i,z)=(1-i,\tfrac{\varepsilon\zeta^{i}}{z})\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(i,z)\in Y_{t}.

One checks that it has order
4k
on
Y
, such that
\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}
acts trivially on
T
and acts on
Y_{t}
as a t-automorphism
\tilde{\varphi}^{2k}(i,z)=(i,-z)
. Use this to define a diagonal
\mu_{4k}
-action by
Y\times A
(translation of order
4k
on
A
), so that the quotient
(Y\times A)/\tilde{\varphi}\longrightarrow S
has a central fiber of type
2k\cdot\mathrm{I}^{-}_{kr}
. See
Theorem

7.3.2
.

8.0.13
Type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}_{R}^{*}
for odd
m=k
and
R=kr
.

Again start with
Y\longrightarrow T
in
Example

8.0.11
of type
\mathrm{I}_{2kr}
, but assume furthermore that
k
is odd. Endow
Y\times A
with a diagonal
\mu_{2k}
-action

\varphi(x,y)=(\varphi(x),y+a)

but with
a\in A
of order
k
(instead of the original
2k
). Then
\varphi^{k}
acts as
\varphi^{k}(i,z,y)=(-i,\frac{(-1)^{i}}{z},y)
on the central fiber because
k
is odd. This is the automorphism described in
Proposition

6.2.1
(2), so
(Y\times A)/\varphi^{k}\longrightarrow T/\varphi^{k}
has a minimal model of type
\mathrm{I}_{kr}^{*}
. Its subsequent
\varphi
-quotient has type
k\cdot\mathrm{I}_{kr}^{*}
. See
Lemma

7.4.13
.

8.0.14
Other unstable-like multiple fibers.

Start with a trivial family
E\times A\times T\longrightarrow T=\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Take a group automorphism
\omega
on
E
of order
d=2,3,4,
or
6
, an integer
m
coprime with
d
, and a primitive
dm
-th root
\zeta
such that
\omega=\zeta^{m}
. Endow it with a
\mu_{dm}
-action by

\varphi(x,y,t)=(\omega^{\pm 1}x,y+a,\zeta t)\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(x,y,t)\in E\times A\times T,

where
a\in A
has order
m
. The quotient
(E\times A\times T)/\varphi\longrightarrow S
has an unstable-like multiple central fiber as described in
Lemma

7.4.11
. Changing the first entry of
\varphi
to
\omega^{\pm 1/2}x
or
\omega^{\pm 1/3}x
will yield four exceptional types (and their quotients). See
Lemma

7.4.11
.

To produce the most exotic type
m\cdot\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-b, assume
j(E)=1728
,
d=2
,
m=2k
for odd
k
, and endow it with a
\mu_{4k}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
-action on
E\times A\times U
by

\tilde{\varphi}(x,y,t)=(\pm\sqrt{-1}x+g,y+a,\zeta t),\qquad\tau(x,y,t)=(x+(1+\sqrt{-1})g,y+2ka,t),

where
g\in E[2]\setminus E^{\sqrt{-1}}
,
a\in A
has order
4k
, and
\zeta
is a primitive
4k
-th root. The quotient
(E\times A\times U)/\langle\tilde{\varphi},\tau\rangle\longrightarrow S=U/\mu_{4k}
has a central fiber of type
2k\cdot\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
-b. See
Lemma

7.4.15
.

Appendix A
Auxiliary results

All discussions are over a fixed field
k
of characteristic
0
. In many cases, we need to apply these results to non-reduced and reducible schemes
X
.

We thank Takumi Murayama for explaining his results on the Kollár injectivity and torsion-freeness theorem.

A.0.1
.

Let
S
be a Dedekind scheme over
k
, and
f:X\longrightarrow S
a flat projective morphism of relative dimension
n
where
X
has rational singularities. Then
R^{i}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is locally free for
0\leq i\leq n
and its formation commutes with base change.

Proof.

This is a consequence of
[
KK25
, Theorem 1]
, where
S
assumed to be a variety over
k
. We simply point out their arguments applies the same when
S
is a Dedekind scheme, which we summarize as follows.

Our claim is Zariski local on the base, so we may assume
S=\Spec R
where
R
is a Dedekind domain with residue field
k
. First,
R^{i}f_{*}\omega_{X}
is locally free because it is torsion-free by
[
Mur24
, Theorem 8.6.1]
(or by
[
Mur25
, Theorem A(ii)]
with the injectivity
\Longrightarrow
torsion-freeness argument). By (16) and (19) of
[
KK25
]
, both
R^{i}f_{*}\omega_{f}
and
R^{i}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
are locally free as well. To show the formation of higher direct images of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
commutes with arbitrary base change, it is enough to prove a quasi-isomorphism
Rf_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}R^{i}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}[-i]
by the cohomology base change for quasi-coherent sheaves
[
Stacks
, Tag 08IB]
.

Again by (16) in op. cit., we may prove
Rf_{*}\omega_{f}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}R^{i}f_{*}\omega_{f}[-i]
instead. For it, take a sufficiently general and positive divisor
H\subset X
, the composition
g:H\subset X\longrightarrow S
, and apply the induction argument of Theorem 1 in op. cit and
[
Kol86
, Theorem 3.1]
. The only part that needs to be checked is the splitting of the surjective homomorphism
\alpha:g_{*}\omega_{g}\longrightarrow R^{1}f_{*}\omega_{f}
. Since
S
is affine and
R^{1}f_{*}\omega_{f}
is locally free, we have
\Ext^{1}_{S}(R^{1}f_{*}\omega_{f},\ker\alpha)=0
. This completes the proof.
∎

A.1.
Algebraic groups and actions

An
isogeny
between two commutative connected algebraic groups
G
and
H
is a quasi-finite surjective homomorphism
f:G\longrightarrow H
. An
isogeny
between two abelian torsors is a finite étale morphism between them, which is necessarily Galois.

A.1.1
.

Let
G
and
H
be connected commutative algebraic groups. Then an isogeny
f:G\longrightarrow H
induces isogenies of their linear and abelian variety parts. In particular, the linear parts of
G
and
H
are isomorphic.

Proof.

The homomorphism
f
induces a homomorphism between Chevalley exact sequences

The homomorphism
h
is surjective by the Snake lemma. The neutral component of
\ker h
is an abelian variety, so it does not admit a surjection onto a connected linear algebraic group. This forces
\coker g
to be finite, or equivalently
g
is surjective. Hence both
g
and
h
are isogenies. For the second statement, notice that every connected and commutative linear algebraic group over
k
is of the form
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times a}\times\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\times b}
. If the two are isogenous, then they are isomorphic (not by the isogeny).
∎

A
locally algebraic group
is a locally finite type group scheme
G
over
k
. An
algebraic group
is a finite type group scheme
G
over
k
, or equivalently a locally algebraic group with finite
\pi_{0}(G)
.

A.1.2
.

Let
G
be a commutative locally algebraic group.

(1)

If
\pi_{0}(G)
is finitely generated, then
0\longrightarrow G^{\circ}\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(G)\longrightarrow 0
splits.

(2)

If
\pi_{0}(G)
is finite and
G^{\circ}
is unipotent, then the splitting is unique.

Proof.

Fix an isomorphism
\pi_{0}(G)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\mathbb{Z}/d_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r}\mathbb{Z}
. For each
i
, take a connected component
G^{i}\subset G
corresponding to a generator
1\in\mathbb{Z}/d_{i}\subset\pi_{0}(G)
. The
d_{i}
-th power morphism
[d_{i}]:G^{i}\longrightarrow G^{\circ}
is surjective by
[
BLR90
, Lemma 7.3.1]
, so take any point
g_{i}\in G^{i}
such that
d_{i}\cdot g_{i}=0
. For each
j
, take any point
g_{j}\in G
in the component corresponding to a generator of the
j
-th
\mathbb{Z}
. Then the subgroup of
G
generated by all
g_{i}
and
g_{j}
is isomorphic to
\pi_{0}(G)
and contains precisely one element in each connected component of
G
. This proves the
\pi_{0}
-sequence splits. The splitting is unique iff
\Hom(\pi_{0}(G),G^{\circ})=0
.
∎

A.1.3
.

Let
A
,
B
, and
\tilde{B}
be abelian torsors over a scheme
S
,
f:A\longrightarrow B
a morphism of abelian torsors, and
\tilde{B}\longrightarrow B
an isogeny. Assume that
f_{s}:A_{s}\longrightarrow B_{s}
over a closed point
s\in S
lifts to a morphism
\tilde{f}_{s}:A_{s}\longrightarrow\tilde{B}_{s}
. Then shrinking
(S,s)
étale locally, there exists a unique lift of
f
to a morphism of abelian torsors
\tilde{f}:A\longrightarrow\tilde{B}
whose restriction over
s
is
\tilde{f}_{s}
.

Proof.

We may assume
S=\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,s}^{sh}
is strictly henselian. Then all abelian torsors are trivial, so we may assume they are abelian schemes and
f
is a homomorphism. Take a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\tilde{B}\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow 0
of fppf abelian sheaves over
S
(here
G
is a constant finite group scheme) and its long exact sequence

\Hom(A,G)=0\longrightarrow\Hom(A,\tilde{B})\longrightarrow\Hom(A,B)\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}(A,G).

The last term is the global section of the sheaf-Ext
\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}^{1}(A,G)
since
S
is strictly henselian
[
Stacks
, Tag 03QO]
. Noticing
\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}^{1}(A,G)=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\hat{G},\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}^{1}(A,\mathbb{G}_{m}))=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\hat{G},\check{A})
, the last term is
\Hom(\hat{G},\check{A})
. A torsion section of
\check{A}
is trivial if and only if its restriction to
s
is trivial.
∎

A.1.4
.

Let
G
be an algebraic group acting on a quasi-projective scheme
X
over
k
. If
K\subset Z_{G}
is a finite subgroup of the center of
G
, then there exists an induced
G/K
-action on
X/K
.

Proof.

Endow the action morphism
G\times X\longrightarrow X
with an equivariant
K^{\times 2}
-action by
(k,k^{\prime})\cdot(g,x)=(kg,k^{\prime}\cdot x)
and
(k,k^{\prime})\cdot x=(kk^{\prime})\cdot x
. Its scheme-theoretic quotient yields a morphism
G/K\times X/K\longrightarrow X/K
. The group action axioms are easily checked.
∎

The following is a slight generalization of
[
AF16
, Lemma 5.16]
.

A.1.5
.

Let
G
be an algebraic group acting on a proper connected scheme
X
over
k
. If the kernel of the action is affine, then then the stabilizer group
\St_{x}
is affine for every point
x\in X
.

Proof.

We may assume
G
is connected. Let us first prove the claim when
X
is reduced. Writing
X=\bigcup_{i}X_{i}
for its irreducible decomposition,
G
acts on each
X_{i}
. Since
X_{i}
is integral, the proof of
[
AF16
, Lemma 5.16]
shows that every
x\in X_{i}
has an affine stabilizer if and only if
\ker(G\longrightarrow\Aut_{X_{i}})
is affine. Assume on the contrary that there exists an irreducible component
X_{i}
with non-affine kernel, and let
H\subset G
be the (nontrivial)
anti-affine part
of the kernel. Then
H
acts trivially on the entire
X_{i}
. By connectedness of
X
, there exists another component
X_{j}
such that
H
fixes a point
x\in X_{i}\cap X_{j}
. By
[
Bri09
, Lemma 1.1]
,
H
acts on the entire
X_{j}
trivially. Induction (and connectedness of
X
) concludes that
H
acts on
X
trivially, or
H\subset\ker(G\longrightarrow\Aut_{X})
. But the kernel was assumed to be affine, so it cannot contain a nontrivial anti-affine subgroup.

In a general case, we finish the proof by showing the induced
G
-action on
X_{\red}
has an affine kernel. This will imply the claim because the problem of considering stabilizer groups
\St_{x}
for
x\in X
is set-theoretic and thus can be checked on
X_{\red}
.

Assume on the contrary that
\ker(G\longrightarrow\Aut_{X_{\red}})
is a not affine, and let
H
be its anti-affine part. Since every point
x\in X
is fixed by
H
, the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
admits an
H
-action. Writing
\mathfrak{m}
for its maximal ideal,
H
acts trivially on
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}
for
i\geq 0
again by
[
Bri09
, Lemma 1.1]
. Since
\bigcap_{i}\mathfrak{m}^{i}=0
, this means that
H
acts trivially on
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
for every point
x\in X
. Hence
H
acts on
X
trivially, violating the fact that the kernel of the
G
-action is affine.
∎

A.1.6
.

Let
C
be a proper curve whose irreducible components, with reduced induced structures, are rational curves. Then
\Aut_{C}
is affine.

Proof.

We claim that every connected algebraic group
G
acting faithfully on
C
is affine. Take an induced
G
-action on the normalization
C^{\nu}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. This constructs a morphism
G\longrightarrow\PGL_{2}^{\times r}
, so it suffices to show that its kernel is affine. That is, we may assume
G
acts on
C^{\nu}
trivially, or on
C_{\red}
trivially.

Now we have a faithful
G
-action on
C
acting trivially on
C_{\red}
. We can use Brion’s method as in
Lemma

A.1.5
. Assume on the contrary that
G
is not affine, and take its anti-affine part
H\subset G
. Then every point
x\in C
is fixed by
H
so we have an
H
-action on
\mathcal{O}_{C,x}
. By
[
Bri09
, Lemma 1.1]
,
H
acts trivially on the quotient
\mathcal{O}_{C,x}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}
for every
i
, whence acts trivially on the entire
\mathcal{O}_{C,x}
. This shows
H
acts on
C
trivially, violating the assumption that
G
acts faithfully on
C
.
∎

A.1.1.
Picard schemes

A.1.7
.

Let
G
be a finite abelian group acting freely on a projective scheme
X
over
k
with
h^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=1
, and
q:X\longrightarrow Z=X/G
its quotient. Then
q^{*}:\Pic_{Z}\longrightarrow\Pic_{X}
has a kernel
\hat{G}=\Hom(G,\mathbb{G}_{m})
.

Proof.

The stacky quotient of the structure morphism
X\longrightarrow\Spec k
is a morphism
f:Z\longrightarrow BG=[\Spec k/G]
. The Leary spectral sequence applied to
f
is:

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}(G,H^{q}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m}))\quad\Longrightarrow\quad H^{p+q}(Z,\mathbb{G}_{m}).

This yields the desired left exact sequence
0\longrightarrow H^{1}(G,\mathbb{G}_{m})\longrightarrow H^{1}(Z,\mathbb{G}_{m})\longrightarrow H^{1}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})
. Note that
H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=k
implies
H^{0}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})=\mathbb{G}_{m}
(an fppf sheaf on
BG
), and
H^{1}(G,\mathbb{G}_{m})=\Hom(G,\mathbb{G}_{m})=\hat{G}
.
∎

The following is a generalization of
[
Stacks
, Tag 0C6S]
for proper schemes of characteristic
0
.

A.1.8
.

Let
Y
be a proper scheme over
k
and
i:Z\hookrightarrow Y
a closed subscheme defined by a nilpotent ideal. Then

(1)

i^{*}:\Pic^{\circ}_{Y}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{Z}
has a unipotent kernel and cokernel.

(2)

i^{*}:\NS(Y_{\bar{k}})\longrightarrow\NS(Z_{\bar{k}})
is injective and has a torsion-free cokernel.

Proof.

We may assume
k
is algebraically closed. All claims are stable under compositions
W\hookrightarrow Z\hookrightarrow Y
of infinitesimal thickenings, so we may further assume
i
is a first-order thickening, i.e., the ideal sheaf
I=I_{Z}
satisfies
I^{2}=0
. Take a short exact sequence of fppf abelian sheaves
0\longrightarrow I\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m,Y}\longrightarrow i_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m,Z}\longrightarrow 0
on
Y
, where the first homomorphism is defined by
f\longmapsto 1+f
using the assumption
I^{2}=0
. Its long exact sequence

(A.1.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(Y,I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pic_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pic_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(Y,I)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}

immediately shows
\coker i^{*}
is torsion-free. Taking
d
-torsions of (
A.1.9
),
i^{*}:\Pic_{Y}[d]\longrightarrow\Pic_{Z}[d]
is an isomorphism of
d
-torsion points for every
d\in\mathbb{Z}

[
Stacks
, Tag 0C6S]
. Therefore,
\ker i^{*}
is torsion-free as well.

(Cokernels of (1) and (2)) Take a homomorphism between short exact sequences

Note that
\coker f
is connected and
\coker g
is discrete. Therefore,
\coker(\ker g\longrightarrow\coker f)
is the neutral component of
\coker i^{*}
. Since
\coker i^{*}
was torsion-free, its neutral component
\coker(\ker g\longrightarrow\coker f)
is unipotent. But
\ker g
is discrete, so this mans
\coker f
is unipotent and
\ker g\longrightarrow\coker f
is the zero map. Moreover, the torsion-freeness of
\coker i^{*}
implies that of
\coker g
by
Lemma

A.1.2
.

(Kernels of (1) and (2)) Since
\ker i^{*}
is torsion-free, so is
\ker f
. This proves the neutral component of
\ker f
is unipotent. But
\ker f
is of finite type and hence
\pi_{0}(\ker f)
is a finite group, so
\pi_{0}(\ker f)
must be trivial and hence
\ker f
unipotent. Now the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\ker f\longrightarrow\ker i^{*}\longrightarrow\ker g\longrightarrow 0
(uniquely) splits again by
Lemma

A.1.2
. Since
\ker i^{*}=\ker f\times\ker g
is torsion-free,
\ker g\cong\mathbb{Z}^{r}
is torsion-free and
\ker i^{*}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\times s}\times\mathbb{Z}^{r}
. Finally, recall from (
A.1.9
) that the
k
-point group
(\ker i^{*})(k)=k^{s}\times\mathbb{Z}^{r}
is a quotient of an abelian group
H^{1}(Y,I)\cong k^{t}
. Since
k^{t}
is a divisible group, its quotient
k^{s}\times\mathbb{Z}^{r}
is divisible as well. This proves
r=0
, or
\ker g=0
as claimed.
∎

A.2.
Albanese morphisms

Let us make precise the version of the Albanese morphism we use in this article.

A.2.1
.

Let
X
be a finite type scheme over
k
. The
Albanese morphism
of
X
is a morphism
\alb:X\longrightarrow\Alb_{X}
universal among morphisms from
X
to an abelian torsor over
k
. The abelian torsor
\Alb_{X}
is called the
Albanese torsor
of
X
.

Notice that this definition fixes no
k
-point of
X
(as a result,
\Alb_{X}
should be an abelian torsor instead of abelian variety). This subtle distinction is necessary when
k
is not algebraically closed or when
X
is equipped with a group action. It is well-known that every smooth proper connected variety
X
has an Albanese morphism. The following is a vast generalization of this fact.

A.2.2
[
Con17
]
,
[
LS24
]
.

Let
X
be a proper scheme over
k
with
h^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=1
. Then

(1)

The Albanese morphism
\alb:X\longrightarrow\Alb_{X}
exists.

(2)

The pullback under
\alb
is an injective homomorphism

\alb^{*}:\Pic^{\circ}_{\Alb_{X}}=\Alb_{X}^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{X},

whose image is the maximal abelian subvariety of
\Pic^{\circ}_{X}
. In particular,
\Alb_{X}
is a torsor under the dual of the maximal abelian subvariety of
\Pic^{\circ}_{X}
.

A.2.3
.

Let
Y
be a proper scheme over
k
and
i:Z\hookrightarrow Y
a closed subscheme defined by a nilpotent ideal. Assume
h^{0}(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})=h^{0}(Z,\mathcal{O}_{Z})=1
. Then there exists a surjective morphism
\Alb_{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\Alb_{Y}
and a commutative diagram

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Proof.

Theorem

A.2.2
applies to both
Y
and
Z
. Hence it is enough to show
i^{*}:\Pic^{\circ}_{Y}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{Z}
induces an injective homomorphism between their maximal abelian subvarieties. Let
A=\Alb_{Y}^{\vee}
be the maximal abelian subvariety of
\Pic^{\circ}_{Y}
. Since
\ker i^{*}
is unipotent by
Lemma

A.1.8
, we have
A\cap\ker i^{*}=0
, or
i^{*}
injects
A
into
\Pic^{\circ}_{Z}
.
∎

A.3.
Non-normal schemes via conductor subschemes

This subsection follows
[
Stacks
, Tag 0ECH]
and
[
Sch12
]
. It is a generalization of the construction for singular curves in
[
Ser88
, §IV.1]
.

A.3.1
[
Stacks
, Tag 0E25]
.

Let
\tilde{Y}
be a quasi-projective scheme over
k
,
\tilde{Z}\subset\tilde{Y}
a closed subscheme, and
\tilde{Z}\longrightarrow Z
a finite morphism. Then there exists a pushout
Y=\tilde{Y}\amalg_{\tilde{Z}}Z
in the category of schemes:

(A.3.2)

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Moreover,
Z\subset Y
is a closed subscheme,
\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow Y
is finite, and the diagram is also cartesian.

The diagram (
A.3.2
) is a pushout in the category of topological spaces as well, so it really looks like contracting
\tilde{Z}
to
Z
.

Let us now start with a reduced quasi-projective scheme
Y
over
k
and take its normalization

\nu:\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow Y.

The homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{Y}\longrightarrow\nu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{Y}}
is injective because
Y
is reduced. The scheme-theoretic support of
F=\nu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{Y}}/\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is a closed subscheme
Z\subset Y
, the
conductor subscheme
of
Y
. Its ideal sheaf
\mathfrak{c}=I_{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is called the
conductor ideal
, which is equivalently defined as

\mathfrak{c}=\{f\in\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{Y}}:f\,\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{Y}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Y}\}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Y}.

One checks that
\mathfrak{c}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{Y}}
is an ideal as well, so it defines a closed subscheme
\tilde{Z}=\nu^{-1}(Z)\subset\tilde{Y}
. We thus arrive at a cartesian diagram (
A.3.2
).

A.3.3
Reduced schemes from normal schemes
[
Sch14
]
.

Let
Y
be a reduced quasi-projective scheme,
\nu:\tilde{Y}\longrightarrow Y
be its normalization, and
\tilde{Z}\subset\tilde{Y}
and
Z\subset Y
be the conductor subschemes as above. Then the cartesian diagram (
A.3.2
) is a pushout diagram.

Proof.

The following is a copy of Schwede’s proof. We may assume
Y=\Spec A
for a reduced
k
-algebra
A
of finite type. Writing
\bar{A}
for the normalization of
A
and
\mathfrak{c}\subset A\subset\bar{A}
the conductor ideal, we need to show that the diagram

(A.3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{A}/\mathfrak{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\mathfrak{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

is a pullback diagram of commutative rings. In other words, we need to show the ring homomorphism
A\longrightarrow\{(f,\bar{h})\in\bar{A}\times A/\mathfrak{c}:\bar{f}=\bar{h}\in\bar{A}/\mathfrak{c}\}
defined by
f\longmapsto(f,\bar{f})
is an isomorphism. It is injective because the normalization
A\subset\bar{A}
is injective. It is surjective because if
f-h\in\mathfrak{c}\subset A
for
f\in\bar{A}
and
h\in A
, then
f\in A
.
∎

In other words, every reduced scheme
Y
is obtained by “contracting” its normalization
\tilde{Y}
in a preferred way, using the notion of the conductor subscheme/ideal.

A.3.1.
Equivariance

The above discussion about conductors is equivariant.

A.3.5
.

Let
G
be a finite group acting on a quasi-projective scheme
Y
over
k
. Then taking normalization and quotient commute:
Y^{\nu}/G=(Y/G)^{\nu}
.

Proof.

The quasi-projectiveness is simply to guarantee the existence of a quotient as a scheme. Since the statement is local on
Y
and
Y_{\red}/G=(Y/G)_{\red}
, we may assume
Y=\Spec A
for a reduced
k
-algebra
A
of finite type.

Denote the total ring of fractions by
K=S^{-1}A
, where
S\subset A
is the set of all nonzero-divisors, and the normalization of
A
by
\bar{A}
. We have
A\subset\bar{A}\subset K
. The
G
-invariant of
K
is the total ring of fractions of
A^{G}
because
K^{G}=(S^{-1}A)^{G}=(S\cap A^{G})^{-1}A^{G}
by
[
AM69
, Exercise 5.12]
. We need to show
\overline{A^{G}}=\bar{A}^{G}
. The inclusion
(\subset)
is clear. To show
(\supset)
, assume that
f\in\bar{A}
is
G
-invariant. Take an identity
f^{n}+a_{n-1}f^{n-1}+\cdots+a_{0}=0
for
a_{i}\in A
. Applying each
g\in G
and adding them together, we have
|G|\cdot f^{n}+(\sum_{g}g\cdot a_{n-1})f^{n-1}+\cdots+(\sum_{g}g\cdot a_{0})=0
. Since
k
has characteristic
0
, we conclude
f\in\overline{A^{G}}
.
∎

A.3.6
.

Let
G
be a finite group acting on a reduced quasi-projective scheme
Y
over
k
. Consider the conductor subscheme diagram (
A.3.2
) in
Proposition

A.3.3
. Then the diagram is
G
-equivariant and its quotient is a pushout diagram as in
Proposition

A.3.1
.

Proof.

Recall the coherent sheaf
F=\nu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{Y}}/\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. The
G
-action uniquely lifts to
\tilde{Y}
so
F
is a
G
-equivariant sheaf on
Y
. Hence its support
Z
and its ideal sheaf
\mathfrak{c}
are
G
-equivariant. This proves that the conductor subscheme diagram is equivariant.

Let us now prove a quotient of an equivariant pushout diagram (
A.3.2
) is again a pushout diagram. Start with a
G
-equivariant pullback diagram of commutative rings

\hbox to88.11pt{\vbox to43.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.05371pt\lower-21.81943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-44.05371pt}{-15.6597pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.74 18.21 L 8.24 18.21}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{5.75584pt}{13.1597pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

or an isomorphism
A\longrightarrow\{(f,\bar{h})\in B\times A/I:\bar{f}=\bar{h}\in B/IB\}
. Taking its
G
-invariant, the composition

A^{G}\longrightarrow\{(f,\bar{h})\in B^{G}\times A^{G}/I^{G}:\bar{f}=\bar{h}\}\subset\{(f,\bar{h})\in(B\times A/I)^{G}:\bar{f}=\bar{h}\}

is an isomorphism. This proves that both the first and second maps are isomorphisms. That is, the
G
-invariant part of the diagram is cartesian. We do not know whether the
G
-quotient of the conductor diagram of
Y
is again the conductor diagram of
Y/G
(but it is still a pushout diagram).
∎
